Weyl Law and convergence in the classical limit for min-max nonlocal minimal surfaces
Enric Florit-Simon

Abstract
We study nonlocal minimal surfaces as a new approximation theory for the area functional, and more specifically in the context of Yau’s conjecture on the existence of minimal surfaces in closed three-dimensional manifolds. This programme offers an alternative to the Almgren–Pitts and Allen–Cahn approaches, with advantageous features both from the existence and regularity viewpoints. We build on recent work in which the author and collaborators constructed infinitely many nonlocal s s -minimal hypersurfaces (via min-max methods) on any closed n n -dimensional Riemannian manifold, establishing a full analogue of Yau’s conjecture for s ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) s\in(0,1) . The present article first proves a Weyl-type Law for the fractional perimeters of these hypersurfaces. The rest—and main part—of the article is devoted to obtaining uniform estimates (in the classical limit s → 1 s\to 1 ) for min-max s s -minimal surfaces in closed three-manifolds, eventually establishing their convergence to smooth, classical minimal surfaces. We recover in particular recent results on existence, generic density and equidistribution of minimal surfaces, which are a strong form of Yau’s conjecture in this setting.



1
Introduction and main results

In
[
19
]
, the author and collaborators obtained the existence (for any fixed
s\in(0,1)
) of infinitely many nonlocal
s
-minimal (hyper)surfaces
\{E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}\}_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}}
in any closed Riemannian manifold
M
of arbitrary dimension
n
, which in particular proves the nonlocal version of a well-known conjecture (recently proved by Song
[
54
]
) of Yau
[
58
]
on the existence of infinitely many classical minimal surfaces on closed three-dimensional manifolds. A partial statement of the result in
[
19
]
is the following:

Theorem 1.1

(
Fractional Yau Conjecture
[
19
]
)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be an
n
-dimensional, closed Riemannian manifold. Then, for every
s\in(0,1)
and every natural number
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, there exists an
s
-minimal surface
E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
with Morse index at most
\mathfrak{p}
(in the sense of Definition
3.3
) and fractional perimeter

\frac{c(M)}{1-s}\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}\leq{\rm Per}_{s}(\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s})=:l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)\leq\frac{C(M)}{1-s}\mathfrak{p}^{s/n},

(1)

where
c(M)
and
C(M)
are uniform as
s\to 1
.

In particular,
M
contains infinitely many
s
-minimal surfaces. In addition, if
s\in(s_{0},1)
where
s_{0}\in(0,1)
is a universal constant, if
n=3
or
n=4
then
\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
is a smooth hypersurface, whereas if
n\geq 5
then
\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
is smooth outside of a set of Hausdorff dimension at most
n-5
.

Here
l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)
corresponds to a (limiting) min-max value arising from considering certain
\mathfrak{p}
-parameter families of functions on
M
, and is defined in Section
3.2
in the present article. We remark that we will be employing slightly different min-max families than those in
[
19
]
— all the relevant setting and necessary modifications to obtain Theorem
1.1
with our definitions are given in Section
3
.

The main motivation behind the present article is to study nonlocal minimal surfaces as a
new approximation theory for the area functional
, more specifically in the context of min-max problems. The first result in this work is that, perhaps surprisingly, a Weyl-type Law holds for the fractional perimeters of these
s
-minimal surfaces, already for a fixed
s
:

Theorem 1.2

(
Weyl Law for nonlocal minimal surfaces
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed manifold of dimension
n
, and let
s\in(0,1)
. There exists a universal constant
\tau(n,s)>0
, depending only on
n
and the fractional parameter
s
, such that

\lim_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\tau(n,s)\textnormal{vol}(M,g)^{\frac{n-s}{n}}\,.

(2)

This gives a nonlocal analogue of the Weyl-type Law for classical minimal hypersurfaces, which was conjectured by Gromov
[
37
]
in general codimension and proved by Liokumovich–Marques–Neves
[
42
]
in the case of minimal hypersurfaces obtained via the Almgren–Pitts min-max procedure. Soon after that, Gaspar–Guaraco
[
33
]
showed that the Weyl Law holds for limits (as
\varepsilon\to 0
) of min-max Allen–Cahn solutions as well, with a similar proof to the one in
[
42
]
. Interestingly, their Weyl Law can hold only for the limits obtained as
\varepsilon\to 0
; on the other hand, Theorem
1.1
holds already for any fixed
s\in(0,1)
. We emphasise here that
s
-minimal hypersurfaces are geometric objects on their own, with a canonical definition on Riemannian manifolds (see Section
3.1.1
and
[
18
]
), and moreover the parameter
s
is “dimensionless”; in the Allen–Cahn case,
\varepsilon
has “length units”, thus being an Allen–Cahn solution for a certain
\varepsilon
is not a scaling invariant notion anymore, and solutions with larger energy (i.e. more transitions) can only exist when making
\varepsilon
smaller.

The rest (and main part) of the article focuses on the classical limit
s\to 1^{-}
. For a smooth open set
E\subset M
, it will follow from our results that

\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}(1-s){\rm Per}_{s}(\partial E)=\gamma_{n}{\rm Per}(\partial E)\,,

with
\gamma_{n}
defined in (
11
). This compels us to define

l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M):=\liminf_{s\to 1}[(1-s)l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)]=\liminf_{s\to 1}[(1-s)\text{Per}_{s}(\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s})]\,,

(3)

and we will obtain a Weyl Law for these quantities as well:

Theorem 1.3

(
Weyl Law in the classical limit
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed manifold of dimension
n
. There exists a universal constant
\tau(n,1)>0
, depending only on
n
, such that

\lim_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{1}{n}}l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\tau(n,1)\textnormal{vol}(M,g)^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\,.

(4)

One would expect for
l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)
to correspond to the area of a classical minimal hypersurface
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
of optimal regularity, obtained as a limit of the
\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
after letting
s\to 1
. For this reason, the main bulk of the article is devoted to studying the convergence, as
s\to 1
, of sequences of
s
-minimal surfaces with bounded Morse index, focusing on the case
n=3
. We will exhibit their convergence to a smooth classical minimal surface in a remarkably clean way, which could be interpreted as saying that stability assumptions actually confer
s
-minimal hypersurfaces a length scale
\sqrt{1-s}
as
s\to 1
, thus effectively becoming a regularisation of the area functional. A partial statement is the following:

Theorem 1.4

(
Compactness of
s
-minimal surfaces
)
.

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
3
. Let
\{E_{s_{i}}\}_{i}
be a sequence of
C^{2}

s_{i}
-minimal surfaces with Morse index bounded by
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
, and suppose that
s_{i}\to 1^{-}
. Assume that

\sup_{i}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{s_{i}})<\infty\,.

Then, up to passing to a subsequence (not relabeled), the following hold:

There exists a collection of smooth, connected, disjoint classical minimal surfaces
\Sigma_{1},...,\Sigma_{m}
, together with positive integers
n_{1},...,n_{m}
, such that the
\partial E_{s_{i}}
converge in the sense of varifolds to
\sum_{k=1}^{m}n_{k}[\Sigma_{k}]
, where
[\Sigma_{k}]
denotes the integer rectifiable varifold associated to the smooth surface
\Sigma_{k}
. Moreover,

\lim_{i\to\infty}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{s_{i}})=\gamma_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}n_{k}\textnormal{Per}(\Sigma_{k})\,.

Regarding the convergence, there exists a (possibly empty) collection of points
q_{1},...,q_{l}
, with
l\leq\mathfrak{p}
, such that: in a neighbourhood of every point in
\Sigma_{k}\setminus\{q_{1},...,q_{l}\}
, for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
the
\partial E_{s_{i}}
converge in
C^{2,\alpha}
as
n_{k}
ordered normal graphs with separation of order at least
\sqrt{1-s_{i}}
.

Finally, the Morse index is lower semicontinuous, in the sense that

\sum_{k=1}^{m}\textnormal{index}(\Sigma_{k})\leq\mathfrak{p}\,.

Applying this result to the
E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
from Theorem
1.1
, which have index bounded by
\mathfrak{p}
, we get:

Corollary 1.5

(
Attainability of min-max widths
)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be a
3
-dimensional, closed Riemannian manifold. Then, for every natural number
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, there exists a collection of smooth, connected, disjoint classical minimal surfaces
\Sigma_{1},...,\Sigma_{m_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, together with positive integers
n_{1},...,n_{m_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, such that

l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\gamma_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}}n_{k}\textnormal{Per}(\Sigma_{k})\,.

Moreover,
\sum_{i=k}^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}}\textnormal{index}(\Sigma_{k})\leq\mathfrak{p}
.

Combining Corollary
1.5
with the asymptotics for the
l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)
in Theorem
1.3
, this gives a Weyl-type Law for classical minimal surfaces like the one by Liokumovich–Marques–Neves
[
42
]
. With the Weyl Law at hand, we can in particular recover the results by Irie–Marques–Neves
[
40
]
and Marques–Neves–Song
[
44
]
on the density and equidistribution of minimal surfaces on closed manifolds with a generic metric:

Theorem 1.6

(
Density
)
.

Let
M
be a closed manifold of dimension
3
.
For a
C^{\infty}
-generic Riemannian metric
g
on
M
, the union of all closed, smooth, embedded minimal hypersurfaces in
(M,g)
is dense (and in particular, there are infinitely many such hypersurfaces).

Theorem 1.7

(
Equidistribution
)
.

Let
M
be a closed manifold of dimension
3
.
For a
C^{\infty}
-generic Riemannian metric
g
on
M
, there exists a sequence
\{\Sigma_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of closed, smooth, embedded minimal hypersurfaces in
(M,g)
which is equidistributed. More precisely, for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\rm Per}(\Sigma_{i})}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{\Sigma_{i}}f\,d\Sigma_{i}=\frac{1}{{\rm Vol}_{g}(M)}\int_{M}f\,dM\,.

We refer the reader to
[
3
,
23
,
43
,
46
,
49
,
59
]
as well as the references
[
40
,
42
,
44
,
54
]
already mentioned in this introduction for an overview of some of the main developments that have led to the proof of Yau’s conjecture.

2
Overview of the article and further directions

The proofs of the Weyl Law(s) and of Theorems
1.6
and
1.7
follow the ideas of
[
42
]
and of
[
40
,
44
]
. The main difficulties in the nonlocal case stem from the presence of nonlocal interactions and the fact that the fractional Sobolev energy on a closed manifold
M
is canonically defined through a singular kernel
K_{s}(p,q)
which depends on the heat kernel of the entire manifold (see (
5
)): Indeed, the proof of the Weyl Law is based on comparison between
M
and its subdomains, and between subdomains of
M
and subdomains of Euclidean space. Likewise, the proofs of Theorems
1.6
and
1.7
require the precise knowledge of how changing the metric of
M
affects the behaviour of the problem. Therefore, the right setting needs to be selected carefully. Our chosen approach, which we believe to be of independent interest, is to first prove a Weyl Law for a related min-max problem, where an analogous fractional Sobolev energy is defined through a different kernel which depends explicitly on the distance function of
M
. We then show how this implies the Weyl Law for the canonical, original problem as well. The study of the precise behaviour of the singular kernel
K_{s}
, which was initiated in
[
18
]
and is continued in the present paper, is used in an essential way in the aforementioned analysis as well as in the rest of the paper.

The proof of the compactness of
s
-minimal surfaces with bounded index occupies the rest (and main bulk) of our work. Most of the results are of a remarkably “clean” nature, providing—together with
[
19
]
—a self-contained and robust (as
s\to 1
) regularity theory for min-max
s
-minimal surfaces. Combined with the elementary existence theory in
[
19
]
, this showcases the nonlocal approximation as an attractive alternative to the Almgren–Pitts and Allen–Cahn approaches. For comparison, the Allen–Cahn approach offers a simple existence theory too, but at the expense of a very hard regularity theory:

•

In general dimension, the only available results are via the deep and powerful regularity theory in
[
57
]
for stable integral varifolds. This method presents moreover important difficulties, such as a potential loss of geometric information in the prescribed mean curvature
[
8
]
or manifold-with-boundary
[
45
]
cases.

•

In dimension
n=3
, a stronger, alternative regularity theory is present, developed in the (similarly deep) breakthrough articles
[
55
,
56
,
23
]
. An extension to higher dimensions depends on the longstanding open problem of classification of stable solutions (with bounded energy density) to Allen–Cahn, which so far has only been achieved for
n=4
in the very recent (independent) article
[
31
]
.

Now, despite the “clean” nature of the nonlocal approach, the regularity and compactness part of the article will require a lengthier exposition. The main reason is that, in fact, we need to build most of the theory from scratch: As an example, our very first step is to obtain the (challenging, yet deceptively simple-looking) formula for the second variation of the fractional perimeter of a domain on a manifold, which will require considering a smooth extension to the entire manifold of the normal vector of the domain, and will then take the form of a singular integral which will be shown to converge due to second order cancellations.

A point we consider most interesting in our strategy is the eventual proof of global estimates for
s
-minimal surfaces with bounded index, including the uniform, quantitative
L^{1}
decay of their
classical
mean curvatures as
s\to 1
which holds even around points of singular convergence, and which will allows us to obtain the convergence in the varifold sense to a limit. Once the limit varifold is obtained, we will show its regularity outside of finitely many points; then, a careful argument using this partial regularity will allow us to show the convergence of the second (or any) variations of the approximating
s
-minimal surfaces to those of the limit, as well as the convergence of the fractional perimeters, and only then we will remove the singularities thanks to the stability properties inherited by the limit. We emphasise here that, to our knowledge, this is the first instance of a compactness theorem for
s
-minimal surfaces as
s\to 1
without the presence of uniform curvature estimates; in particular, the convergence of critical points under exclusively an area bound assumption, as in
[
1
]
or
[
39
]
, is an open and very interesting problem.

Section
5.3
, which exhibits uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
and sheet separation estimates for almost-stable
s
-minimal surfaces, builds on the recent breakthrough article
[
21
]
, which among other things studies the stable case (without any area bound assumption) on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and proves the classification of stable
s
-minimal cones in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
as hyperplanes for
s
close to
1
. Most of the proofs in our Section
5.3
introduce alternative arguments, in order to deal with the challenging form of the second variation formula and the Riemannian setting. As a notable point, through our approach we obtain uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, an improvement of the result in
[
21
]
which is for
\alpha
sufficiently small.

We expect our methods and results to be extended soon to dimensions
4\leq n\leq 7
; the missing points are a classification of stable
s
-minimal cones (under the assumption of area bounds as
s\to 1
) in dimensions
5\leq n\leq 7
, a problem which should be much simpler than an unconditional classification of stable
s
-minimal cones
1
1

1

This should be compared, for example, with the stable Bernstein conjecture on the classification of stable minimal hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with and without assuming area bounds.
, and an adaptation of Section
5.3
to dimensions
4\leq n\leq 7
. For
n=3
, one can decouple the problem of quantifying the separation between different layers of an
s
-minimal surface from the regularity of the individual layers. Attacking both problems at the same time using an approach as in
[
56
]
(see also
[
23
]
) would seem like a promising strategy for
4\leq n\leq 7
, and we believe that the methods introduced in the present article will be useful for this approach.

Regarding the nonlocal Weyl Law in Theorem
1.2
, it would be interesting to study which properties it implies for the
s
-minimal hypersurfaces
\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
. The proof of Theorem
1.6
would seem hard to adapt to the nonlocal case, given the nonlocal interactions inherent to the objects under consideration. The proof of Theorem
1.7
, a result which is stronger anyway, could however potentially have an adaptation to the nonlocal setting. As a matter of fact, we would expect even stronger equidistribution properties to hold in the nonlocal case, such as the possibility of obtaining results for arbitrary (and not only generic) metrics.

3
Setting

3.1
The fractional setting on a manifold

3.1.1
Definitions

Let
(M,g)
be a closed (i.e. compact and without boundary) Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Let
H_{M}(t,p,q)
be the heat kernel of
M
, and let
s\in(0,2)
. Set

K_{M,s}(p,q):=\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}H_{M}(p,q,t)\frac{dt}{t^{1+s/2}}\,.

(5)

We will often omit the dependence in
M
and
s
of
K
when there is no risk of confusion.

Define

\alpha_{n,s}=\frac{2^{s}\Gamma\Big(\tfrac{n+s}{2}\Big)}{\pi^{n/2}|\Gamma(-s/2)|}=\frac{s2^{s-1}\Gamma\Big(\tfrac{n+s}{2}\Big)}{\pi^{n/2}\Gamma(1-s/2)}.

(6)

Then, the kernel
K_{M,s}(p,q)
differs from
\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{d_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}
only by a lower order term, see Proposition
4.9
, and they coincide exactly in the case
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We then define the fractional Sobolev space
H^{s/2}(M)
as the space of functions
u\in L^{2}(M)
such that

[u]^{2}_{H^{s/2}(M)}:=\iint_{M\times M}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K_{M,s}(p,q)\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}

(7)

is finite. Several other definitions for the fractional Sobolev seminorm, identical (and not just equivalent) to (
7
), are given in
[
19
]
, showing that it is canonically defined. We recall here in particular the spectral definition

[u]^{2}_{H^{s/2}(M)}=2\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}^{s/2}\langle u,\phi_{k}\rangle^{2}_{L^{2}(M)}\,,

(8)

where
\{\phi_{k}\}_{k}
is an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of the Laplace-Beltrami operator
(-\Delta_{g})
and
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k}
are the corresponding eigenvalues. This shows that in the limit
s\to 2
we recover the usual
[u]^{2}_{H^{1}(M)}
seminorm.

We can now define the fractional perimeter
\text{Per}_{s}
. Given
s\in(0,1)

and a (measurable) set
E\subset M
, we define

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E):=[\chi_{E}]^{2}_{H^{{s/2}}(M)}=\frac{1}{4}[\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}}]^{2}_{H^{{s/2}}(M)}=2\int_{E}\int_{E^{c}}K_{M,s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}\,,

(9)

where
\chi_{E}
is the characteristic function of
E
, and
E^{c}:=M\setminus E
. In practice,
E
will always be a domain with some regularity, and the notation
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\partial E)
will sometimes be used to denote
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)
instead in analogy with the classical case. More generally, if
\Omega\subset M
, we set

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\Omega):=2\int_{E\cap\Omega}\int_{E^{c}\cap\Omega}K_{M,s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}\,.

(10)

We have omitted the interactions between
\Omega
and
\Omega^{c}
, which will be convenient in the present paper, but which the reader should keep in mind when consulting related literature in which different notation might be employed.

One can see that, for every subset
E\subset M
with smooth boundary,

(1-s)\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\partial E)\to\gamma_{n}\textnormal{Per}(\partial E)
as
s\uparrow 1
, where

\gamma_{n}=2\alpha_{n,s}\omega_{n-1}

(11)

and
\omega_{n-1}=|\mathcal{B}^{n-1}|
indicates the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. See
[
6
]
for further details in the case of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, or the proof of Proposition
5.34
in the present paper for a significantly stronger result.

Definition 3.1

(
s
-minimal surface
)
.

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian manifold. Given
s\in(0,1)
, the boundary
\partial E
of a set
E\subset M
(or the set
E
itself) is said to be an
s
-
minimal surface
if
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)<\infty
, and

\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi^{t}_{X}(E))=0\qquad\mbox{for every vector field}\quad X\in C^{2}(M)\,,

(12)

where
\psi^{t}_{X}:M\times\mathbb{R}\to M
denotes the flow of
X
at time
t
.

The previous definition admits a natural local version.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold. Given
\mathcal{U}\subset M
open, the boundary
\partial E
of a set
E\subset M
is said to be an
s
-
minimal surface
in
\mathcal{U}
if
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)<\infty
, and for every smooth and compactly supported vector field
X
in
U
(denoted
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{c}(\mathcal{U})
) we have

\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi^{t}_{X}(E))=0\,.

Definition 3.3

(
Morse index and stability
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold and
\partial E
be an
s
-minimal surface in
\mathcal{U}\subset M
open (as in Definition
3.2
). Then,
\partial E
is said to have
Morse index
at most
m
in
\mathcal{U}
if for every
(m+1)
vector fields
X_{0},\dotsc,X_{m}\in\mathfrak{X}_{c}(\mathcal{U})
there exists some linear combination
X=a_{0}X_{0}+\dots+a_{m}X_{m}
with
a_{0}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}+\dots+a_{m}^{2}=1
such that

\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi^{t}_{X}(E))\geq 0.

In the particular case
m=0
, we say that
\partial E
is
stable
in
\mathcal{U}
.

Remark 3.4
.

As we proved in Lemma 3.10 in
[
18
]
, if
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)<\infty
and
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{c}(\mathcal{U})
then the map
t\mapsto\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi^{t}_{X}(E))
is well-defined for all
t
and of class
C^{\infty}
. Thus, the previous definitions are meaningful.

The
s
-minimal surfaces in Theorem
1.1
are obtained as limits as
\varepsilon\to 0
of solutions to the fractional Allen-Cahn equation on
M
. Given
v:M\to\mathbb{R}
, the fractional Allen-Cahn energy of
v
is defined as

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(v,M):=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{\rm Sob}(v,M)+\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{\rm Pot}(v,M),

(13)

where

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{\rm Sob}(v,M):=\frac{1}{4}\iint_{M\times M}(v(p)-v(q))^{2}K_{M,s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q},\quad\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{\rm Pot}(v,M):=\varepsilon^{-s}\int_{M}W(v)\,dx\,,

and
W(v)=\frac{1}{4}(1-v^{2})^{2}
is the standard quartic double-well potential with wells at
\pm 1
. The double-well potential penalizes functions which are not identical to
\pm 1
, thus why one expects to find nonlocal
s
-minimal surfaces as the limits of critical points of this energy when
\varepsilon\to 0
.
A function
u:M\to\mathbb{R}
is a critical point of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}
if and only if it satisfies the Allen-Cahn equation

(-\Delta)_{M}^{s/2}u+\varepsilon^{-s}W^{\prime}(u)=0\,.

(14)

Here
(-\Delta)^{{s/2}}
is the fractional Laplacian on
(M,g)
, and it can be represented as

(-\Delta)_{M}^{{s/2}}u(p)=\int_{M}(u(p)-u(q))K_{M,s}(p,q)\,dV_{q}\,.

(15)

3.1.2
Fundamental properties

Here, and in the rest of the paper,
\mathcal{B}_{R}(x)
denotes the Euclidean ball of radius
R
centered at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
B_{R}(p)
denotes the metric ball on
M
of radius
R
and center
p
. We will often write
\mathcal{B}_{R}
in place of
\mathcal{B}_{R}(0)
.

Definition 3.5

(
Local flatness assumption
)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be an
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold and
p\in M
. For
R>0
, we say that
(M,g)
satisfies the
\ell
-th order
flatness assumption at scale
R
around the point
p
, with parametrization
\varphi
, abbreviated as
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R,p,\varphi)
, whenever there exists an open neighborhood
V
of
p
and a diffeomorphism

\varphi:\mathcal{B}_{R}(0)\to V,\quad\mbox{with }\varphi(0)=p_{0}\,,

such that, letting
g_{ij}=g\left(\varphi_{*}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\right),\varphi_{*}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\right)\right)

be the representation of the metric
g
in the coordinates
\varphi^{-1}
, we have

\big(1-\frac{1}{100}\big)|v|^{2}\leq g_{ij}(x)v^{i}v^{j}\leq\big(1+\frac{1}{100}\big)|v|^{2}\hskip 7.0pt\hskip 7.0pt\forall\,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mbox{ and }\forall\,x\in\mathcal{B}_{R}(0)\,,

(16)

and

R^{|\alpha|}\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}g_{ij}(x)}{\partial x^{\alpha}}\bigg|\leq\frac{1}{100}\hskip 7.0pt\hskip 7.0pt\forall\alpha\mbox{ multi-index with }1\leq|\alpha|\leq\ell,\mbox{ and }\forall x\in\mathcal{B}_{R}(0).

(17)

Remark 3.6
.

For any smooth closed Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
, given
\ell\geq 0
, there exists
R_{0}>0
for which
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R_{0},p,\varphi_{p})
is satisfied for all
p\in M
. For example,
\varphi_{p}
can be chosen to be the restriction of the exponential map (of
M
) at
p
to the (normal) ball
\mathcal{B}_{R_{0}}(0)\subset T_{p}M\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark 3.7
.

As in
[
19
]
, this notion of local flatness is used in our results to stress the fact that, once the local geometry of the manifold is controlled in the sense of Definition
3.5
, then our estimates are independent of

M
. Interestingly, this makes our estimates of local nature even though the objects we consider are nonlocal.

Remark 3.8
.

Throughout the paper the following scaling properties will be used several times.

(a)

Given
M=(M,g)
and
r>0
, we can consider the ”rescaled manifold”
\widehat{M}=(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g)
. When performing this rescaling, the new heat kernel
H_{\widehat{M}}
satisfies

H_{\widehat{M}}(p,q,t)=r^{n}H_{M}(p,q,r^{2}t)\,.

As a consequence, the ”rescaled kernel”
\widehat{K}_{s}
defining the
s
-perimeter on
\widehat{M}
satisfies

\widehat{K}_{s}(p,q)=r^{n+s}K_{s}(p,q).

(b)

Concerning the flatness assumption, it is easy to show that
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R,p,\varphi)\Rightarrow{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R^{\prime},p,\varphi)
for all
R^{\prime}<R
and
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R,p,\varphi)\Leftrightarrow{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g,R/r,p,\varphi(r\,\cdot\,))
.

(c)

Similarly, if
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R,p,\varphi)
holds, and
q\in\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}(0))
is such that
\mathcal{B}_{\varrho}(\varphi^{-1}(q))\subset\mathcal{B}_{R}(0)
, then

{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g,\varrho/r,q,\varphi_{\varphi^{-1}(q),r})
holds, where
\varphi_{x,\,r}:=\varphi(x+r\,\cdot\,)
.

We now give precise estimates for the kernel
K_{s}(p,q)
which we will need in the article, taken from
[
18
]
.

Proposition 3.9

(
[
18
]
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian
n
-manifold, not necessarily closed,
s\in(0,2)
and let
p\in M
. Assume
{\rm FA_{\ell}}(M,g,R,p,\varphi)
holds, where
l\geq 1
, and denote
K(x,y):=K_{s}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))
.

Given
x\in\mathcal{B}_{R}(0)
, let

A(x)
denote the positive symmetric square root of the matrix
(g_{ij}(x))
—
g_{ij}
being the metric in coordinates
\varphi^{-1}
— and, for
x,z\in\mathcal{B}_{R/2}(0)
, define

k(x,z):=K(x,x+z)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widehat{k}(x,z):=k(x,z)-\alpha_{n,s}\frac{{\rm det}\big(A(x)\big)}{|A(x)z|^{n+s}}.

Then

\big|\widehat{k}(x,z)\big|\leq R^{-1}\frac{C(n,s)}{|z|^{n+s-1}}\quad\mbox{for all }x,z\in\mathcal{B}_{R/4}(0)\,,

(18)

and, for every multi-indices
\alpha,\beta
with
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq\ell
,
we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x^{\alpha}}\frac{\partial^{|\beta|}}{\partial z^{\beta}}k(x,z)\bigg|\leq\frac{C(n,s,\ell)}{|z|^{n+s+|\beta|}}\quad\mbox{for all }x,z\in\mathcal{B}_{R/4}(0).

(19)

Moreover, for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{R/4}(0)
and for all
q\in M\setminus\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}(0))
we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x^{\alpha}}K_{s}(\varphi(x),q)\bigg|\leq\frac{C(n,\ell)}{R^{n+s}}\,,

(20)

and

\int_{M\setminus\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}(0))}\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x^{\alpha}}K_{s}(\varphi(x),q)\bigg|dV_{q}\leq\frac{C(n,\ell)}{R^{s}},

(21)

for every multi-index
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq\ell
.

Here, the constants
c(n,s)
depending on
s
are uniform for
s
away from
0
and
2
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
s_{0}\in(0,2)
and
s\in(s_{0},2)
. Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian
n
-manifold and
p\in M
. Assume that
{\rm FA}_{1}(M,g,p,R,\varphi)
holds. Then

c_{7}\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|x-y|^{n+s}}\leq K_{s}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))\leq c_{8}\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|x-y|^{n+s}}\,,

for all
x,y\in\mathcal{B}_{R/2}(0)
, where
c_{7},c_{8}>0
depend on
n
and
s_{0}
.

Proposition 3.11

(
[
18
]
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold and
s\in(0,2)
. Assume that the flatness assumption
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R,p,\varphi)
holds, and let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
be a smooth vector field supported on
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R/4})
. Writing
\psi_{X}^{t}
for the flow of
X
at time
t
, then for every
x,y\in\mathcal{B}_{R/4}(0)
we have

\bigg|\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}\bigg|_{t=0}K_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(\varphi(x)),\psi_{X}^{t}(\varphi(y)))\bigg|\leq CK_{s}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))\,,

(22)

for some constant
C=C(n,s,l,\|X\|_{C^{\ell}(\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}))})
which stays bounded for
s
away from
0
and
2
.

Lemma 3.12

(
[
18
]
)
.

Let
s\in(0,2)
. Let
M
satisfy the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R,p,\varphi)
, and let
v=\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}}\in H^{s/2}(M)
. Let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
be a smooth vector field supported on
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R/2})
, and put
v_{t}:=v\circ\psi_{X}^{-t}
, where
\psi_{X}^{t}
is the flow of
X
at time
t
. Then, for all
T>0
there holds

\sup_{0<t<T}\left|\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(E))\right|\leq C(1+\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R})))\,,

for some constant
C=C(n,s,l,T,\|X\|_{C^{\ell}(\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}))})
which stays bounded for
s
away from
0
and
2
.

Proof.

This is Lemma 2.17 in
[
18
]
. We give a sketch of its proof, since we will use similar ideas later. One starts by changing variables with the flow of
X
, to see that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(E))

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}\iint|v(\psi_{X}^{-t}(p))-v(\psi_{X}^{-t}(q))|^{2}K_{s}(p,q)\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}\iint|v(p)-v(q)|^{2}K_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(p),\psi_{X}^{t}(q))\,J_{t}(p)J_{t}(q)\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}

\displaystyle=\iint|v(p)-v(q)|^{2}\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}\Big[K_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(p),\psi_{X}^{t}(q))\,J_{t}(p)J_{t}(q)\Big]dV_{p}\,dV_{q}\,.

The Jacobians
J_{t}(p)
and their derivatives can simply be bounded by a constant with the right dependencies. The kernel part and its derivatives can be estimated appropriately using Proposition
3.11
for
p
and
q
close and (
21
) for
p
and
q
separated enough.
∎

We conclude this subsection with two simple interpolation results, which in particular imply that sets of finite (classical) perimeter have bounded fractional perimeter as well.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
M
satisfy the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{1}(N,g,p_{0},4R,\varphi)
. Let
s_{0}\in(0,1)
and
s\in(s_{0},1)
, and let
u:\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R})\to\mathbb{R}
be a function of bounded variation (BV). Then,

\iint_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R})}|u(p)-u(q)|^{2}K_{s}(p,q)\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}\leq\frac{C(n,s_{0})}{1-s}[u]_{BV(\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}))}^{s}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}))}^{1-s}\,.

(23)

In particular, if
E\subset M
is a set of finite perimeter in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R})
, then

R^{s-n}{\rm Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}))\leq\frac{C(n,s_{0})}{1-s}[R^{1-n}{\rm Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{R}))]^{s}\,.

(24)

Proof.

If
v:\mathcal{B}_{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a function of bounded variation, using the fundamental theorem of calculus it is simple to see that

\iint_{\mathcal{B}_{R}\times\mathcal{B}_{R}}\frac{|v(x)-v(y)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+s}}\,dx\,dy\leq\frac{C(n)}{(1-s)s}[v]_{BV(\mathcal{B}_{R})}^{s}\|v\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{R})}^{1-s}\,,

see for instance
[
11
, Proposition 4.2]
. The bound (
23
) then follows from applying this result to
v=u\circ\varphi
and using Lemma
3.10
to compare the corresponding kernels.
∎

We can obtain a global version as well:

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold. Let
s_{0}\in(0,1)
and
s\in(s_{0},1)
, and let
E\subset M
be a set of finite perimeter. Then,

{\rm Per}_{s}(E;M)\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[{\rm Per}(E;M)]^{s}|E|^{1-s}.

(25)

Proof.

Step 1.
Given
u:M\to\mathbb{R}
in
BV(M)
, we show that

\iint_{M\times M}|u(p)-u(q)|^{2}K_{s}\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[u]_{BV(M)}^{s}\|u\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}+C(M)\|u\|_{L^{1}(M)}\,.

(26)

First, note that since
M
is closed, there exists a number
\delta>0
with the property that, given any
p\in M
, the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{1}(M,g,4\delta,p,\varphi_{p})
are satisfied by some
\varphi_{p}
; see Remark
3.6
. Having fixed such a
\delta>0
, by compactness we can find a finite collection of points
p_{1},...,p_{N}
such that the balls
\varphi_{p_{1}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(p_{1})),...,\varphi_{p_{N}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(p_{N}))
cover
M
.

Using (
20
) and (
23
), we can then bound

\displaystyle\iint_{\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(p_{i}))\times M}|u(p)-u(q)|^{2}K_{s}\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{2\delta}(p_{i}))\times\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{2\delta}(p_{i}))}|u(p)-u(q)|^{2}K_{s}\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}+C(M)\|u\|_{L^{1}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[u]_{BV(\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{2\delta}(p_{i})))}^{s}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{2\delta}(p_{i})))}^{1-s}+C(M)\|u\|_{L^{1}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[u]_{BV(M)}^{s}\|u\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}+C(M)\|u\|_{L^{1}(M)}\,,

which adding for
i=1,...,N
shows (
26
).

Step 2.
We prove that

\iint_{M\times M}|u(p)-u(q)|^{2}K_{s}\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[u]_{BV(M)}^{s}\|u\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}\,,

(27)

an inequality which is interesting on its own. Considering
u=\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}}
then yields the Proposition.

The main observation is that the LHS of (
26
) is invariant under addition of a constant, while the RHS is not. This suggests to consider the function
v:=u-\frac{1}{{\rm Vol}(M)}\int_{M}u
instead of
u
in the inequality obtained in (
26
). Combining this with the
L^{1}
–Poincaré inequality on
M
allows us to estimate

\displaystyle\iint_{M\times M}

\displaystyle|u(p)-u(q)|^{2}K_{s}\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}=\iint_{M\times M}|v(p)-v(q)|^{2}K_{s}\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[v]_{BV(M)}^{s}\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}+C(M)\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}

\displaystyle=\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[u]_{BV(M)}^{s}\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}+C(M)\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{s}\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[u]_{BV(M)}^{s}\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}+C(M)[u]_{BV(M)}^{s}\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{1-s}[u]_{BV(M)}^{s}\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}^{1-s}\,.

Bounding
\|v\|_{L^{1}(M)}\leq 2\|u\|_{L^{1}(M)}
leads then to the bound (
27
), concluding the proof.
∎

3.1.3
Monotonicity formula

The monotonicity formula for
s
-minimal surfaces requires using the Caffarelli-Silvestre extension in one extra dimension, which we now recall.

Theorem 3.15

(
[
18
, Theorem 2.25 and Proposition 3.2]
)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold, let
s\in(0,2)
and
u:M\to\mathbb{R}
be in
H^{s/2}(M)
. Consider the product manifold
\widetilde{M}=M\times(0,+\infty)
endowed with the natural product metric
2
2

2

That is, the metric defined by
\widetilde{g}\big((\xi_{1},z_{1}),(\xi_{2},z_{2})\big)=g(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})+z_{1}z_{2}
, and where
\widetilde{\rm div}
and
\widetilde{\nabla}
denote the divergence and Riemannian gradient with respect to this product metric respectively.
. Then, there is a unique solution
U:M\times(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
among functions with
\int_{\widetilde{M}}|\widetilde{\nabla}U|^{2}z^{1-s}\,dVdz<\infty
to

\begin{cases}\widetilde{{\rm div}}(z^{1-s}\widetilde{\nabla}U)=0&\mbox{in}\hskip 7.0pt\widetilde{M}\,,\\
U(p,0)=u(p)&\mbox{for}\hskip 7.0ptp\in\partial\widetilde{M}=M\,,\end{cases}

(28)

and it satisfies

\displaystyle[u]^{2}_{H^{{s/2}}(M)}

\displaystyle=2\beta_{s}\int_{\widetilde{M}}|\widetilde{\nabla}U|^{2}z^{1-s}\,dVdz

(29)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{2\beta_{s}\int_{\widetilde{M}}|\widetilde{\nabla}V|^{2}z^{1-s}\,dVdz\hskip 7.0pt{\mbox{s.t.}}\hskip 7.0ptV(x,0)=u(x)\right\}\,,

(30)

where

\beta_{s}=\frac{2^{s-1}\Gamma(s/2)}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\,.

(31)

In (
28
) and (
30
), the boundary condition is to be interpreted in the trace sense.

We introduce some notational conventions for balls. For
r>0
and
p\in M
, we will denote

\displaystyle B_{r}(p)

\displaystyle=\big\{q\in M\,:\,d_{g}(q,p)<r\big\}\,,

(32)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{r}^{+}(p,0)

\displaystyle=\big\{(q,z)\in\widetilde{M}\,:\,d_{\widetilde{g}}((q,z),(p,0))<r\big\}\,,

\displaystyle\partial\widetilde{B}_{r}^{+}(p,0)

\displaystyle=\partial\left(\widetilde{B}_{r}^{+}(p,0)\right)\,,

\displaystyle\partial^{+}\widetilde{B}_{r}^{+}(p,0)

\displaystyle=\partial\widetilde{B}_{r}^{+}(p,0)\cap\{z>0\}\,.

In the next theorem, we use
\nabla
instead of
\widetilde{\nabla}
to denote the gradient in
\widetilde{M}
with respect to the product metric.

Theorem 3.16

(
Monotonicity formula

[
18
, Theorem 3.4]
)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be an
n
-dimensional, closed Riemannian manifold. Let
s\in(0,1)
and let
\Sigma
be an
s
-minimal surface. For
(p_{\circ},0)\in\widetilde{M}
define

\Phi(R):=\frac{1}{R^{n-s}}\left(\frac{\beta_{s}}{2}\int_{\widetilde{B}^{+}_{R}(p_{\circ},0)}z^{1-s}|\nabla U(p,z)|^{2}\,dV_{p}dz\right),

where
U
is the unique solution given by Theorem
3.15
with
u:M\to\mathbb{R}
given by
u=\chi_{\Sigma}-\chi_{\Sigma^{c}}
. Then, there exist constants
C=C(n)
and
R_{\rm max}=R_{\rm max}(M,p_{\circ})>0
with the following property: whenever
R_{\circ}\leq\textnormal{inj}_{M}(p_{\circ})/4
and
K
is an upper bound for all the sectional curvatures of
M
in
B_{R_{\circ}}(p_{\circ})
, then

R\mapsto\Phi(R)e^{C\sqrt{K}R}\hskip 7.0pt\textit{is nondecreasing for}\hskip 7.0ptR<R_{\circ}\,.

From the proper monotonicity formula in Theorem
3.16
, we now obtain a weaker form which involves the fractional perimeter on balls of
M
(instead of
\widetilde{M}
). This will be repeatedly (and crucially) used in what follows, since it gives the decay of the fractional perimeter with respect to the radius with the right rate. We note here that the proof can be slightly modified so that it recovers the monotonicity formula for classical minimal surfaces in the limit
s\to 1
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
E\subset M
be an
s
-minimal surface in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}\times[-1,1])
, and assume that
{\rm FA}_{1}(M,g,4,p_{0},\varphi)
holds.
Then there exists a constant
C
depending only on
n
such that, for any
0<r_{1}\leq r_{2}\leq 1
, we have that

\frac{(1-s)\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;B_{r_{1}}(p_{0}))}{r_{1}^{n-s}}\leq(1-s)C+C\frac{(1-s)\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;B_{r_{2}}(p_{0}))}{r_{2}^{n-s}}\,.

In particular, setting
r_{2}=1
, for all
0<r_{1}<\frac{1}{8}
we have that

\displaystyle\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;B_{r_{1}}(p_{0}))

\displaystyle\leq Cr_{1}^{n-s}(1+\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;B_{1}(p_{0})))

(33)

\displaystyle\leq Cr_{1}^{n-s}(1+\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;M))\,.

(34)

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\varphi}(x,z)=(\varphi(x),z)
be the natural local parametrisation of
\widetilde{M}
. If
8r_{1}>r_{2}
, letting
C=8^{n}
the Lemma is satisfied, therefore we can assume that
8r_{1}\leq\frac{1}{8}r_{2}
.

Step 1.
We show that, given
u:M\to[-1,1]
, we have the inequality

\iint_{(M\times M)\setminus(\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r}))}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q)\leq C\Big(r^{n-s}+\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4r}^{+})}z^{a}|\nabla U|^{2}\Big)\,.

An easy application of (
21
) from Proposition
3.9
with
\alpha=0
, considering the rescaled manifold
(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g)
and (a) in Remark
3.8
, allows us to bound

\displaystyle\iint_{(M\times M)\setminus(\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r}^{c})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r})^{c})}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q)\leq\iint_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q)+8\iint_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r})\times(M\setminus\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})^{c})}K(p,q)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\iint_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q)+C\int_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r})}r^{-s}\leq\iint_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q)+Cr^{n-s}\,.

We will now continue by proceeding as in
[
15
, Proposition 7.1]
. Let
\xi
be a standard cutoff on
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4}^{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, verifying that
\chi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{+}\cap\{z=0\}}\leq\xi\leq\chi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4}^{+}}
. Define then
\widetilde{\eta}_{r}=\xi(\cdot/r)\circ\widetilde{\varphi}^{-1}
, extended by zero outside of
\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4r}^{+})\subset\widetilde{M}
. Moreover, set
\eta_{r}(p)=\widetilde{\eta}_{r}(p,0):M\to\mathbb{R}
. Then,
\eta_{r}(p)
acts as a cutoff function on
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{4r})\subset M
which verifies that
\chi_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})}\leq\eta_{r}\leq\chi_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{4r})}
. We can then compute

\displaystyle\iint_{(M\times M)\setminus(\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r})^{c}\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r})^{c})}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q)\leq\iint_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2r})}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q)+Cr^{n-s}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\iint_{M\times M}((u\eta_{r})(p)-(u\eta_{r})(q))^{2}K(p,q)+Cr^{n-s}=[u\eta_{r}]_{H^{s/2}(M)}^{2}+Cr^{n-s}\leq C\Big(\int_{\widetilde{M}}z^{1-s}|\nabla(U\widetilde{\eta}_{r})|^{2}+r^{n-s}\Big)\,.

We have applied (
30
) in the last line, using that
U\widetilde{\eta}_{r}(p,0)=u\eta_{r}(p)
for all
p\in M
. We can now bound

\displaystyle\iint_{(M\times M)\setminus(\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r}))}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q)\leq C\Big(\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4r}^{+})}z^{1-s}|\nabla U|^{2}\widetilde{\eta}_{r}^{2}+\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4r}^{+})}z^{1-s}U^{2}|\nabla\widetilde{\eta}_{r}|^{2}+r^{n-s}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq C\Big(\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4r}^{+})}z^{1-s}|\nabla U|^{2}+r^{-2}\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4r}^{+})}z^{1-s}+r^{n-s}\Big)\leq C\Big(\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4r}^{+})}z^{1-s}|\nabla U|^{2}+r^{n-s}\Big)\,.

Step 2.
Applying Step 1 with
u=\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}}
and
r=r_{1}
, we find that

\frac{\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r_{1}}))}{r_{1}^{n-s}}\leq C+\frac{C}{r_{1}^{n-s}}\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{4r_{1}}^{+})}z^{1-s}|\nabla U|^{2}\,.

Applying then the monotonicity formula of Theorem
3.16
, recalling that we are assuming that
4r_{1}<\frac{1}{2}r_{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r_{1}}))}{r_{1}^{n-s}}

\displaystyle\leq C+\frac{C}{r_{2}^{n-s}}\int_{\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\frac{1}{2}r_{2}}^{+})}z^{1-s}|\nabla U|^{2}\,.

We now need essentially the converse of Step 1 to bound the RHS. This is
[
18
, Lemma 3.15]
, or more precisely the chain of inequalities at the end of its proof, and it allows us to conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r_{1}}))}{r_{1}^{n-s}}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\frac{1}{r_{2}^{n-s}}\iint_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r_{2}})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r_{2}})}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}K(p,q))\leq C+C\frac{\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r_{2}}))}{r_{2}^{n-s}}

as desired.
∎

3.2
Min-max procedure – The nonlocal volume spectrum

Critical points of (
13
) can be obtained using an equivariant min-max procedure, exploiting the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}
. In
[
19
]
, a “nonlocal spectrum” is defined employing as a min-max family of sets with
\mathfrak{p}
parameters the one consisting of odd images of the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}
into
H^{s/2}(M)
. This family has a very simple definition and served for the purposes of that article, but others could have been employed in an essentially exchangeable way, including families which come from a topological index; see
[
19
, Remark 3.1]
where this is briefly discussed. To obtain a Weyl Law, one wants to restrict instead to families which arise from some topological index.

Begin by defining

\text{ind}(A)=\sup\{k:\nexists\text{ any continuous odd map }g:A\to\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\backslash\{0\}\}

if
A\subset H^{s/2}(M)
is compact, symmetric (i.e.
A=-A
), and does not contain zero. Here odd means that
g(-x)=-g(x)
. Set moreover
\text{ind}(A)=\infty
if
A
contains
0
, and set
\text{ind}(\emptyset)=0
.

It is standard that this notion of index satisfies all the axioms of a topological index, including the one that we will need and which is its subadditivity:

Lemma 3.18
.

\textnormal{ind}(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\leq\textnormal{ind}(A_{1})+\textnormal{ind}(A_{2})

Proof.

We can assume that
\max\{\textnormal{ind}(A_{1}),\textnormal{ind}(A_{2})\}<\infty
.

By the definition of
ind
, there exist then odd maps
g_{1}:A_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{\textnormal{ind}(A_{1})}\backslash\{0\}
and
g_{2}:A_{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{\textnormal{ind}(A_{2})}\backslash\{0\}
. Extend them to maps
\tilde{g}_{1}
and
\tilde{g}_{2}
defined on all of
H^{s/2}(M)\backslash\{0\}
by the Tietze extension theorem. After antisymmetrizing, we can assume that they are also antisymmetric, and they still coincide with the original maps on
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
.

The map
(\tilde{g}_{1},\tilde{g}_{2})
restricted to
A:=A_{1}\cup A_{2}
is then an odd map from
A
into
\mathbb{R}^{\textnormal{ind}(A_{1})+\textnormal{ind}(A_{2})}\backslash\{0\}
, since either
\tilde{g}_{1}
or
\tilde{g}_{2}
is always nonzero on
A
. This means that
\textnormal{ind}(A)\leq\textnormal{ind}(A_{1})+\textnormal{ind}(A_{2})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

The family of sets to which the min-max procedure will be applied is

\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{A\subset H^{s/2}(M):A\text{ is compact, symmetric, satisfies }\mathcal{H}^{\mathfrak{p}}(A)<\infty\text{, and has ind}(A)\geq\mathfrak{p}+1\}\,,

(35)

where
\mathcal{H}^{\mathfrak{p}}
denotes the Hausdorff measure of dimension
\mathfrak{p}
.

For fixed
\varepsilon
, the min-max value of the family
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is defined as

c_{\varepsilon,s}(\mathfrak{p},M):=\inf_{A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\sup_{u\in A}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M).

(36)

Note that, defining
T(u):=\max\{-1,\min\{u,+1\}\}
the truncation of
u
between the values
\pm 1
, we have that
|T(u)|(x)\leq 1
for all
x\in M
and
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(T(u),M)\leq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)
. Moreover, since
T
is odd, is it immediate to see
{\rm ind}(T(A))\geq{\rm ind}(A)
. Hence

c_{\varepsilon,s}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\inf_{A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\sup_{u\in A}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)=\inf_{A\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\sup_{u\in A}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)\,,

where

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\{A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}:|u|\leq 1\hskip 7.0pt\textnormal{for all}\hskip 7.0ptu\in A\}.

The min-max values just defined are attained by critical points of the Allen-Cahn equation, and satisfy lower and upper bounds depending on
\mathfrak{p}
:

Theorem 3.19

(
Existence of min-max solutions
)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold. Fix
s_{0}\in(0,1)
, and let
s\in(s_{0},1)
. Then, for every
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}>0
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{p}})
, the min-max values of (
36
) satisfy

C^{-1}\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}\leq(1-s)c_{\varepsilon,s}(\mathfrak{p},M)\leq C\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}\,,\hskip 7.0pt\textnormal{for all}\,\,\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{p}})\,,

(37)

and moreover there exists
u_{\varepsilon,\mathfrak{p}}\in H^{s/2}(M)
critical point of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}
with
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u_{\varepsilon,\mathfrak{p}},M)=c_{\varepsilon,s}(\mathfrak{p},M)
and Morse index
m(u_{\varepsilon,\mathfrak{p}})\leq\mathfrak{p}
. Here
C
depends only on
M
and
s_{0}
.

Proof.

We explain the slight modifications needed with respect to
[
19
, Section 3.1]
in Appendix
A
.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

The min-max families employed also give lower bounds for the extended Morse index of the
u_{\varepsilon,\mathfrak{p}}
, i.e. the Morse index plus the nullity of the second variation of the Allen-Cahn energy. We will not use this in this work.

We now consider the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
for the min-max quantities. Define the “nonlocal volume widths”

l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}c_{\varepsilon,s}(\mathfrak{p},M)\,.

(38)

The existence of the limit in
\varepsilon
is obvious, as the Allen-Cahn energy of any function
u
is monotone nondecreasing in
\varepsilon
, and hence so is the value of
c_{\varepsilon,s}(\mathfrak{p},M)
. Furthermore, by the lower and upper bounds from Theorem
3.19
we know that

\frac{1}{C}\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}\leq(1-s)l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)\leq C\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}.

(39)

In
[
19
]
it is proved that critical points of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}
with bounded Morse index converge, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to
s
-minimal surfaces. A partial statement is the following:

Theorem 3.21

(
Convergence as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

[
19
]
)
.

Fix
s\in(0,1)
. Let
u_{\varepsilon_{j}}
be a sequence of solutions of (
14
) on
M
with parameters
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0
and Morse index at most
m
. Then, there exist a subsequence, still denoted by
u_{\varepsilon_{j}}
, and a nonlocal
s
-minimal surface
E\subset M
with Morse index at most
m
(in the sense of Definition
3.3
), such that

u_{\varepsilon_{j}}\xrightarrow{\,H^{s/2}\,}u_{0}=\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}}\,.

In particular
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{\rm Sob}(u_{\varepsilon_{j}},M)\to\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{\rm Sob}(u_{0},M)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{\rm Pot}(u_{\varepsilon_{j}},M)\to 0=\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{\rm Pot}(u_{0},M)
.

In addition, up to changing
E
in a set of measure zero, we have

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{p\in M\ :\liminf_{r\downarrow 0}\tfrac{|E\cap B_{r}(p)|}{|B_{r}(p)|}=1\Big\}\,,

(40)

\displaystyle M\setminus E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{p\in M\ :\limsup_{r\downarrow 0}\tfrac{|E\cap B_{r}(p)|}{|B_{r}(p)|}=0\Big\}\,,

(41)

\displaystyle\partial E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{p\in M\ :\tfrac{|E\cap B_{r}(p)|}{|B_{r}(p)|}\in[c,1-c]\quad\forall r\in(0,r_{\circ})\Big\}.

(42)

Finally, there is a universal constant
s_{0}\in(0,1)
such that if
s\in(s_{0},1)
, then
\partial E
is a smooth hypersurface outside from a set of Hausdorff dimension at most
n-5
.

Combined with Theorem
3.19
, this shows:

Corollary 3.22

(
Attainability of nonlocal widths
)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold. For every
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an
s
-minimal surface
E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}\subset M
such that
{\rm Per}_{s}(E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s})=l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)
. Moreover, there is a universal constant
s_{0}\in(0,1)
such that if
s\in(s_{0},1)
, then
\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
is a smooth hypersurface outside from a set of Hausdorff dimension at most
n-5
.

This gives Theorem
1.1
, with
\text{Per}_{s}(E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s})=l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)
instead of the different min-max values used in
[
19
]
.

4
The Weyl Law

4.1
The Weyl Law with the distance kernel

4.1.1
The distance kernel

The Allen-Cahn energies and fractional perimeters that we have (canonically) defined on a manifold
M
depend on a non-explicit kernel
K_{M,s}
. Since the proof of the Weyl Law will be based on comparison between a manifold and its subdomains, as well as between such subdomains and Euclidean subdomains,
we make the technical choice of first proving the Weyl Law for a different (non-canonical) definition of fractional perimeter and Allen-Cahn energy:

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian manifold or
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
\Omega
be an open subset of
M
. Define the space
H^{s/2,d}(\Omega)
by setting

[u]^{2}_{H^{s/2,d}(\Omega)}:=\iint_{\Omega\times\Omega}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}^{n+s}(p,q)}\,dV_{p}\,dV_{q}\,,

(43)

and define

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(v,\Omega):=\frac{1}{4}\iint_{\Omega\times\Omega}(v(p)-v(q))^{2}\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}^{n+s}(p,q)}dV_{p}dV_{q}+\varepsilon^{-s}\int_{\Omega}W(v)\,dx\,,

(44)

where
W(v)=\frac{1}{4}(1-v^{2})^{2}
.

Likewise, define the min-max value

c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega):=\inf_{A\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}}\sup_{u\in A}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,\Omega)\,,

(45)

where

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}=\{A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}:|u|\leq 1\ \forall u\in A\}

(46)

and

\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}=\{A\subset H^{s/2,d}(\Omega):A\text{ is compact, symmetric, satisfies }\mathcal{H}^{\mathfrak{p}}(A)<\infty\text{, and has ind}(A)\geq\mathfrak{p}+1\}\,,

and put

l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\,.

(47)

The lower and upper bounds (
39
), which we have proved in Theorem
37
, are immediately seen to also hold for
l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
with the same proof (since essentially the only property of the kernel
K_{M,s}
that they use is its comparability with
\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}^{n+s}(p,q)}
). Therefore, given
s_{0}\in(0,1)
, for
s\in(s_{0},1)
we have that

\frac{1}{C}\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}\leq(1-s)l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\leq C\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}

(48)

with
C=C(s_{0},\Omega)
.

We will first show a Weyl Law in this setting:

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed manifold of dimension
n
. There exists a universal
\tau(n,s)>0
such that

\lim_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\tau(n,s)\textnormal{vol}(M,g)^{\frac{n-s}{n}}\,.

(49)

We will afterwards show that this implies the Weyl Law with the canonical kernel
K_{M,s}
of (
2
), and in fact with the
same constant

\tau(n,s)
. This is reflective of the “homogenisation” property which is behind the Weyl Law in the first place.

4.1.2
Euclidean domain case

The Weyl Law (
49
) will be proved first for Euclidean domains with piecewise smooth boundary, adapting the reasoning in
[
42
]
to our case. As mentioned before, if
\Omega
is such a domain, the Allen-Cahn energy will be defined using (
44
) with the ambient Euclidean kernel
\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|x-y|^{n+s}}
.

The main tool is the so-called Lusternik-Schnirelman inequality, which compares the min-max quantities of domains with those for their subdomains:

Lemma 4.2

(
Lusternik–Schnirelman inequality
)
.

Let
\Omega
,
\{\Omega_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and
\{\Omega_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{N}
be bounded open subsets in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with piecewise smooth boundaries such that

1.

|\Omega|=|\Omega_{i}|=1
for
i=1,...,N

2.

\Omega_{i}^{*}
is similar to
\Omega_{i}
for
i=1,...,N

3.

\{\Omega_{i}^{*}\}
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
\Omega

Then

\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{-\frac{s}{n}}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i})-\frac{c}{pV},

(50)

where
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\lfloor\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|\rfloor
,
V=\min\{|\Omega_{i}^{*}|\}
and
c=\max_{i}\gamma(\Omega_{i})
. Here
\gamma(\Omega_{i})
is a bound for
\mathfrak{q}^{-s/n}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{q},\Omega_{i})
independent of
\mathfrak{q}
(which exists by (
48
)).

Proof.

For simplicity, we will write
c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
and
l^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
instead of
c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
and
l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
during the proof, and likewise for other instances of this notation such as for
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(u,\Omega)
instead of
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,\Omega)
. We shall use both notations interchangeably also in other proofs as long as there is no possible confusion. The proof consists of two steps.

Step 1.
We prove that

c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}^{*})\,,

(51)

i.e. that
c_{\varepsilon}
is superadditive in a certain sense.

Fix
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Choose some small room
\delta
that will be made to go to zero, and define
A_{i}:=\{u\in A:\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(u,\Omega_{i}^{*})\leq c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}^{*})-\delta\}
. By definition of
c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}^{*})
as the min-max value among sets in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}(\Omega_{i}^{*})
, the set
A_{i}
does not belong to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}(\Omega_{i}^{*})
, which shows that
\text{ind}(A_{i})\leq\mathfrak{p}_{i}
.

What we need to see to show (
51
) is precisely that there exists some
u\in A\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{N}A_{i}
: Then,

\sup_{v\in A}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(v,\Omega)\geq\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(u,\Omega)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(u,\Omega_{i}^{*})>\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}^{*})-N\delta\,,

which sending
\delta
to
0
and taking infima among all
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
gives
c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}^{*})
.

To see that
A\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{N}A_{i}\neq\emptyset
, observe that by Lemma
3.18

\text{ind}(\cup_{i=1}^{N}A_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\text{ind}(A_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{p}_{i}\leq\mathfrak{p}\,,

but on the other hand
\text{ind}(A)\geq\mathfrak{p}+1
by definition since
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Step 2.
Conclusion.

From (
51
), we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}^{*})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|^{\frac{n-s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon/|\Omega_{i}^{*}|^{1/n}}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}).

We have used that
\Omega_{i}
is isometric to
|\Omega_{i}^{*}|^{-\frac{1}{n}}\Omega_{i}^{*}
together with the scaling of the Allen-Cahn energy (
44
) under dilations.

Next, we multiply and divide by
\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{-\frac{s}{n}}
inside each term in the sum, and then use
\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}{\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|}\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|}\geq(1-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|})^{\frac{n}{s}}
which comes from
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\lfloor\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|\rfloor\geq\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|-1
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|^{\frac{n-s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon/|\Omega_{i}^{*}|^{1/n}}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|(\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}{\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|})^{\frac{s}{n}}\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{-\frac{s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon/|\Omega_{i}^{*}|^{1/n}}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|(1-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|})\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{-\frac{s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon/|\Omega_{i}^{*}|^{1/n}}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i}).

Now, we let
\varepsilon\to 0
and use that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|\leq|\Omega|=1
to find that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}l^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Omega_{i}^{*}|\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{-\frac{s}{n}}l^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega_{i})-\frac{\max_{j}\gamma(\Omega_{j})}{\mathfrak{p}V}\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Set
\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega):=\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
. We will first prove the Weyl Law for Euclidean cubes, which amounts to showing

Proposition 4.3
.

For the unit cube
C
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)=\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)
.

Once we prove this proposition, we will be able to designate the universal constant
\tau(n,s)
that will appear in the Weyl Law of an arbitrary manifold. Indeed, if Theorem
1.2
holds then Proposition
4.3
implies that the constant needs to be
\tau(n,s):=\lim_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)
.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Choose sequences
\{\mathfrak{p}_{k}\}
and
\{\mathfrak{q}_{j}\}
giving the values of the limsup and the liminf of
\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)
, respectively.

Fix
k
, and for all
j
large enough define
\delta_{j}=\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{k}}{\mathfrak{q}_{j}}<1
. We will use the Lusternik–Schnirelman inequality with
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
on the left side and
\mathfrak{p}_{k}
on the right side, together with the similarity between a cube and its subcubes, to prove that liminf
\geq
limsup. This will conclude the proof.

Let
N_{j}
be the maximal number of cubes
C_{i}^{*}
of volume
\delta_{j}
contained in
C
and with pairwise disjoint interiors. From the definition of
\delta_{j}
,
\lfloor\mathfrak{q}_{j}|C_{i}^{*}|\rfloor=\mathfrak{q}_{j}|C_{i}^{*}|=\mathfrak{p}_{k}
. Lemma
4.2
then gives

\displaystyle\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{q}_{j},C)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{j}}|C_{i}^{*}|\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{k},C)-\frac{\gamma(C)}{\mathfrak{q}_{j}\delta_{j}}=N_{j}\delta_{j}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{k},C)-\frac{\gamma(C)}{\mathfrak{p}_{k}}.

(52)

Since
N_{j}\delta_{j}
tends to the volume of
C
by definition of
N_{j}
, letting
j\to\infty
we get that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)\geq\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{k},C)-\frac{\gamma(C)}{\mathfrak{p}_{k}}.

Sending now
k
to infinity, we arrive at
\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)\geq\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)
.
∎

Next, we will consider an arbitrary Lipschitz domain
\Omega
with piecewise smooth boundary (assumed to be of volume 1, after scaling) and show that
\lim_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
exists and is equal to
\tau(n,s)=\lim_{p}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)
:

Proposition 4.4

(Weyl Law for Euclidean domains)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Lipschitz domain with piecewise smooth boundary. Then

\lim_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)=\tau(n,s)\textnormal{vol}(\Omega)^{\frac{n-s}{n}}\,,

(53)

where
\tau(n,s)=\lim_{p}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)>0
is a universal constant depending only on
n
and
s
.

Proof.

By scaling, we can assume that
{\rm vol}(\Omega)=1
.

Step 1.
We prove that
\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\geq\tau(n,s)
.

Fix
\delta>0
. We can find a family of
N
similar cubes
C_{i}^{*}
with volume
\delta_{i}
inside
\Omega
with pairwise disjoint interiors and with total volume
\sum_{i=1}^{N}|C_{i}^{*}|\geq(1-\delta)
. Applying Lemma
4.2
,

\displaystyle\tilde{l}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}|C_{i}^{*}|\tilde{l}(\lfloor\mathfrak{p}\delta_{i}\rfloor,C)-\frac{\gamma(c)}{\mathfrak{p}\min_{i}\delta_{i}}\,.

(54)

Taking
\liminf
on both sides, we find for every
\delta>0
that

\displaystyle\liminf_{p}\tilde{l}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\geq(1-\delta)\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}(\mathfrak{p},C)=(1-\delta)\tau(n,s)\,,

which gives the conclusion after letting
\delta\to 0
.

Step 2.

\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\leq\tau(n,s)
.

We will need the following simple result, corresponding to
[
42
, Lemma 3.5]
:

Claim.
We can find a family
\{\Omega_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of
C
with disjoint interiors, all of them similar to
\Omega
, such that given
\varepsilon>0
there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{i})\geq 1-\varepsilon
.

Proof of the Claim:

Let
\Omega_{1}
be similar to
\Omega
and contained in
C
, and write
v=\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{1})
. Using the notation and reasoning in
[
42
]
, define
R_{1}
to be the closure of
C\setminus\Omega_{1}
. We can find disjoint cubes
\{C_{i,1}\}_{i=1}^{Q_{1}}
contained in
R_{1}
and such that
\sum_{i=1}^{Q_{1}}\textnormal{vol}(C_{i,1})\geq\textnormal{vol}(R_{1})/2
. Now, for each
i=1,...,Q_{1}
, we know that there exists a region
\Omega_{i,1}\subset C_{i,1}
similar to
\Omega
and with volume
v\textnormal{vol}(C_{i,1})
, by correspondence with the case of
\Omega_{1}
and
C
. Let
\Omega_{2}=\cup_{1\leq i\leq Q_{1}}\Omega_{i,1}
.

The next step is to define
R_{2}=C\setminus(\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2})
. Once again, we can find disjoint cubes
\{C_{i,2}\}_{i=1}^{Q_{2}}
contained in
R_{2}
and such that
\sum_{i=1}^{Q_{2}}\textnormal{vol}(C_{i,2})\geq\textnormal{vol}(R_{2})/2
, and regions
\Omega_{i,2}\subset C_{i,2}
for each
i=1,...,Q_{2}
with volume
v\textnormal{vol}(C_{i,2})
. We can now define
\Omega_{3}=\cup_{1\leq i\leq Q_{2}}\Omega_{i,2}
, and repeat the procedure inductively.

We claim that for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists an
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{i})\geq 1-\varepsilon
. Indeed, by construction,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{i})+v(1-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{i}))/2=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{i})(1-v/2)+v/2
. This shows that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{i})\geq v/2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-v/2)^{i}
, and the right side goes to
1
as
n\to\infty
.
∎

We proceed with the proof of Step 2. Consider the
\{\Omega_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
from the claim above. Given
\varepsilon>0
, select
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\textnormal{vol}(\Omega_{i})\geq 1-\varepsilon
. Moreover, given
\delta>0
, let
\{C_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N_{\delta}}
be a maximal collection of
N_{\delta}
cubes contained in
C
with volume
\delta
and pairwise disjoint interiors; in particular,
\delta N_{\delta}\to 1
as
\delta\to 0
. For each
C_{j}
, by correspondence with the case of
C
we can find regions
\{\Omega_{i,j}\}_{i=1}^{N}
inside
C_{j}
with pairwise disjoint interiors, similar to
\Omega
and such that
|\Omega_{i,j}|=\delta|\Omega_{i}|
.

The Lusternik-Schinerlman inequality of Lemma
4.2
tells us then that, given
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\lfloor\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{i,j}|\rfloor=\lfloor\mathfrak{p}\delta|\Omega_{i}|\rfloor
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\delta}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\Omega_{i,j}|\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega)-\frac{\gamma(\Omega)}{\mathfrak{p}\delta v}=N_{\delta}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta|\Omega_{i}|\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\Omega)-\frac{\gamma(\Omega)}{\mathfrak{p}\delta v}\,.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{N}
; selecting
\delta(\mathfrak{p})=\frac{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}|\Omega_{1}|}
, we have that
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\lfloor\mathfrak{q}\frac{|\Omega_{i}|}{|\Omega_{1}|}\rfloor
, and in particular
\mathfrak{p}_{1}=\mathfrak{q}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)

\displaystyle\geq\delta(\mathfrak{p})N_{\delta(\mathfrak{p})}\Big[|\Omega_{1}|\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{q},\Omega)+\sum_{i=2}^{N}|\Omega_{i}|\tilde{l}^{d}(\Big\lfloor\mathfrak{q}\frac{|\Omega_{i}|}{|\Omega_{1}|}\Big\rfloor,\Omega)\Big]-\frac{|\Omega_{1}|\gamma(\Omega)}{\mathfrak{q}\delta v}\,.

(55)

Taking the limit in
\mathfrak{p}
, since
\delta(\mathfrak{p})N_{\delta(\mathfrak{p})}\to 1
we get that

\displaystyle\tau(n,s)\geq\Big[|\Omega_{1}|\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{q},\Omega)+\sum_{i=2}^{N}|\Omega_{i}|\tilde{l}^{d}(\Big\lfloor\mathfrak{q}\frac{|\Omega_{i}|}{|\Omega_{1}|}\Big\rfloor,\Omega)\Big]-\frac{|\Omega_{1}|\gamma(\Omega)}{\mathfrak{q}\delta v}\,.

Choosing now
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}_{k}
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{\mathfrak{q}_{k}}(\Omega)=\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\Omega)
and taking
\liminf_{k}
in the inequality, we get that

\displaystyle\tau(n,s)

\displaystyle\geq\Big[|\Omega_{1}|\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)+\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\sum_{i=2}^{N}|\Omega_{i}|\Big]\geq\Big[|\Omega_{1}|\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)+\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)(1-|\Omega_{1}|-\varepsilon)\Big]\,.

Finally, using Step 1 which stated that
\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\geq\tau(n,s)
we find that

\displaystyle(|\Omega_{1}|+\varepsilon)\tau(n,s)\geq|\Omega_{1}|\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\,,

which finishes the proof after letting
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

4.1.3
Closed manifold case – Proof of Proposition
4.1

Let
(M,g)
be a closed manifold. We will use the Weyl Law for Euclidean domains that we just proved to show the Weyl Law for closed manifolds, following as before the strategy in
[
42
]
. For that, we will need to be able to compare min-max energies on a domain of a closed manifold with min-max energies on a Euclidean domain, for which the next simple lemma will prove useful.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(M_{1},g_{1})
and
(M_{2},g_{2})
be Riemannian manifolds, and let
\Omega_{1}\subset M_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}\subset M_{2}
be subdomains with piecewise
\mathcal{C}^{1}
boundary. Let
F:\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{2}
be a
(1+\delta)
-biLipschitz diffeomorphism, meaning that
\max\{{\rm Lip}(F),{\rm Lip}(F^{-1})\}\leq(1+\delta)
. Then, if
u\in H^{s/2,d}(\Omega_{2})
, it holds that
u\circ F\in H^{s/2,d}(\Omega_{1})
and

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u\circ F,\Omega_{1})\leq(1+\delta)^{3n+s}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,\Omega_{2})\,.

The Lipschitz constant
{\rm Lip}(F)
of
F
is computed using the ambient Riemannian distances of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
.

Proof.

By definition
\text{dist}_{M_{2}}(F(p),F(q))\leq\text{Lip}(F)\text{dist}_{M_{1}}(p,q)
. Moreover, letting
JF^{-1}
denote the Jacobian of the map
F^{-1}
, we have the bound

|JF^{-1}|(p)\leq\|DF^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{n}\leq\text{Lip}(F^{-1})^{n}\,.

Then, changing variables with
F
and using the facts above, we can compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u\circ F

\displaystyle,\Omega_{1})=\frac{1}{4}\iint_{\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1}}dp\,dq\,(u\circ F(p)-u\circ F(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M_{1}}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{s}}\int_{\Omega_{1}}dp\,W(u\circ F(p))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{\rm Lip}(F)^{n+s}}{4}\iint_{\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1}}dp\,dq\,(u\circ F(p)-u\circ F(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M_{2}}(F(p),F(q))^{-(n+s)}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{s}}\int_{\Omega_{1}}dp\,W(u\circ F)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm Lip}(F)^{n+s}}{4}\iint_{\Omega_{2}\times\Omega_{2}}dp\,dq\,(u(p)-u(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M_{2}}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}|JF^{-1}(p)||JF^{-1}(q)|+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{s}}\int_{\Omega_{2}}dp\,W(u(p))|JF^{-1}(p)|

\displaystyle\leq\{{\rm Lip}(F)^{n+s}\|DF^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{2n},\|DF^{-1}\|_{\infty}^{n}\}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}(u,\Omega_{2})\leq(1+\delta)^{3n+s}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,\Omega_{2})\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

We show Proposition
4.1
in two comparisons. We start with the simpler one:

Proposition 4.6
.

\liminf_{p}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)\geq\tau(n,s)\text{{vol}}(M,g)^{\frac{n-s}{n}}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume (by scaling) that
\textnormal{vol}(M,g)=1
. Let
\delta>0
, and select disjoint geodesic balls
B_{i}
inside
M
of radius
r_{i}\leq\bar{r}
very small such that
\sum\textnormal{vol}(B_{i})\geq\textnormal{vol}(M)/(1+\delta)=1/(1+\delta)
. We know that
c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)\geq\sum c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},B_{i})
with
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\lfloor\mathfrak{p}\textnormal{vol}(B_{i})\rfloor
, by Step
1
in the proof of Lemma
4.2
. Consider now normal coordinates centered at the centers of the
B_{i}
, with associated diffeomorphisms
F_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{i}\to B_{i}
going from Euclidean balls
\mathcal{B}_{i}
to the geodesic balls
B_{i}
. Then, thanks to the compactness of
M
we have that

1-\delta(\bar{r})\leq{\rm Lip}(F_{i})\leq 1+\delta(\bar{r})\,,

where
\delta(\bar{r})\to 0
as
\bar{r}\to 0
.
Then Lemma
4.5
immediately gives (after taking min-max values) that, denoting by
\mathcal{B}
the Euclidean ball of volume
1
,

c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},B_{i})\geq(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i})=(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}|\mathcal{B}_{i}|^{\frac{n-s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon|\mathcal{B}_{i}|^{-\frac{1}{n}}}{1+\delta(\bar{r})}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathcal{B})\,,

thus

c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)\geq\sum_{i}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},B_{i})\geq(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}\sum_{i}|\mathcal{B}_{i}|^{\frac{n-s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon|\mathcal{B}_{i}|^{-\frac{1}{n}}}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathcal{B})\,.

Multiplying by
\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}
and manipulating a bit as in the proof of Lemma
4.2
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)

\displaystyle\geq(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}\sum_{i}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{s}{n}}|\mathcal{B}_{i}|^{\frac{n-s}{n}}c_{\varepsilon|\mathcal{B}_{i}|^{-\frac{1}{n}}}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle=(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}\sum_{i}|\mathcal{B}_{i}|(\frac{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}{\mathfrak{p}|\mathcal{B}_{i}|})^{\frac{s}{n}}\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{-s/n}c_{\varepsilon|\mathcal{B}_{i}|^{-\frac{1}{n}}}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\geq(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}\sum_{i}|B_{i}|(\frac{|B_{i}|}{|\mathcal{B}_{i}|}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{p}|\mathcal{B}_{i}|})^{\frac{s}{n}}\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{-s/n}c_{\varepsilon|\mathcal{B}_{i}|^{-\frac{1}{n}}}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathcal{B})\,.

Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)

\displaystyle\geq(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}\sum_{i}|B_{i}|(\frac{|B_{i}|}{|\mathcal{B}_{i}|}-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{p}|\mathcal{B}_{i}|})^{\frac{s}{n}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p}_{i},\mathcal{B})\,.

(56)

Taking then
\liminf
in
\mathfrak{p}
on both sides, and using the Weyl Law for the Euclidean domain
\mathcal{B}
(which is a ball with volume
1
), we get that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)

\displaystyle\geq(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}\sum_{i}|B_{i}|(\frac{|B_{i}|}{|\mathcal{B}_{i}|})^{\frac{s}{n}}\tau(n,s)

Using that
{\rm Lip}(F_{i}^{-1})\leq 1+\delta(\bar{r})
and
\sum_{i}|B_{i}|\geq 1/(1+\delta)
, we find that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)

\displaystyle\geq(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+s)}(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-s}\sum_{i}|B_{i}|\tau(n,s)\geq(1+\delta(\bar{r}))^{-(3n+2s)}(1+\delta)^{-1}\tau(n,s)\,.

Letting
\delta\to 0
and
\bar{r}\to 0
, we conclude the result.
∎

We now prove the opposite inequality:

Proposition 4.7
.

\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)\leq\tau(n,s)\text{{vol}}(M,g)^{\frac{n-s}{n}}
.

We will need the following auxiliary result.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
N
be a compact manifold, possibly with boundary. There exists a decomposition
\{C_{i}\}_{i}
of
N
into connected Lipschitz sets such that the
C_{i}
intersect only at the boundaries and for each
i
there exists a
(1+\delta)
-biLipschitz equivalence
F_{i}:C_{i}\to\mathcal{C}_{i}
which maps
C_{i}
to a Euclidean domain
\mathcal{C}_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. More precisely, the
F_{i}
are given by the restriction of geodesic normal coordinate maps to the
C_{i}
.

Proof of the lemma.

This simple result is proved in
[
42
]
. One first covers
N
with small metric balls (possibly centered at boundary points)
B_{r/2}(p_{1}),...,B_{r/2}(p_{d})
. One then constructs the
C_{i}
as follows: Put
\widetilde{C}_{1}=B_{r}(p_{1})
, and inductively set
\widetilde{C}_{k+1}=B_{r_{k+1}}(p_{k+1})\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{k}\widetilde{C}_{k}
, where
r_{k+1}\in[r/2,r]
is selected so that
\partial B_{r_{k+1}}
intersects the boundaries of
\widetilde{C}_{1},...,\widetilde{C}_{k}
transversally. Finally, one defines the collection
\{C_{i}\}_{i}
by considering the connected components of the
\widetilde{C}_{i}
. If
r
is small enough, the normal coordinate maps of the balls
B_{r}(p_{1}),...,B_{r}(p_{d})
are biLipschitz equivalences with constants close to
1
, thus their restrictions to the
C_{i}
act as the
F_{i}
we are looking for.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.7
.

The proof is split into four steps.

Step 1.

We consider a decomposition
\{C_{i}\}_{i}
of
M
as in the Lemma, with associated
(1+\delta)
-biLipschitz equivalences
F_{i}:C_{i}\to\mathcal{C}_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By translating the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
if necessary, we can assume that they are pairwise disjoint; this gives rise to a Euclidean domain
\widetilde{\Omega}=\bigcup\mathcal{C}_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Construct then the connected Euclidean domain
\Omega=\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{j}T_{j}
, where the
T_{j}
are small tubes connecting the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and with total volume less than
\delta\textnormal{vol}(M)
, so that

\displaystyle{\rm vol}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\leq{\rm vol}(\widetilde{\Omega})+\delta{\rm vol}(M)=\sum_{i}{\rm vol}(\mathcal{C}_{i})+\delta{\rm vol}(M)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)^{n}\sum_{i}{\rm vol}(C_{i})+\delta{\rm vol}(M)\leq(1+2\delta)^{n}{\rm vol}(M).

(57)

To prove the Proposition, given an admissible set
A
in
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(\Omega)
we need to construct an admissible set
A^{\prime}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M)
with energy not surpassing that of
A
by much. We just need an equivariant assignation from functions on
\Omega
to functions on
M
which doesn’t increase energy by more than a little.

Let, then,
u\in H^{s/2,d}(\Omega)
with
|u|\leq 1
; it suffices to consider functions with absolute value at most one by (
45
)-(
47
). Thanks to our functional setting, we can obtain our desired function
U
on
H^{s/2,d}(M)
simply by composition:
Given
i
, define the function
U\big|_{C_{i}}
on
C_{i}
by
U\big|_{C_{i}}=u\circ F_{i}
. Since
M=\cup_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}
and the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
intersect only on a set of zero Lebesgue measure, we get a well-defined function
U\in L^{2}(M)
. Let
f:H^{s/2,d}(\Omega)\to H^{s/2,d}(M)
be defined by
f(u):=U
. Then, given
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(\Omega)
, we set
A^{\prime}=f(A)
. All of the properties in (
35
) are easily seen to be satisfied by
A^{\prime}
if they are by
A
, with the exception of the Hausdorff measure condition in (
35
). In fact,
A^{\prime}
might not be in
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M)
, but we can find a set
A^{\prime}_{\delta}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M)
which is arbitrarily close to
A^{\prime}
in terms of Allen–Cahn energies, which allows us to argue as if
A^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M)
without loss of generality. The set
A^{\prime}_{\delta}
is constructed by regularisation as in the proof of Theorem
3.19
, using a cutoff depending on the distance to the boundaries of the
C_{i}
; we postpone this construction to the last step of the proof.

Step 2.

Our job now is to compare the energy of
U
with the energy of
u
. First, we bound the contributions of the interactions between different pieces, using that
|U|\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U,M)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U,C_{i})+\sum_{i\neq j}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{j}}(U(p)-U(q))^{2}\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U,C_{i})+\sum_{i\neq j}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{j}}4\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U,C_{i})+\sum_{i\neq j}\iint_{C_{i}\times(M\setminus C_{i})}4\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U,C_{i})+c\sum_{i,j}\text{Per}_{s}^{d}(C_{i})\,.

Here the fractional perimeter
\text{Per}_{s}^{d}
is of course understood as being defined with
\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}^{n+s}}
as the associated kernel. Since the
C_{i}
are Lipschitz domains, which in particular have bounded classical perimeter, we have that

{\rm Per}_{s}^{d}(C_{i})\leq\frac{C}{1-s}{\rm Per}(C_{i})^{s}<\infty\,,

(58)

see (
25
).

Now, by Lemma
4.5
we can bound
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U,C_{i})\leq(1+\delta)^{3n+s}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(u,C_{i})
. Substituting, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U,M)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)^{3n+s}\sum_{i}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(u,C_{i})+c\sum_{i,j}\text{Per}_{s}^{d}(C_{i})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)^{3n+s}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(u,\Omega)+c\sum_{i,j}\text{Per}_{s}^{d}(C_{i})\,.

(59)
