In particular, this shows that
U\in H^{s/2,d}(M)
.

Step 3.

Let
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}
. Taking min-max values in (
59
), letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, and dividing by
\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}
, we arrive at

\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)\leq(1+\delta)^{3n+s}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)+\frac{c}{\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}}\sum_{i,j}\text{Per}_{s}^{d}(C_{i})\,.

(60)

Taking
\limsup
in
\mathfrak{p}
on both sides, using the Weyl Law of Proposition (
4.4
) for the Euclidean domain
\Omega
, and using (
57
), we get that

\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}}\tilde{l}_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)\leq(1+\delta)^{3n+s}\tau(n,s)\text{{vol}}(\Omega)^{\frac{n-s}{n}}\leq(1+\delta)^{3n+s}(1+2\delta)^{n-s}\tau(n,s)\text{{vol}}(M,g)^{\frac{n-s}{n}}.

We conclude by letting
\delta\to 0
.

Step 4.
To conclude, it only remains to deal with the technical Hausdorff measure issue from Step 1. We give the necessary modifications in Appendix
B
.
∎

We can now conclude:

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

It follows from the combination of Propositions
4.6
and
4.7
.
∎

4.2
Equivalence with the canonical Sobolev kernel – Proof of Theorem
1.2

In this subsection we show that using the canonical kernel
K_{M,s}(p,q)
for the Sobolev energy
H^{s/2}(M)
we obtain exactly the same nonlocal Weyl Law. We use the same notation as in the previous sections, so that
l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)
denotes the limit in (
38
) and which uses the kernel
K_{M,s}(p,q)
in the definition (
13
) of the Allen–Cahn energy, and likewise for
l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)
and the associated kernel
\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}^{n+s}(p,q)}
.

We will need two preliminary results.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
M
be a closed manifold, and let
s_{0}\in(0,1)
. Then, there exists
C=C(M,s_{0})
such that if
s\in(s_{0},1)
, then

\Big|K_{M,s}(p,q)-\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\Big|\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}\,.

In other words, the difference between the kernels in the left hand side is of lower order.

Proof.

As stated in Remark
3.6
, there is
R_{0}>0
depending on
M
such that the assumptions
{\rm FA}_{1}(M,g,R_{0},p,\varphi_{p})
are satisfied at every
p\in M
, with
\varphi_{p}
given by the restriction of the exponential map centered at
p
to
\mathcal{B}_{R_{0}}(0)
.

Let then
p
and
q
be such that
\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)\leq R_{0}/4
. We will apply Proposition
3.9
with
x=0=\varphi_{p}^{-1}(p)
, so that
A(x)=Id
since the metric in normal coordinates is Euclidean at the origin. Letting
y=\varphi^{-1}(q)
, (
18
) then gives that

\displaystyle\Big|K(p,q)-\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|x-y|^{n+s}}\Big|\leq R_{0}^{-1}\frac{C(n,s)}{|x-y|^{n+s-1}}\,,

where
C(n,s)
is uniform for
s\in(s_{0},1)
.

Since
\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)=|x-y|
thanks to having chosen normal coordinates centered at
p
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\Big|K(p,q)-\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}\,.

Consider now
p
and
q
such that
\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)\geq R_{0}/4
instead. The bound

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\leq C(n,s_{0})\frac{R_{0}/4}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}=\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}

is then immediate. Moreover, applying (
20
) with
\alpha=0
and
R=R_{0}/4
we can bound

\displaystyle K(p,q)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(n,s_{0})}{(R_{0}/4)^{n+s}}=C(M,s_{0})\,.

Since
\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)\leq{\rm diam}(M)<\infty
, we can then deduce that

\displaystyle\Big|K(p,q)-\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq K(p,q)+\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\leq C(M,s_{0})+\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}\leq\frac{C(M,s_{0})}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}\,.

Combining the results for
\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)\leq R_{0}/4
and
\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)\geq R_{0}/4
, we reach the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

The function spaces
H^{s/2}(M)
and
H^{s/2,d}(M)
coincide, and the associated fractional Sobolev norms are equivalent. Moreover, if
s_{0}\in(0,1)
and
s\in(s_{0},1)
, given
\delta>0
we can bound

|\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)|\leq C(M,s_{0})\big[C(\delta)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}^{2}+\delta\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)\big]\,

for all
u\in H^{s/2}(M)\cap L^{\infty}(M)
.

Proof of the Lemma.

We divide the proof in two steps. We will omit the dependence of the constants on
s_{0}
.

Step 1.
We show that
u\in H^{s/2}(M)
if and only if
u\in H^{s/2,d}(M)
.

Proposition
4.9
shows that there is some
R(M)>0
such that, for
p
and
q
with
{\rm dist}(p,q)\leq R(M)
,

\frac{c(M)}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\leq K_{M,s}(p,q)\leq\frac{C(M)}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\,,

where
0<c(M)\leq C(M)<\infty
. Moreover, it gives that

K_{M,s}(p,q)\leq C(M)\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}

for
{\rm dist}(p,q)\geq R(M)
as well. To also get a lower bound in this inequality, observe that, by strict positivity of the heat kernel at a fixed time and continuity,

H_{M}(p,q,t)\geq c(M)>0

for
t\in[1,2]
and all
p,q
in
M
, so that if
{\rm dist}(p,q)\geq R(M)
we can bound

K_{M,s}(p,q)=\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}H_{M}(p,q,t)\frac{dt}{t^{1+s/2}}\geq c(M)\geq\frac{c(M)}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\,.

Putting everything together, we deduce that

\frac{c(M)}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\leq K_{M,s}(p,q)\leq\frac{C(M)}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\,,

(61)

for any
p,q\in M
. This shows that
u\in H^{s/2}(M)
if and only if
u\in H^{s/2,d}(M)
, and that the associated fractional Sobolev norms are equivalent.

Step 2.
To obtain the inequality in the Lemma, we make our comparison precise.

Using Proposition
4.9
once again,

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Bigg|\iint_{M\times M}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}\Big[K_{M,s}(p,q)-\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\Big]\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\iint_{M\times M}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}\frac{C_{1}(M)}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}\,.

Let
\delta>0
. There is some
R(M,\delta)>0
be such that if
\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)\leq R(M,\delta)
, then

\frac{C_{1}(M)}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}\leq\delta\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}\,.

Moreover, if
\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)\geq R(M,\delta)
then

\frac{C(M)}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s-1}}\leq C(M,\delta)\,.

Substituting these bounds, we deduce that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)|

\displaystyle\leq\delta\frac{1}{4}\iint_{M\times M}(u(p)-u(q))^{2}\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{n+s}}+C(M,\delta)\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}^{2}\,,

which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

We are ready to give:

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

By Proposition
4.1
, which shows the Weyl Law for the
l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)
, it suffices to see that

\lim_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\big|\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)-\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)\big|=0\,.

(62)

For that, let
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Lemma
4.10
tells us that this is equivalent to
A
being in
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}
, since the Hausdorff dimension of a set is preserved under equivalent metrics, and moreover that (omitting the dependence on
s_{0}
)

|\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)|\leq C(M,\delta)+\delta\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)\,

for every
u\in A
, since
|u|\leq 1
by definition of
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

In particular,

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)\leq C(M,\delta)+(1+\delta)\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)\,,

so that

\displaystyle c_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)

\displaystyle=\inf_{A\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\max_{u\in A}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)\leq C(M,\delta)+\inf_{A\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\max_{u\in A}(1+\delta)\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)=C(M,\delta)+(1+\delta)c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)\,.

Similarly, we obtain that

(1-\delta)c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)\leq C(M,\delta)+c_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)\,.

In other words, we have shown that

|c_{\varepsilon,s}(u,M)-c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)|\leq C(M,\delta)+\delta c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M)\,;

letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we get that

|l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)-l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)|\leq C(M,\delta)+\delta l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)\,.

(63)

If we now multiply by
\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}
and take
\limsup
in
\mathfrak{p}
on both sides, we find that

\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}}|\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)-\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)|\leq\delta\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}}[\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)]\,.

We know that
\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}}[\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)]
is a bounded quantity by (
48
), so letting
\delta\to 0
the right-hand side goes to
0
and we conclude the proof.

∎

4.3
The Weyl Law in the classical limit
s\to 1
– Proof of Theorem
1.3

This section proves Theorem
1.3
, which we restate for the reader’s convenience:

Theorem 4.11

(
Weyl Law for classical minimal surfaces
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed manifold of dimension
n
. There exists a universal constant
\tau(n,1)>0
, depending only on
n
, such that

\lim_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{1}{n}}l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\tau(n,1)\textnormal{vol}(M,g)^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\,.

(64)

Proof.

Recall that

l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\liminf_{s\to 1}[(1-s)l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)]\,.

In particular, by (
39
) we deduce the bounds

c(M)\mathfrak{p}^{1/n}\leq l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)\leq C(M)\mathfrak{p}^{1/n}\,.

(65)

We then split the proof into two steps.

Step 1
.
Define analogously

l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M):=\liminf_{s\to 1}[(1-s)l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)]

(66)

and
\tilde{l}_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{1}{n}}l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)
. We will first prove (
4
) for the quantities
l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)
, meaning that

\displaystyle\lim_{p\to\infty}\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{1}{n}}l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\tau(n,1)\textnormal{vol}(M)^{\frac{n-1}{n}}

(67)

for some dimensional constant
\tau(n,1)
. To see this, recall that in the proof of the Weyl Law for
s<1
we found several inequalities for quantities of the type
\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)
, and then we took
\mathfrak{p}\to\infty
on those inequalities to obtain the results leading to the Weyl Law. Roughly, if we first multiply those inequalities by
(1-s)
and take
\liminf_{s\to 1}
instead, and only then send
\mathfrak{p}\to\infty
, we will find (
67
). More precisely, we proceed as follows:

•

We first obtain the analogue of Lemma
4.3
for
l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)
, by multiplying (
52
) by
(1-s)
and taking
\liminf_{s\to 1}
, and then finishing the proof with the argument that ensues, up to selecting the sequences
\mathfrak{p}_{k}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
so that they give the values of the limsup and the liminf of
l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)
instead.

This shows that

\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)=\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},C)

(68)

for the unit cube
C
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and it allows us to define
\tau(n,1)
as the common limit.

•

We then prove the analogue for
l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
of Proposition
4.4
, meaning that

\lim_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)=\tau(n,1)\textnormal{vol}(\Omega)^{\frac{n-1}{n}}

(69)

for any Euclidean domain
\Omega
.

To show that

\liminf_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\geq\tau(n,1)\textnormal{vol}(\Omega)^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\,,

we use the first step of the proof of Proposition
4.4
: We first multiply (
54
) by
(1-s)
and take
\liminf_{s}
. We then finish the proof by taking
\liminf_{p}
on both sides of the resulting inequality, applying (
68
), and sending
\delta\to 0
.

Likewise, to show that

\limsup_{\mathfrak{p}\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)\leq\tau(n,1)\textnormal{vol}(\Omega)^{\frac{n-1}{n}}\,,

we use the second step of the proof of Proposition
4.4
: We multiply (
55
) by
(1-s)
, take
\liminf_{s}
, and follow the same arguments of the proof but selecting the subsequence
\mathcal{q}_{k}
so that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{1}^{d}(\mathcal{q}_{k},\Omega)=\limsup_{p\to\infty}\tilde{l}_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},\Omega)
.

•

To prove the Weyl Law in (
67
) for a closed manifold
M
, we prove the analogues of Proposition
4.6
and Proposition
4.7
. They follow from multiplying the inequalities (
56
) and (
60
) by
(1-s)
, taking
\liminf_{s\to 1}
, and replicating the few steps that follow them in the proofs (using in particular the Weyl Law (
69
) for Euclidean domains and
s=1
, which we have just argued to be true, instead of the one for
s<1
). We remark that by (
25
) we have
\limsup_{s\to 1}(1-s)\text{Per}_{s}^{d}(\mathcal{C}_{i})\leq C
, so that the second term on the right hand side of (
60
) remains bounded when arguing as indicated.

This concludes the proof of (
67
).

Step 2
. To prove the Weyl Law for
l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)
from the one for
l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)
in (
67
), we will actually prove that
l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)=l_{1}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)
for any
\mathfrak{p}
, a result which is interesting on its own and which of course gives (
64
) from (
67
). It suffices, then, to show that

\lim_{s\to 1}|(1-s)l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)-(1-s)l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)|=0\,.

(70)

By (
63
), we already know that

|l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)-l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)|\leq C(M,\delta)+\delta l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)

for any
\delta>0
.

Multiplying by
(1-s)
and taking
\limsup
in
s
on both sides, we find that

\limsup_{s\to 1}|(1-s)l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)-(1-s)l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)|\leq\delta\limsup_{s\to 1}[(1-s)l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)]\,.

Since
\limsup_{s\to 1}[(1-s)l_{s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M)]
is a bounded quantity by (
48
), letting
\delta\to 0
we obtain the desired result.
∎

5
Compactness of
s
-minimal surfaces with bounded index as
s\to 1

5.1
First and second variation formulas

We obtain for the first time a formula for the second variation of the fractional perimeter on a Riemannian manifold. The Euclidean formula, which was obtained in
[
26
, Theorem 6.1]
, contains a difference of normal vectors based at different points, which does not have a Riemannian analogue. Moreover, a nonlocal correspondent of the Ricci curvature should implicitly appear. For these reasons, both the formula and its proof need fundamental modifications.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian manifold. Let
E\subset M
be an open domain with boundary of class
C^{2}
, and let
\nu_{\partial E}
denote its outer normal vector. Let
X
be a vector field of class
C^{2}
on
M
, and define
\xi(p)=\langle X,\nu_{\partial E}\rangle_{g}(p)
on
\partial E
. Then the following hold:

•

First variation
. The first variation of the fractional perimeter of
E
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(E))

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\xi(p)\,,

(71)

where the nonlocal mean curvature
\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)
is defined as a principal value integral by

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)

\displaystyle=-\,{\rm p.v.}\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K(p,q)

\displaystyle:=-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\,.

Here
K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)
denotes the regularised (smooth) kernel

K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)=\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}H_{M}(p,q,t)e^{-\varepsilon^{2}/4t}\frac{dt}{t^{1+s/2}}\,.

(72)

Other regularisations are possible, for example one can see that

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)

\displaystyle=-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{M\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(p)}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K(p,q)

by arguing as in
[
18
, Proposition 2.5]
.

•

Second variation
. Choose a
C^{1}
extension of
\nu_{\partial E}
, the normal vector to
E
, to a vector field (still denoted by
\nu_{\partial E}
) on all of
M
. The second variation of fractional perimeter of
E
can then be expressed as

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(E))=

\displaystyle\iint_{\partial E\times\partial E}(\xi(p)-\xi(q))^{2}K(p,q)\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi(p)^{2}\,{\rm p.v.}\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)[\textnormal{div}_{q}(\nu_{\partial E}(q)K(p,q))+\textnormal{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p)K(p,q))]

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\Big[\xi^{2}{\rm div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E})+\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]-\xi{\rm div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)\,.

The integral over
M
is to be taken in the principal value sense around
p
, meaning that it is defined as the limit as
\varepsilon
tends to
0
of the same expression computed with the smooth kernel (
72
) instead. This limit is convergent due to second-order cancellations, as will be seen in the proof.

Proof.

The proof is divided into two steps.

Step 1.
We compute formally, assuming our kernel (and its derivatives) to be smooth. We will explain how to reduce to this case in Step 2, where we will consider the smooth kernel defined in (
72
).

Define
I_{t}:=\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(E))
.

We can write:

\displaystyle I_{t+r}

\displaystyle=\int_{\psi^{t}(\psi^{r}(E))}dp\,\int_{\psi^{t}(\psi^{r}(E^{c}))}\,dq\,K(p,q)=\int_{\psi^{t}(E)}dp\,\int_{\psi^{t}(E^{c})}\,dq\,K(\psi^{r}(p),\psi^{r}(q))J_{r}(p)J_{r}(q)\,.

Differentiating at
r=0
, using the divergence theorem, and using that
K(p,q)=K(q,p)
, we find

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\big|_{r=0}I_{t+r}

\displaystyle=\int_{\psi^{t}(E)}dp\,\int_{\psi^{t}(E^{c})}\,dq\,[\text{div}_{p}(K(p,q)X(p))+\text{div}_{q}(K(p,q)X(q))]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\psi^{t}(\partial E)}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{\psi^{t}(E)}-\chi_{\psi^{t}(E^{c})})(q)K(p,q)\,.

Substituting
t=0
, we get the formula for the first variation.

To compute the second variation, we want to first differentiate the expression with respect to
t
instead, and then substitute
t=0
. By the product rule, separating according to which of the two integrals gets differentiated, we can write

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\frac{d}{dr}\big|_{r=0}I_{t+r}=

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\int_{\psi^{t}(\partial E)}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K(p,q)

(73)

\displaystyle-2\int_{\partial E}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{\partial E}dq\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(q)K(p,q)\,.

To compute the first term, we proceed as follows. Using the divergence theorem, we can write

\int_{\psi^{t}(\partial E)}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K(p,q)=\int_{\psi^{t}(E)}dp\,\int_{M}dq\,\text{div}_{p}(K(p,q)X(p))(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\,.

The time dependence in the right hand side is only on the domain of integration
\psi^{t}(E)
, which allows us to compute

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\int_{\psi^{t}(\partial E)}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K(p,q)=\int_{\partial E}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{M}dq\,\text{div}_{p}(K(p,q)X(p))(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\,.

The divergence
\text{div}_{p}(K(p,q)X(p))
can be split into its tangential and perpendicular parts to
\partial E
. Applying the divergence theorem (on
\partial E
) to the tangential part, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial E}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{M}dq\,\text{div}_{p}^{\rm tan}(K(p,q)X(p))(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g},X\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{M}dq\,K(p,q)(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}^{2}(p)H[\partial E](p)\int_{M}dq\,K(p,q)(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial E}dp\,[\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]](p)\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\,.

We now focus on the perpendicular part of the divergence. Using
\nabla
to denote covariant differentiation (unless stated otherwise,
\nabla
always denotes the Riemannian gradient operator), we can compute

\displaystyle\text{div}_{p}^{\perp}(K(p,q)X(p))

\displaystyle=\langle\nu_{\partial E}(p),\nabla_{\nu_{\partial E}(p)}[K(p,q)X(p)]\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle=\langle\nu_{\partial E}(p),X(p)\rangle_{g}\langle\nu_{\partial E}(p),\nabla_{p}K(p,q)\rangle_{g}+\langle\nu_{\partial E}(p),\nabla_{\nu_{\partial E}(p)}X(p)\rangle_{g}K(p,q)

\displaystyle=\langle\nu_{\partial E}(p),\nabla_{p}K(p,q)\rangle_{g}\xi(p)+[\text{div}_{p}^{\perp}X(p)]K(p,q)\,.

Combining these expressions, we find that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\int_{\psi^{t}(\partial E)}dp\,\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle_{g}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K(p,q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)[\nu_{\partial E}(p)\cdot\nabla_{p}K(p,q)]

\displaystyle\ \ -\int_{\partial E}\Big[\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}-\xi\text{div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)\int_{M}dq\,K(p,q)(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)[\nu_{\partial E}(p)\cdot\nabla_{p}K(p,q)]

\displaystyle\ \ +\int_{\partial E}\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\Big[\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]-\xi\text{div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)\,.

Substituting everything into (
73
), we reach

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\frac{d}{dr}\big|_{r=0}I_{t+r}=

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)(\nu_{\partial E}(p)\cdot\nabla_{p}K(p,q))

\displaystyle-2\int_{\partial E}dp\,\int_{\partial E}dq\,\xi(p)\xi(q)K(p,q)-\int_{\partial E}\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\Big[\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]-\xi\text{div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)\,.

Adding and subtracting the quantity

\int_{\partial E}dp\,\int_{\partial E}dq\,(\xi^{2}(p)+\xi^{2}(q))K(p,q)=2\int_{\partial E}dp\,\int_{\partial E}dq\,\xi^{2}(p)K(p,q)\,,

we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\frac{d}{dr}\big|_{r=0}I_{t+r}=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial E}dp\,\int_{\partial E}dq\,(\xi(p)-\xi(q))^{2}K(p,q)-2\int_{\partial E}dp\,\int_{\partial E}dq\,\xi^{2}(p)K(p,q)

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\langle\nu_{\partial E}(p),\nabla_{p}K(p,q)\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\Big[\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]-\xi\text{div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)\,.

We recognise the first term as the fractional Sobolev term in the statement of the Proposition. On the other hand, we note that, were we not considering
K
to be smooth for now, the second term in the first line and the second line would both involve divergent integrals.

We will now rewrite the second term in a way which hints at the possibility of cancellations. Recall that we are considering a fixed
C^{1}
extension of
\nu_{\partial E}
to all of
M
. Using that for
q\in\partial E
we can write
K(p,q)=\langle\nu_{\partial E}(q),\nu_{\partial E}(q)K(p,q)\rangle_{g}
, and applying the divergence theorem on
E
and on
E^{c}
, we can write

\displaystyle-2\int_{\partial E}dp\,\int_{\partial E}dq\,\xi^{2}(p)K(p,q)

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{\partial E}dq\,\langle\nu_{\partial E}(q),\nu_{\partial E}(q)K(p,q)\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{E}dq\,\text{div}_{q}(\nu_{\partial E}(q)K(p,q))+\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{E^{c}}dq\,\text{div}_{q}(\nu_{\partial E}(q)K(p,q))

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E^{c}}-\chi_{E})(q)\text{div}_{q}(\nu_{\partial E}(q)K(p,q))\,.

Moreover, the second line in the second variation can be rewritten as

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\langle\nu_{\partial E}(p),\nabla_{p}K(p,q)\rangle_{g}=

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p))\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K(p,q)-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p)K(p,q))

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p))\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)+\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E^{c}}-\chi_{E})(q)\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p)K(p,q))\,.

Substituting all of this into the second variation, we finally get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\frac{d}{dr}\big|_{r=0}I_{t+r}=

\displaystyle\iint_{\partial E\times\partial E}(\xi(p)-\xi(q))^{2}K(p,q)\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi(p)^{2}\,\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)[\textnormal{div}_{q}(\nu_{\Sigma}(q)K(p,q))+\textnormal{div}_{p}(\nu_{\Sigma}(p)K(p,q))]

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\Big[\xi^{2}\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E})+\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]-\xi\text{div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)\,,

which is the formula we wanted to prove.

Step 2.
We remove the smoothness assumption on the kernel.

Let
K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)=\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}H_{M}(p,q,t)e^{-\varepsilon^{2}/4t}\frac{dt}{t^{1+s/2}}
, which is smooth, and which as
\varepsilon\to 0
increases monotonically to
K(p,q)
and converges uniformly to
K(p,q)
away from the diagonal of
M\times M
.

Fix a vector field
X
on
M
. Applying Step 1 with
K_{\varepsilon}
, we see that, defining

\textnormal{Per}_{s}^{\varepsilon}(F):=2\int_{F}\int_{F^{c}}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}\,,

and moreover
I_{t}^{\varepsilon}:=\textnormal{Per}_{s}^{\varepsilon}(\psi_{X}^{t}(E))
and
\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)=\int_{M}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)
,
the formulas

\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}I_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)\xi(p)

(74)

and

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=0}I_{t}^{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle\iint_{\partial E\times\partial E}(\xi(p)-\xi(q))^{2}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\,dp\,dq

(75)

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi(p)^{2}\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)[\textnormal{div}_{q}(\nu_{\Sigma}(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q))+\textnormal{div}_{p}(\nu_{\Sigma}(p)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q))]

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)\Big[\xi^{2}{\rm div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E})+\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]-\xi{\rm div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)

hold. We want to argue now by approximation to show that the formulas are true for
K
as well, as formulated in the statement of the present Proposition.

We start by showing that, fixed
t
, we have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{d}{dt}I_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{d}{dt}I_{t}\hskip 8.5359pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 8.5359pt\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}I_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}I_{t}\,.

(76)

By monotone convergence, since
K_{\varepsilon}\nearrow K
it is already clear that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}I_{t}^{\varepsilon}=I_{t}

for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

To conclude (
76
), it suffices to show that the
I_{t}^{\varepsilon}
, viewed as functions of
t
, are locally uniformly bounded in
C^{3}
. Indeed, then by Ascoli–Arzelà we deduce that for every sequence
\varepsilon_{i}\to 0
, there is a further subsequence
\varepsilon_{i_{j}}
such that
\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}I_{t}^{\varepsilon_{i_{j}}}
,
k\in\{1,2\}
, converge locally uniformly to some functions, but since
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}I_{t}^{\varepsilon}=I_{t}

it then easily follows that actually
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}I_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}I_{t}
as desired.

It remains then to see that
\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}I_{t}^{\varepsilon}
is locally uniformly bounded for
1\leq k\leq 3
. This follows directly from Lemma
3.12
, applied to a covering of
M
with small enough balls as given in Remark
3.8
; to be precise, Lemma
3.12
is stated for
I_{t}
instead, using the bound in Proposition
3.11
for
K
, but the same hold for
K_{\varepsilon}
instead of
K
with exactly the same proof. With this we conclude the proof of (
76
).

Now that we know that (
76
) holds, and in particular for
t=0
, we see that the LHS of (
74
) and (
75
) converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
to the desired limits. The arguments which follow will be devoted to showing that each of the terms in the RHS of (
74
) and (
75
) converge to the analogous ones with
K
instead of
K_{\varepsilon}
, and with this we will conclude the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

We start by showing that

\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)\xi(p)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\xi(p)\,,

(77)

for which it suffices to prove that

\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\ \mbox{ uniformly in }p

(78)

to some limit
\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)
.

Now, by Remark
3.6
, the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,R_{0},p,\varphi)
are satisfied at every
p\in M
, where
\varphi
denotes the exponential map on
T_{p}M
. Fix one such
p\in M
, and let
F:=\varphi^{-1}(E)
. From the definition of
\widetilde{H}_{s}
, given
\delta>0
by passing to coordinates we can write

\displaystyle-\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{F}-\chi_{F^{c}})(y)K_{\varepsilon}(0,\varphi(y))\sqrt{|g|(y)}+\int_{M\setminus\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{\delta})}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\,,

and analogously for
\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)
.

It is clear that, fixed
\delta
,

\displaystyle\int_{M\setminus\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{\delta})}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{M\setminus\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{\delta})}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\,.

Therefore, to conclude that
\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)\to\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)
it suffices to show that
\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{F}-\chi_{F^{c}})(y)K_{\varepsilon}(0,y)\sqrt{|g|(y)}
converges to zero as
\delta\to 0
, uniformly in
\varepsilon
. Thanks to having chosen normal coordinates we have
g=Id+O(|x|^{2})
, which combined with (
18
) (see also Lemma
3.10
) shows that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{F}-\chi_{F^{c}})(y)K_{\varepsilon}(0,\varphi(y))\sqrt{|g|(y)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{F}-\chi_{F^{c}})(y)K_{\varepsilon}(0,\varphi(y))+O\Big(\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,K_{\varepsilon}(0,\varphi(y))|y|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{F}-\chi_{F^{c}})(y)\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}+O\Big(\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s-1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{F}-\chi_{F^{c}})(y)\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}+O(\delta^{1-s})\,.

Up to a rotation, we can assume that
\partial F
is tangent to
\{x_{n}=0\}
at
x=0
. Since
\partial E
is a compact
C^{2}
hypersurface, there is some
r>0
(independent of
p
) such that we can write
\partial F
around
0
as the graph of a function
g:\mathcal{B}_{\delta}^{\prime}\to(-\delta,\delta)
, satisfying moreover
g(x^{\prime})=h(x^{\prime})|x^{\prime}|^{2}
for some continuous function
h
. Let
M:=\max|h|
, which is again bounded independently of
p
. Letting
H=\chi_{\{y_{n}<0\}}
we see that
F\Delta H\subset\{|y_{n}|\leq M|y^{\prime}|^{2}\}
, and likewise for
F^{c}\Delta H^{c}
. Moreover, observe that (by symmetry)

\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{H}-\chi_{H^{c}})(y)\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}=0\,.

We can then write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{F}-\chi_{F^{c}})(y)\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\big[(\chi_{F}-\chi_{F^{c}})-(\chi_{H}-\chi_{H^{c}})\big](y)\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}

\displaystyle=O\Big(\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}\cap(F\Delta H)}dy\,\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}\Big)=O\Big(\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}\cap\{|y_{n}|\leq M|y^{\prime}|^{2}\}}dy\,\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}\Big)\,.

Passing to polar coordinates it is immediate to see that the last integral is bounded by
C\int_{0}^{\delta}dr\,\frac{1}{r^{s}}=C\delta^{1-s}
. With this, as mentioned above, we conclude our proof of (
78
). In particular, we deduce (
77
).

We now focus on the terms in the RHS of (
75
). Thanks to (
78
), we immediately see that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)\Big[\xi^{2}{\rm div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E})+\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]-\xi{\rm div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\partial E}dp\,\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\Big[\xi^{2}{\rm div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E})+\langle\nabla_{p}^{\rm tan}\xi,X\rangle_{g}+\xi^{2}H[\partial E]-\xi{\rm div}_{p}^{\perp}X\Big](p)\,,

Moreover, it is clear that

\int_{\partial E}dp\,\int_{\partial E}dq\,(\xi(p)-\xi(q))^{2}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\partial E}dp\,\int_{\partial E}dq\,(\xi(p)-\xi(q))^{2}K(p,q)\,,

simply by monotone convergence.

It remains to see that the second term in (
75
) converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Recall that
\nu_{\partial E}
denotes a
C^{1}
extension to
M
of the outer normal vector to
E
. We start by applying the Leibniz (product) rule for the divergences appearing in the integral, by which we can expand:

\displaystyle\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)[\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q))+\text{div}_{q}(\nu_{\partial E}(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q))]=

(79)

\displaystyle\hskip 21.33955pt=\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p))\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)+\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\text{div}_{q}(\nu_{\partial E}(q))K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)

\displaystyle\hskip 35.56593pt+\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\langle\nu_{\partial E}(p),\nabla_{p}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\rangle_{g}+\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\langle\nu_{\partial E}(q),\nabla_{q}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\rangle_{g}\,.

Thanks to the smoothness of
\nu_{\partial E}(p)
, we can Taylor–expand
\text{div}_{q}(\nu_{\partial E}(q))=\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p))+h(p,q)
, with
h(p,q)=O({\rm dist}(p,q))
. We can then write the first two terms on the right hand side as

\displaystyle 2\text{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p))\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)+\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)h(p,q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\,.

The first integral (which corresponds to
\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}(p)
) converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
by (
78
). The second integral can be shown to converge simply by dominated convergence (without the need for cancellations), since combining Lemma
3.10
(see also Proposition
4.9
) and the fact that
h(p,q)=O({\rm dist}(p,q))
we can bound
h(p,q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)\leq\frac{C}{{\rm dist}^{n+s-1}(p,q)}
, and the latter is Lebesgue integrable in
q
.

We now focus on the last two terms on the RHS of (
79
). These are the most challenging ones, since it is not obvious at all at first sight how they can be made to converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
(observe that
|\nabla_{p}K(p,q)|
is of order
\frac{1}{{\rm dist}^{n+s+1}(p,q)}
, which is
1+s
orders away from being Lebesgue integrable), and necessarily one needs to exploit some cancellations. In fact, the two terms need to be considered together, i.e. we write them as

\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)[\nu_{\partial E}(p)\nabla_{p}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)+\nu_{\partial E}(q)\nabla_{q}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)]\,.

Let
\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}
denote the flow of the extended vector field
\nu_{\partial E}
, which is defined on all of
M
, at time
t
. Note that this is not related in any way to
\psi_{X}^{t}
, the flow of the vector field
X
that we fixed at the beginning, and which does not appear at all in the integral we are considering. The first crucial observation is that we can actually write

\displaystyle\nu_{\partial E}(p)\nabla_{p}K(p,q)+\nu_{\partial E}(q)\nabla_{q}K(p,q)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}[K(\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}(p),q)+K(p,\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}(q))]

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}K(\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}(p),\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}(q))

\displaystyle=:K^{\prime}(p,q)\,.

This new kernel
K^{\prime}(p,q)
can be bounded by
K(p,q)
, thanks to (
22
) of Proposition
3.11
. Likewise, defining
K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(p,q)=\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}K_{\varepsilon}(\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}(p),\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}(q))
, we get that it can also be bounded by
K(p,q)
: Proposition
3.11
is stated for
K
, but its proof works exactly the same for
K_{\varepsilon}
. We then need to make sense of

\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K^{\prime}(p,q)
as a singular integral, and for that, the second crucial observation is that
K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
is actually symmetric at first order:

Claim.
Denoting
K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x,y):=K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))
, we have that
|K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x,x+z)-K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x,x-z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|^{n+s-1}}
.

Proof of the claim.

Since
K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(p,q)=K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(q,p)
, we can rewrite this as
|K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x,x+z)-K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x-z,x)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|^{n+s-1}}
. Equivalently, defining the constant vector field
v=\frac{z}{|z|}
, this corresponds to
|K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x-z+|z|v,x+|z|v)-K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x-z,x)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|^{n+s-1}}
. Consider the vector field
V=v\eta
, where
\eta
is a cutoff with small support around
x
, and let
\psi_{V}^{t}
denote the flow of
V
at time
t
; then,
(x-z+|z|v,x+|z|v)=(\psi_{V}^{|z|}(x-z),\psi_{V}^{|z|}(x))
, so that our desired inequality can be rewritten as
|K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\psi_{V}^{|z|}(x-z),\psi_{V}^{|z|}(x))-K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x-z,x)|\leq\frac{C}{|z|^{n+s-1}}
. By the fundamental theorem of calculus, the LHS is

\displaystyle\Big|K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\psi_{V}^{|z|}(x-z),\psi_{V}^{|z|}(x))-K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x-z,x)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{0}^{|z|}dt\,\frac{d}{dt}K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\psi_{V}^{t}(x-z),\psi_{V}^{t}(x))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{|z|}dt\,\Big|\frac{d}{dt}K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\psi_{V}^{t}(x-z),\psi_{V}^{t}(x))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C|z|\sup_{0\leq t\leq|z|}\Big|\frac{d}{dt}K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\psi_{V}^{t}(x-z),\psi_{V}^{t}(x))\Big|\,.

Finally, arguing as in the proof of Proposition
3.11
, which corresponds to
[
18
, Proposition 3.9]
(recall that
K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(p,q)=\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}K_{\varepsilon}(\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}(p),\psi_{\nu_{\partial E}}^{t}(q))
), we see that we can bound

\sup_{0\leq t\leq|z|}\Big|\frac{d}{dt}K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\psi_{V}^{t}(x-z),\psi_{V}^{t}(x))\Big|\leq CK(x-z,x)\,,

which after applying Lemma
3.10
concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

With this claim at hand, the proof of the convergence of

\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)[\nu_{\partial E}(p)\nabla_{p}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)+\nu_{\partial E}(q)\nabla_{q}K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)]=\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(p,q)

to

\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K^{\prime}(p,q)

follows like the proof of the convergence of
\widetilde{H}_{s}^{\varepsilon}[\partial E](p)=\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{\varepsilon}(p,q)
to
\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)
. The only difference is that, in place of the full cancellation property

\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{H}-\chi_{H^{c}})(y)\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}=0\,,

one needs to estimate

\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{H}-\chi_{H^{c}})(y)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,y)

by some function of
\delta
which goes to zero as
\delta\to 0
. To see that the latter holds, using the symmetry of the Lebesgue measure and the claim, we compute

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\chi_{H}(y)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\chi_{H}(-y)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,-y)=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\chi_{H^{c}}(y)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,-y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\chi_{H^{c}}(y)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,y)+O\Big(\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,|K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,-y)-K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,y)|\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\chi_{H^{c}}(y)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,y)+O\Big(\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s-1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,\chi_{H^{c}}(y)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,y)+O\Big(\delta^{1-s}\Big)\,.

Bringing the first term on the RHS to the LHS, we see that

\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}dy\,(\chi_{H}-\chi_{H^{c}})(y)K^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(0,y)=O\Big(\delta^{1-s}\Big)

as desired.
∎

The formulas above motivate the following definitions.

Definition 5.2
.

For a set
E
as in Proposition
5.1
, as indicated there the fractional mean curvature
\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)
at a point
p\in\partial E
is defined as

\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)=-\,{\rm p.v.}\int_{M}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{s}(p,q)\,.

In particular, by arbitrariness of
\xi
in (
71
),
E
is an
s
-minimal surface (recall Definition
3.1
) iff
\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)=0
for every
p\in\partial E
.

Denoting
\sigma:=1-s
, we also set

H_{s}[\partial E](p):=\sigma\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)\,,

with which we will work in the rest of the article.
Then, up to a multiplicative dimensional constant,
H_{s}[\partial E]
converges as
s\to 1
to the classical mean curvature
H[\partial E]
, as we will see later.

Definition 5.3
.

The second variation
\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)
of an
s
-minimal surface
E
is defined as the quadratic form acting on test functions
\xi\in C^{1}(\partial E)
by

\displaystyle\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]=\iint_{\partial E\times\partial E}

\displaystyle|\xi(p)-\xi(q)|^{2}K(p,q)

(80)

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial E}\,dp\,\xi^{2}(p)\,{\rm p.v.}\int_{M}\,dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)\Big[\textnormal{div}_{p}(\nu_{\partial E}(p)K(p,q))+\textnormal{div}_{q}(\nu_{\partial E}(q)K(p,q))\Big]\,.

(81)

If
X
is a
C^{1}
vector field on
M
, set
\xi=X\cdot\nu
on
\partial E
. By Proposition
5.1
, since
\widetilde{H}_{s}[\partial E](p)=0
we then have that
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X}^{t}(E))=\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]
.

5.2
A first classical perimeter estimate for
s
-minimal surfaces with bounded index

In this section, we will prove the following classical perimeter estimate in all dimensions:

Theorem 5.4

(
Perimeter estimate
)
.

Let
p_{0}\in M
,
s_{0}\in(0,1)
,
s\in(s_{0},1)
and assume that
M
satisfies the flatness assumption
{\rm FA}_{2}(M,g,1,p_{0},\varphi)
. Let
E
be a
C^{2}

s
-minimal surface with index bounded by
m
in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
.

Then, there exists a constant
C=C(n,s_{0},m)
such that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq\frac{C}{1-s}\,.

(82)

If moreover we add the assumption that

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1}))\leq\frac{\kappa}{1-s}=\frac{\kappa}{\sigma}

(83)

for some
\kappa>0
, then we get the improved estimate

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq C\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\,.

(84)

Remark 5.5
.

The proof is similar to the one in
[
19
]
, which deals with a BV estimate for solutions of Allen–Cahn with bounded index.

Remark 5.6
.

In the case where
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1}))\leq\frac{\kappa}{\sigma}
, for
n=3
we will eventually show (see Proposition
5.29
) that
\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq C(\kappa)
, which is what one naturally expects: Recall that, at least for a fixed smooth set
E
,
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 1}(1-s){\rm Per}_{s}(E)=\gamma_{n}{\rm Per}(E)
, and a similar convergence will be obtained in the present article for sequences
E_{k}
with bounded index. Nevertheless, the non-optimal perimeter bound in Theorem
5.4
will prove very helpful in the proof of the sharp result.

Before giving the proof of Theorem
5.4
, we need some preliminary results that will be repeatedly used during the article. The first one is a lemma which deals with the finite index property in the nonlocal case. For critical points of local functionals, it is a standard fact that having Morse index bounded by
m
implies stability in one out of every
m+1
disjoint open sets. For nonlocal functionals this is not true anymore, but in one of the sets we can obtain a weaker, quantitative lower bound on the second derivative which we will refer to as
almost stability
.

Definition 5.7

(
Almost stability
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset M
open and
E\subset M
be an
s
-minimal surface in
\Omega
. Given
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we say that
E
is
\Lambda
-almost stable in
\Omega
if

\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]\geq-\Lambda\Big(\int_{\partial E\cap\Omega}|\xi|\Big)^{2}\,\quad\forall\,\xi\in C_{c}^{1}(\partial E\cap\Omega)\,.

(85)

Lemma 5.8

(
Finite Morse index and almost stability
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset M
open and let
E\subset M
be an
s
-minimal surface in
\Omega
with Morse index at most
m
. Consider a collection
\mathcal{U}_{1},\dotsc,\mathcal{U}_{m+1}
of
(m+1)
disjoint open subsets of
\Omega
, and set

\Lambda:=m\max_{i\neq j}\sup_{\mathcal{U}_{i}\times\mathcal{U}_{j}}K(p,q)\,.

Then, there is (at least) one set
\mathcal{U}_{k}
among
\mathcal{U}_{1},\dotsc,\mathcal{U}_{m+1}
such that
E
is
\Lambda
-almost stable in
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, that is

\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]\geq-\Lambda\Big(\int_{\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{k}}|\xi|\Big)^{2}\,\quad\forall\,\xi\in C_{c}^{1}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{k})\,.

Proof.

We prove the Lemma just for
m=1
for the sake of clarity, the proof goes on exactly the same for general
m
. Let
\xi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{1})
and
\xi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})
. Testing the second variation of the fractional perimeter, the expression of which we gave in Definition
5.3
, with linear combinations of
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
gives (by expanding the square in the Sobolev part of the expression)

\displaystyle\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[a\xi_{1}+b\xi_{2},a\xi_{1}+b\xi_{2}]=\,

\displaystyle a^{2}\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi_{1},\xi_{1}]+b^{2}\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi_{2},\xi_{2}]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-2ab\iint_{(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{1})\times(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})}\xi_{1}(p)\xi_{2}(q)K(p,q)\,.

Since by assumption
K(p,q)\leq\Lambda
for all
(p,q)\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\times\mathcal{U}_{2}
, the interaction term can be bounded as

\begin{split}-2ab\iint_{(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{1})\times(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})}\xi_{1}(p)\xi_{2}(q)K(p,q)&\leq 2ab\Lambda\|\xi_{1}\|_{L^{1}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{1})}\|\xi_{2}\|_{L^{1}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})}\\
&\leq a^{2}\Lambda\|\xi_{1}\|_{L^{1}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{1})}^{2}+b^{2}\Lambda\|\xi_{2}\|_{L^{1}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})}^{2}.\end{split}

Hence

\displaystyle\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[a\xi_{1}+b\xi_{2},a\xi_{1}+b\xi_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq a^{2}\Big(\underbrace{\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi_{1},\xi_{1}]+\Lambda\|\xi_{1}\|_{L^{1}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{1})}^{2}}_{=:F_{1}(\xi_{1})}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+b^{2}\Big(\underbrace{\delta^{2}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi_{2},\xi_{2}]+\Lambda\|\xi_{2}\|_{L^{1}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})}^{2}}_{=:F_{2}(\xi_{2})}\Big).

(86)

We want to show that either
F_{1}(\xi_{1})\geq 0
for all
\xi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{1})
or
F_{2}(\xi_{2})\geq 0
for all
\xi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\partial E\cap\mathcal{U}_{2})
. Suppose neither of these two held, then there would exist
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
such that
F_{1}(\xi_{1})<0
and
F_{2}(\xi_{2})<0
. This would imply, however, that (
86
) is negative for all
(a,b)\neq(0,0)
, thus contradicting that the Morse index of
u
is at most one.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

Almost-stability improves upon zooming in. More precisely, if
E
is a
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal set in
\Omega\subset M
for
(M,g)
, it is a
(r^{n+s}\Lambda)
-almost stable
s
-minimal set in
\Omega
for
(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g)
. This follows from (a) in Remark
3.8
, since it immediately gives that the
s
-perimeter of any set just rescales by a constant factor
(\frac{1}{r})^{n-s}
under the scaling of the metric, which shows that the scaling preserves the criticality/
s
-minimality condition and it transforms (as seen by rescaling both sides of equation (
85
))
\Lambda
-almost stability into
(r^{n+s}\Lambda)
-almost stability.

We first prove Theorem
5.4
in the almost-stable case.

Proposition 5.10

(
Almost-stable case
)
.

Let
p_{0}\in M
,
s_{0}\in(0,1)
,
s\in(s_{0},1)
and assume that
M
satisfies the flatness assumption
{\rm FA}_{2}(M,g,1,p_{0},\varphi)
. Let
E
be a
C^{2}

\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal surface in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
.

Then, there exists a constant
C=C(n,s_{0},\Lambda)
such that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq\frac{C}{1-s}\,.

(87)

If moreover we add the assumption that

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1}))\leq\frac{\kappa}{1-s}=\frac{\kappa}{\sigma}

(88)

for some
\kappa>0
, then we get the improved estimate

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq C\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\,.

(89)

Proof.

Step 1.
We first prove that there exists a
\Lambda_{0}>0
, depending only on
n
and
s_{0}
, such that if
E
is
\Lambda
-almost stable in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})\subset M
(see Definition
5.7
) with
\Lambda\leq\Lambda_{0}
, then the Proposition is true.

The proof goes as follows.
Let
X
be a
C^{1}
vector field, compactly supported on
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
, and define
\xi=\langle X,\nu_{\partial E}\rangle_{g}
on
\partial E
. Considering the test functions
\xi
and
|\xi|
for the second variation formula (recall Definition
5.3
), and applying the
\Lambda
-almost stability assumption to bound
\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[|\xi|,|\xi|]
from below appropriately (see Definition
5.7
, and observe that it can be extended to Lipschitz functions such as
|\xi|
by standard approximation arguments), we get

\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]-\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[|\xi|,|\xi|]\leq\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]+\Lambda\Big(\int_{\partial E}|\xi|\Big)^{2}\,.

(90)

On the other hand, both second variations in the left hand side have the same “local parts”, since obviously
\xi^{2}=|\xi|^{2}
. Also for this reason, looking at the expression in Definition
5.3
, one also sees that the difference of the terms of the form
\iint_{\partial E\times\partial E}
has a very simple form, since only the cross-terms appearing after expanding the squares do not cancel out. Precisely, we obtain that

\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]-\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[|\xi|,|\xi|]=2\iint_{\partial E\times\partial E}\Big[|\xi(p)||\xi(q)|-\xi(p)\xi(q)\Big]K(p,q)\,.

(91)

Using the identity
|a||b|-ab=2a_{+}b_{-}+2a_{-}b_{+}
, which holds for real numbers
a,b
and their positive and negative parts, together with the symmetry of
K
we find that

\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]-\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[|\xi|,|\xi|]=8\iint_{\partial E\times\partial E}\xi_{+}(p)\xi_{-}(q)K(p,q)\,.

Thanks to Proposition
3.10
, we can bound
K(p,q)\geq c>0
for
(p,q)\in\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})\times\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
. Applying this to (
91
), we then find that

c\Big(\int_{\partial E}dp\,\xi_{+}(p)\Big)\Big(\int_{\partial E}dq\,\xi_{-}(q)\Big)\leq\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]-\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[|\xi|,|\xi|]\,.

Recall now that
\xi=X\cdot\nu
, where
X
is a
C^{1}
vector field supported on
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
. By the divergence theorem, we then have that

\Big|\int_{\partial E}\xi(p)\,dp\Big|=\Big|\int_{\partial E}X\cdot\nu(p)\,dp\Big|=\Big|\int_{E}\textnormal{div}(X)\Big|\leq|B_{2}|C_{X}\,.

Therefore, putting together all the above, we can bound

\displaystyle\Big(\int_{\partial E}\Big|\xi\Big|\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{\partial E}(X\cdot\nu)_{+}+\int_{\partial E}(X\cdot\nu)_{-}\Big)^{2}=\Big(\int_{\partial E}(X\cdot\nu)_{+}-\int_{\partial E}(X\cdot\nu)_{-}\Big)^{2}+4\Big(\int_{\partial E}(X\cdot\nu)_{+}\Big)\Big(\int_{\partial E}(X\cdot\nu)_{-}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{\partial E}X\cdot\nu\Big)^{2}+4\Big(\int_{\partial E}(X\cdot\nu)_{+}\Big)\Big(\int_{\partial E}(X\cdot\nu)_{-}\Big)\leq C+C\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]+C\Lambda\Big(\int_{\partial E}|\xi|\Big)^{2}\,.

In case
\Lambda_{0}
is small enough (say, so that
C\Lambda_{0}\leq 1/2
), we can absorb the last term of the inequality on the LHS, which after taking square roots in the expression gives

\displaystyle\int_{\partial E}\Big|X\cdot\nu\Big|=\int_{\partial E}\Big|\xi\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(C+C\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]\Big)^{1/2}\,.

We now make the precise choice of
X
. We consider
X_{1}=\varphi_{*}(e_{1}\eta)
,
X_{2}=\varphi_{*}(e_{2}\eta)
, … , up to
X_{n}=\varphi_{*}(e_{n}\eta)
, where
e_{i}
is the
i
-th basis coordinate vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{1/2}}\leq\eta\leq\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{3/4}}
is a standard cutoff. We denote then
\xi_{i}=\langle X_{i},\nu_{\partial E}\rangle_{g}
. Adding up the inequalities that we obtain for these choices of
X
, and since the
e_{1},...,e_{n}
form an orthonormal basis of vectors for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, together with the flatness assumptions we obtain the perimeter in the LHS:

\displaystyle\text{Per}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\Big(C+C\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi_{i},\xi_{i}]\Big)^{1/2}=\sum_{i}\Big(C+C\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\text{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X_{i}}^{t}(E))\Big)^{1/2}\,.

On the other hand, applying Lemma
3.12
we can get rid of the second derivatives on the RHS, obtaining

\displaystyle\text{Per}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))

\displaystyle\leq\Big(C+C\text{Per}_{s}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1}))\Big)^{1/2}\,.

Under the assumption (
88
), i.e. assuming a constant bound for the fractional perimeter
\text{Per}_{s}(E)
, we conclude (
89
) as desired.

The former is the only case we will need in this article. If, on the other hand, we did not assume (
88
) and wished to obtain (
87
) instead, we would need to continue the argument as in the proof of
[
19
, Proposition 3.14]
. First, we would use the interpolation result in (
24
) and the inequality
ab\leq\frac{a^{2}}{\delta}+\delta b^{2}
, valid for any
\delta>0
, to get

\displaystyle\text{Per}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1-s)\delta}+C\delta\text{Per}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1}))\,.

Rescaling and translating this inequality, see Step 2 next for full details in a similar argument, we would find that

\displaystyle r^{1-n}\text{Per}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(x)))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1-s)\delta}+C\delta r^{1-n}\text{Per}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)))

for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{3/4}
and
r\leq 1/8
. Using then a covering lemma from
[
53
]
, see
[
19
, Lemma 3.18]
for the exact statement, we would then conclude that
\text{Per}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq\frac{C}{1-s}
as desired.

Step 2.
We obtain the Proposition in the
\Lambda
-almost stable case, for any
\Lambda
.

We need to get rid of the requirement in Step 1 that
\Lambda\leq\Lambda_{0}
; this will follow from a scaling + finite covering argument. Let
0<r<1/2
be a small radius to be chosen later depending only on
\Lambda
. Given
x_{k}\in\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
, define
\varphi_{x_{k},r}(z):=\varphi(x_{k}+rz)
, for
z\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
. By (c) in Remark
3.8
and our flatness assumptions, the rescaled manifold
(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g)
satisfies the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g,1,\varphi(x_{k}),\varphi_{x_{k},r})
. Moreover, since by assumption
E
is a
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal set in
\varphi_{x_{k},r}(\mathcal{B}_{1})
for
(M,g)
, by Remark
5.9
it is a
(r^{n+s}\Lambda)
-almost stable
s
-minimal set in
\varphi_{x_{k},r}(\mathcal{B}_{1})
for
(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g)
.

Assume for concreteness that we are assuming (
88
) and we want to prove (
89
); the proof of (
87
) is even simpler. Letting
r
small enough so that
r^{n+s}\Lambda\leq\Lambda_{0}
, the above discussion shows that
E
satisfies the almost-stability requirement in Step 1 on
\varphi_{x_{k},r}(\mathcal{B}_{1})
for
(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g)
. Moreover, applying (
33
) with
r_{1}=r
and rescaling shows that (up to changing the value of
\kappa
), the condition (
88
) also holds for
E
in the rescaled setting. Therefore, by Step 1 we find that (
89
) holds on
(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g)
. Scaling back this information, this gives that

\displaystyle\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(x_{k})))

\displaystyle\leq C(n,s_{0})\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}r^{n-1}

(92)

\displaystyle=C(n,s_{0},\Lambda)\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\,,

where the perimeter is now taken on
(M,g)
. We can now conclude by covering
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
with a finite number
C(n,\Lambda)
of balls
\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(x_{k})
as above, so that summing over
k
the perimeter bounds on each of them we conclude that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq C(n,s_{0},\Lambda)\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}

as desired.
∎

The proof of Theorem
5.4
in the finite index case will need the following covering-type lemma, which was devised in
[
19
]
to exhibit estimates in the finite Morse index case by iteratively reducing to the almost-stable case. It was inspired by the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 2.6]
. Essentially, it says that if every time we take
(m+1)
cubes which are sufficiently far from each other we are able to prove an estimate of power-type on at least one of them, then the estimate actually holds everywhere. The proof consists in subdividing a cube at scale 1 into small subcubes, adding the estimate on the small subcubes (except for a “bad” set, which will consist of at most
m
subcubes
and their close neighbours), and then iterating the procedure dyadically by subdividing the “bad” subcubes once again into smaller subcubes and starting the argument again.

Lemma 5.11

(
[
19
, Lemma 3.19]
)
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
m\geq 0
,
\theta\in(0,1)
,
D_{0}>0
and
\beta>0
. Let
\mathcal{S}:\mathfrak{B}\to[0,+\infty)
be a subadditive
3
3

3

Meaning subadditive for finite unions of (hyper)cubes.
function defined on the family
\mathfrak{B}
of the (hyper)cubes contained in
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Denote by
\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
the closed (hyper)cube of center
x
and side
r
, and assume that

(i)

\displaystyle\sup_{\{x\,:\,\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x)\in\mathfrak{B}\}}\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x))\to 0
as
r\to 0
.

(ii)

Whenever
\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x_{0}),\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x_{1}),\dotsc,\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x_{m})\subset\mathcal{Q}_{1}(0)
are
(m+1)
disjoint cubes of the same side at pairwise distance at least
D_{0}r
, then

\exists\,i\in\{0,1,\dotsc,m\}\hskip 7.0pt\textit{such that}\hskip 7.0pt\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Q}_{\theta r}(x_{i}))\leq r^{\beta}M_{0}\,.

Then

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Q}_{1/2}(0))\leq CM_{0}\,,

for some
C=C(n,\theta,m,\beta,D_{0})>0
.

With the lemma at hand, we can finally give the

Proof of Theorem
5.4
.

As in previous arguments, we assume for concreteness that we are assuming (
83
) and we want to prove (
84
); the proof of (
82
) is again even simpler.

Let
\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x_{0}),\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x_{1}),\dotsc,\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x_{m})\subset\mathcal{Q}_{1}(0)
be
(m+1)
disjoint cubes of the same side
r
and at pairwise distance at least
D_{0}r
. By Lemmas
5.8
and
3.10
, we immediately see that there exists some
\ell\in\{0,\dotsc m\}
such that the inequality

\delta^{2}{\rm Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]\geq-\frac{Cm}{(D_{0}r/2)^{n+s}}\|\xi\|^{2}_{L^{1}(\partial E\cap\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(x_{\ell})))}=:-\frac{\Lambda}{(r/2)^{n+s}}\|\xi\|^{2}_{L^{1}(\partial E\cap\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(x_{\ell})))}

(93)

holds for all
\xi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\partial E\cap\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(x_{\ell})))
and some
C=C(n,s_{0})
; we have used that
\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(x_{l})\subset\mathcal{Q}_{r}(x_{l})
. Considering the rescaled manifold
\widehat{M}:=(M,(2/r)^{2}g)
, by Remarks
3.8
and
5.9
the flatness assumption
{\rm FA}_{2}(M,(2/r)^{2}g,1,q_{\ell},\varphi_{x_{\ell},r/2})
holds and
E
is a
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal surface in
\varphi_{x_{\ell},r/2}(\mathcal{B}_{1}(x_{\ell}))\subset\widehat{M}
. Therefore, we can apply Proposition
5.10
(selecting
D_{0}
be large enough that
\Lambda=\frac{Cm}{D_{0}^{n+s}}\leq\Lambda_{0}
) and get that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi_{x_{\ell},1/2}(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq C\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\,,

which scaling back to
M
shows that

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Q}_{r/C}(x_{\ell}))\leq\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{r/4}(x_{l})))\leq C\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}r^{n-1}

for some
C=C(n,s_{0},m)
.
Defining the subadditive function
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Q}):={\rm Per}(E;\varphi(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\mathcal{Q}))
,
where
\lambda\mathcal{Q}:=\{\lambda y\,:\,y\in\mathcal{Q}\}\,,

this shows that (ii) in Lemma
5.11
is satisfied, with
\beta=n-1
,
M_{0}=C\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}
, and
\theta
and
D_{0}
depending only on
n
,
s_{0}
and
m
. Moreover, (i) in Lemma
5.11
trivially holds, since
E
is just a fixed set with finite perimeter and thus has small perimeter on small enough subsets. Hence, applying the Lemma we find that

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{Q}_{1/2}(0))={\rm Per}(E;\varphi(\frac{1}{4\sqrt{n}}\mathcal{Q}))\leq C\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\,,

which after a simple scaling+covering argument shows that

{\rm Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}))\leq C\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}

as well.
∎

5.3
Estimates for almost-stable
s
-minimal surfaces

In all of Sections
5.3
and
5.4
we will always assume that
n\geq 2
and

l:=n+2\,.

(94)

5.3.1
L^{-2}
separation estimate for almost-stable graphs

We will prove our local estimates under the assumption that

{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p_{0},\varphi)\hskip 7.0pt\mbox{is satisfied}

(95)

and that there is
\kappa<\infty
such that
E\subset M
is an
s
-minimal set in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})

with a fractional perimeter bound

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2}))\leq\frac{\kappa}{1-s}=\frac{\kappa}{\sigma}\,.

(96)

Recall that in (
95
),
\varphi:\mathcal{B}_{4}\to M
denotes a chart parametrization. We will suppose, in addition, that defining
4
4

4

The notation
\mathcal{B}_{r}^{\prime}
denotes the
(n-1)
-th dimensional Euclidean ball of radius
r
and centered at
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

\Omega_{0}:=\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-1,1)\subset\mathcal{B}_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\varphi^{-1}(\partial E)\mbox{ coincides with }\ \Gamma=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\,\Gamma_{i}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\,\{x_{n}=g_{i}(x^{\prime})\}\ \mbox{ inside }\Omega_{0},\quad{\mbox{for some }}g_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}

(97)

\displaystyle{\mbox{satisfying}}\quad g_{1}<g_{2}<\cdots<g_{N}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\|\nabla g_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime})}+\|D^{2}g_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime})}<\delta\,.

(98)

Here the number
N
of graphs is some arbitrarily large positive integer. We will shortly be able to deduce that, in a smaller cylinder contained in
\Omega_{0}
, the number of graphical layers is actually bounded by a constant depending only on the choice of constants above (but independent of
\sigma
, so that it is uniform as
s\to 1
).

We now record a simple comparison lemma for graphs verifying flatness assumptions as in our hypotheses.

Lemma 5.12

(
[
21
, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

Let
1-s=\sigma\in(0,1/2)
.
Assume that
E
is a set such that (
95
) and (
97
)–(
98
) hold. Moreover, assume that for some
i<j
we have
\min_{\overline{B_{1}^{\prime}}}(g_{j}-g_{i})\leq\widetilde{\delta}
for some
\widetilde{\delta}>0
,
and let
5
5

5

Note that, in terms of the standard Beta and Gamma functions,

c_{n,s}=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-2})}{2}{\mathrm{B}}\Bigl(\frac{n-1}{2},\frac{1+s}{2}\Bigr)=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-2})\Gamma\bigl(\frac{n-1}{2}\bigr)\Gamma\bigl(\frac{1+s}{2}\bigr)}{2\Gamma\bigl(\frac{n+s}{2}\bigr)}=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-2})}{n-1}+O(\sigma)

is universally comparable to
1
.

c_{n,s}:=\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-2})\int_{0}^{\infty}(1+t^{2})^{-\frac{n+s}{2}}\,t^{n-2}\,dt.

Then, it follows that

\big(1-c_{\delta,\widetilde{\delta}}\big)\frac{c_{n,s}\;\sigma}{|g_{j}(x^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|^{1+s}}\leq\int_{\Gamma_{j}}\frac{\sigma}{|x-y|^{n+s}}\,dy\leq\big(1+c_{\delta,\widetilde{\delta}}\big)\frac{c_{n,s}\;\sigma}{|g_{j}(x^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|^{1+s}}

for all
x\in\Gamma_{i}\cap B_{3/4}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
c_{\delta,\widetilde{\delta}}\leq C(\sqrt{\delta}+\widetilde{\delta})\downarrow 0
as
\delta,\widetilde{\delta}\downarrow 0
.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
E
be
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, and assume that (
95
)–(
98
) hold true, with
(1-s)=\sigma\in(0,1/2)
. There exists
C<\infty
, depending only on
\Lambda
,
n
, and the constants in (
95
)–(
98
), such that for every
i
such that

\min_{\overline{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2},

we have that

\sigma\int_{B^{\prime}_{1/2}}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{|g_{i+1}-g_{i}|^{1+s}}\leq C.

(99)

Proof.

Observe that
B^{\prime}_{1}\times[-1,1]\subset\mathcal{B}_{2}
.
Let
X
be a
C^{1}
vector field of the form
\varphi_{*}(e_{n}\eta)
, where
e_{n}
is the
n
–th basis coordinate vector of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(i.e. pointing ”upwards”, in the direction in which the
\Gamma_{i}
are graphical) and
\eta
is a standard cutoff verifying
\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}\times[-3/4,3/4]}\leq\eta\leq\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{2/3}^{\prime}\times[-4/5,4/5]}
. Define the function
\xi=\langle X,\nu_{\partial E}\rangle_{g}
on
\partial E
. As in (
90
)–(
91
), considering the test functions
\xi
and
|\xi|
, the
\Lambda
-almost stability assumption gives

2\iint_{\partial E\times\partial E}\Big[|\xi(p)||\xi(q)|-\xi(p)\xi(q)\Big]K(p,q)\leq\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]+\Lambda\Big(\int_{\partial E}|\xi|\Big)^{2}\,.

(100)

Now, we have defined

\xi(p)=\langle X,\nu_{\partial E}\rangle_{g}(p)=(\eta\circ\varphi^{-1})(p)\langle\varphi_{*}(e_{n}),\nu_{\partial E}\rangle_{g}(p)\,.

(101)

Recall that, by assumptions (
97
) and (
98
),
\varphi^{-1}(\partial E)
is a union of very flat graphs
\Gamma_{j}
of the form
\Gamma_{j}=\{x_{n}=g_{j}(x^{\prime})\}
. Together with the flatness assumptions on the metric, this implies that

|\langle\varphi_{*}(e_{n}),\nu_{\partial E}\rangle_{g}|\geq c_{1}|e_{n}\cdot\varphi_{*}^{-1}(\nu_{\partial E})|\geq c_{2}\,,

where
c_{2}>0
depends only on
\delta
from (
98
).
Furthermore,
\langle\varphi_{*}(e_{n}),\nu_{\partial E}\rangle_{g}
changes sign on
\varphi(\Gamma_{i})
and
\varphi(\Gamma_{i+1})
, since the consecutive layers
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Gamma_{i+1}
have normal vectors pointing in (almost) opposite directions.

These simple facts and (
101
) imply that, for
p\in\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}\times[-3/4,3/4])
and
q\in\varphi(\Gamma_{i+1}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}\times[-3/4,3/4])
, then

\Big[|\xi(p)||\xi(q)|-\xi(p)\xi(q)\Big]=2|\xi(p)||\xi(q)|\geq 2c_{2}^{2}[(\eta\circ\varphi^{-1})(p)][(\eta\circ\varphi^{-1})(q)]\geq 2c_{2}^{2}\,.

Substituting into the original inequality (
100
), we deduce that

8c_{2}^{2}\iint_{\Sigma_{i}\times\Sigma_{i+1}}K(p,q)\leq\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]+\Lambda\Big(\int_{\partial E}|\xi|\Big)^{2}\,,

where
\Sigma_{j}:=\varphi(\Gamma_{j}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}\times[-3/4,3/4])
.
So, passing to coordinates with
\varphi
, using Lemma
3.10
and Lemma
5.12
we obtain

c\sigma\int_{B^{\prime}_{1/2}}\frac{1}{|g_{i+1}(x^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|^{1+s}}dx^{\prime}\leq\sigma\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]+\Lambda\sigma\Big(\int_{\partial E}|\xi|\Big)^{2}\,.

(102)

On the other hand, the right hand side in this inequality is bounded: To estimate the first term, recalling Definition
5.3
, by Lemma
3.12
and the assumption (
96
) we can bound

\sigma\delta^{2}\text{Per}_{s}(E)[\xi,\xi]=\sigma\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\phi_{X}^{t}(E))\leq\sigma C\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E)\leq C\kappa\,.

(103)

To estimate the second term (corresponding to the error introduced by almost-stability), we apply the classical perimeter bound (
88
) given by Proposition
5.10
, since then we can bound

\sigma\Lambda\Big(\int_{\partial E}|\xi|\Big)^{2}\leq C\sigma\Lambda\textnormal{Per}^{2}(E,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{2/3}\times[-4/5,\,4/5])\leq C(\Lambda,n,s_{0})\,.

(104)

To be precise, Proposition
5.10
is stated on a ball of radius one. We can cover
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{2/3}\times[-4/5,\,4/5]
with a finite number
C_{n}
of balls
\mathcal{B}_{1/10}(x_{k})
so that
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}(x_{k})\subset\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}\times[-1,1]
for all
1\leq k\leq C_{n}
. As in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
5.10
, applying Proposition
5.10
on the manifold
(M,5^{2}g)
and scaling back we see that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\mathcal{B}_{1/5}(x_{k}))\leq C\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\,,

which summing over
k
shows that

\textnormal{Per}(E,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{2/3}\times[-4/5,\,4/5])\leq C(\Lambda,n,s_{0})\frac{1+\sqrt{\kappa}}{\sqrt{\sigma}}

thus giving (
104
).

Applying (
103
) and (
104
) to the RHS of (
102
), we conclude that

\sigma\int_{B^{\prime}_{1/2}}\frac{1}{|g_{i+1}(x^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|^{1+s}}dx^{\prime}\leq C\,,

where
C
has the right dependencies.
∎

The next lemma gives a rough lower bound for the separation between layers. Any bound which is polynomial in
\sigma
would serve our purposes here; later, we will upgrade it to the optimal rate
\sigma^{1/2}
in the case
n=3
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
E\subset M
be
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, and assume that (
95
)–(
98
) hold true, with
(1-s)=\sigma\in(0,1/2)
. There exists
C<\infty
, depending only on
\Lambda
,
n
, and the constants in (
95
)–(
98
), such that for every
i
such that

\min_{\overline{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2},

we have that

\inf_{B_{1/2}^{\prime}}(g_{i+1}-g_{i})\geq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{C}.

Proof.

Let
i
be as in the statement, and assume that there exists a point
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/2}
such that
g_{i+1}(z^{\prime})-g_{i}(z^{\prime})=\tau^{2}\sigma^{2}
for some
\tau\in(0,1)
. Our goal will be to bound
\tau
away from zero (hence we may assume without loss of generality that
\tau
is very small whenever needed).

By the assumption on
i
, we have that
|g_{i}(z^{\prime})|<3/4
. Let
z:=(z^{\prime},g_{i}(z^{\prime}))
and
\varrho:=\tau\sigma
. Define
\varphi_{z,\varrho}:=\varphi(z+\varrho\,\cdot\,)
. In the coordinates given by
\varphi_{z,\varrho}
,
\Gamma
is simply transforming into the rescaled set
\widetilde{\Gamma}:=\frac{\Gamma-z}{\varrho}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}
, in the sense that
(\varphi_{z,\varrho}^{-1}\circ\varphi)(\Gamma\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varrho}(z))=\widetilde{\Gamma}
.

By (c) in Remark
3.8
and (
95
), if
\rho
is sufficiently small the rescaled manifold
(M,\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}g)
satisfies the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}g,1,\varphi(z),\varphi_{z,\varrho})
. Moreover, by the same argument we gave in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
5.10
,
E
is a
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal set for
(M,\varrho^{2}g)
in
\varphi_{z,\varrho}(\mathcal{B}_{1})
; in fact, it is even
\varrho^{n+s}\Lambda
-almost stable. Likewise, thanks to (
33
) the fractional perimeter bound (
96
) is satisfied in this rescaled setting, up to changing the value of
\kappa
. Finally, we also observe that the “layers”
\{x_{n}=g_{j}(x^{\prime})\}

become, in the coordinates given by
\varphi_{z,\varrho}
,

\{x_{n}=\widetilde{g}_{j}(x^{\prime})\}\qquad\mbox{where }\|D^{2}\widetilde{g}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\varrho\delta,\quad\mbox{for all }j=1,\dots,N.

Therefore, we can apply Proposition
5.13
in our rescaled situation and deduce that

\sigma\int_{B_{1/2}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{|\widetilde{g}_{i+1}(x^{\prime})-\widetilde{g}_{i}(x^{\prime})|^{1+s}}\,dx^{\prime}\leq C.

On the other hand, since the point
z
is mapped to
0
, we have that

\widetilde{g}_{i}(0)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widetilde{g}_{i+1}(0)\leq\varrho.

Combining this with the bound for the
D^{2}\widetilde{g}_{j}
, a Taylor expansion immediately gives that, for
\delta,\varrho
sufficiently small,

0\leq\widetilde{g}_{i+1}-\widetilde{g}_{i}\leq 2\varrho\quad\mbox{in }B_{1}^{\prime}.

Therefore, we also get a lower bound

\frac{1}{\varrho^{1+s}}\leq C\int_{B_{1/2}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{|\widetilde{g}_{i+1}(x^{\prime})-\widetilde{g}_{i}(x^{\prime})|^{1+s}}\,dx^{\prime}\,.

Combining the above, we deduce that

\frac{\sigma}{\varrho^{1+s}}\leq C\,,

which since
\varrho=\tau\sigma
gives that
\sigma^{-s}\leq C\tau^{1+s}
. In particular, this shows
that
\tau
is bounded away from zero with a uniform bound for
\sigma\in(0,1/2)
, as desired.
∎

From this rough separation estimate, we can already deduce that the number of layers inside a smaller cylinder is bounded.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let the same assumptions as in Lemma
5.14
hold. Then, defining
N_{2}
as the number of layers of
\varphi^{-1}(\partial E)
intersecting
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}\times[-1/2,1/2]
, there exists
C<\infty
, depending only on
\Lambda
,
n
and the constants in (
95
)–(
98
), such that

N_{2}\leq C\,.

Therefore, we deduce that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}\times[-1/2,1/2]))\leq C\,.

Proof.

N_{2}
corresponds to the number of indices
i
such that

\min_{\overline{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Since for any such
i
the associated
\Gamma_{i}
is a graph with uniform estimates (by (
97
)–(
98
)), bounding the number
N_{2}
of such
i
immediately gives a bound for the total classical perimeter. We thus focus on bounding
N_{2}
, which will be possible thanks to every layer
\Gamma_{i}
“contributing a positive amount to the fractional perimeter” due to the rough separation estimate between layers.

Claim.
Let
i
be such that
\min_{\overline{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
,
and let
j\neq i
.
Then there is
c>0
small enough such that, given points
x\in\Gamma_{i}
and
y\in\Gamma_{j}
, the balls
\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)
and
\mathcal{B}_{r}(y)
are disjoint for
r=c\sigma^{4}
.

Proof of the claim.

Indeed, if
\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)\cap\mathcal{B}_{r}(y)\neq\emptyset
, then in particular
|y^{\prime}-x^{\prime}|\leq 2r
and
|g_{j}(y^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|\leq 2r
. But then, by Lemma
5.14
and (
98
),

\displaystyle 2c\sigma^{4}

\displaystyle=2r\geq|g_{j}(y^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|\geq|g_{j}(x^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|-|g_{j}(y^{\prime})-g_{j}(x^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sigma^{4}}{C}-\delta|y^{\prime}-x^{\prime}|\geq\frac{\sigma^{4}}{C}-2\delta r=\frac{\sigma^{4}}{C}-2\delta c\sigma^{4}\,,

or in other words
\frac{1}{C}\leq 2(1+\delta)c
,
which is a contradiction for
c=\frac{1}{2C(2+\delta)}
or smaller.
∎

Now that the claim has been proved, denote
F=\varphi^{-1}(E)
, and let
r
be as in the claim. Now, thanks to the uniform
C^{2}
estimates on our graphs, for
s
sufficiently close to
1
(i.e.
\sigma
close to
0
) we have the following: if
x\in\Gamma_{i}
, then both
|F\cap\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)|\geq 1/4|\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)|
and
|F^{c}\cap\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)|\geq 1/4|\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)|
hold. This is true since satisfying uniform
C^{2}
estimates means that
\Gamma_{i}
separates from its tangent hyperplane at
x
at most
Cr^{2}
inside
\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)
, so that for
\sigma
(and thus
r
) sufficiently small
\Gamma_{i}
actually divides the ball
\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)
in two pieces of almost equal volume. Denote

\textnormal{Per}_{s}|_{\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)}(F):=\iint_{(F\cap\mathcal{B}_{r}(x))\times(F^{c}\cap\mathcal{B}_{r}(x))}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s}}\,dx\,dy\,.

Then, by the relative fractional isoperimetric inequality (see
[
30
]
) together with
\min\{|F\cap\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)|,|F^{c}\cap\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)|\}\geq 1/4|\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)|
, we have that

\textnormal{Per}_{s}|_{\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)}(F)\geq\frac{c}{1-s}r^{n-s}=\frac{c}{\sigma}r^{n-s}

with
c(n)>0
. Moreover, since
\Gamma_{i}
is a graph over the
(n-1)
-dimensional ball of radius
1/2
in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, we can find at least
k\geq c/r^{n-1}
disjoint balls of radius
r
centered at points
x_{1}^{i}\in\Gamma_{i},...,x_{k}^{i}\in\Gamma_{i}
(indeed, it is clear that one can arrange at least
k\geq c/r^{n-1}
points in a square lattice inside the Euclidean ball of radius
1/2
in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
with distance
r
between adjacent points, and then it suffices to take their images under the graph parametrisation
g_{i}
). Repeating this for each
i=1,...,N_{2}
and observing that any pair of balls obtained in this way will be disjoint (by construction if they belong to the same
\Gamma_{i}
, and by the choice of
r
at the beginning of the proof if they belong to
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Gamma_{j}
with
i\neq j
), we can bound

\displaystyle\textnormal{Per}_{s}(F,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-3/4,\,3/4))

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{1\leq i\leq N_{2}}\sum_{1\leq l\leq k}\textnormal{Per}_{s}|_{\mathcal{B}_{r}(x_{l}^{i})}(F)\geq N_{2}k\frac{c}{\sigma}r^{n-s}

\displaystyle\geq N_{2}\frac{c}{\sigma}r^{1-s}=N_{2}\frac{c}{\sigma}\sigma^{-4\sigma}\geq N_{2}\frac{c}{2\sigma}\,.

In the last line we are taking
s
sufficiently close to
1
(equiv.
\sigma=1-s
sufficiently close to
0
) so that

\sigma^{-4\sigma}=1+O(\sigma|\log(\sigma)|)\geq\frac{1}{2}\,.

In other words, we have found that the number
N_{2}
of layers is bounded by

N_{2}\leq C(1-s)\textnormal{Per}_{s}(F,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-3/4,\,3/4))\,.

Moreover, by Lemma
3.10
and the flatness of the metric it is immediate that

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(F,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-3/4,\,3/4))\leq C\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E,\varphi(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-3/4,\,3/4)))\leq C\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E,\varphi(\Omega_{0}))\,.

Combining these two things, by assumption (
96
) we then deduce that
N_{2}\leq C\kappa
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We now introduce the following observation, which will be very useful since it will permit us to relate volume and boundary integrals through integration by parts while allowing us to reuse our known estimates for the family of kernels of the type
K_{s}
(defined through (
5
)):

Lemma 5.16
.

The identity

K_{s}(p,q)=-\textnormal{div}_{q}(\nabla_{q}K_{s-2})(p,q)

holds.

Proof.

By definition,
K_{s}(p,q)=\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{t}\frac{H_{t}}{t^{1+s/2}}\,dt
. We start by computing, using the identity
\Gamma(1+z)=z\Gamma(z)
:

\displaystyle K_{s-2}(p,q)=\frac{(s-2)/2}{\Gamma(1-(s-2)/2)}\int_{t}H_{t}(p,q)/t^{1+(s-2)/2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1-s/2}{\Gamma(1+[1-s/2])}\int_{t}H_{t}(p,q)/t^{s/2}=-\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{t}H_{t}(p,q)/t^{s/2}\,.

Therefore, by an application of the heat equation and integration by parts (in time), we can compute

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{q}(\nabla_{q}K_{s-2})(p,q)=\Delta_{q}K_{s-2}(p,q)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{t}\Delta_{p}H_{t}(p,q)/t^{s/2}=-\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{t}(\partial_{t}H_{t}(p,q))/t^{s/2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{t}H_{t}(p,q)/t^{1+s/2}=-K_{s}(p,q)\,,

giving the claimed result.
∎

This allows us to make the following ”generalised” definition, justified by the remark that follows it:

Definition 5.17
.

The fractional mean curvature (or nonlocal mean curvature, or NMC) of a two-sided regular hypersurface
\Sigma\subset M
with choice of normal vector
\nu_{\Sigma}
, where
\Sigma\subset M
is not assumed to be part of the boundary of a set, at
p\in\Sigma
is defined as

H_{s}[\Sigma](p):=2\sigma\int_{\Sigma}\langle\nu_{\Sigma}(q),\nabla_{q}K_{s-2}(p,q)\rangle_{g}\,dq\,.

Remark 5.18
.

Definition
5.17
is compatible with Definition
5.2
, in the sense that given a regular open domain
E
, setting
\Sigma=\partial E
and
\nu_{\Sigma}
the outer normal vector gives the same value for
H_{s}[\Sigma]
with both definitions. This is immediately seen by an application of the divergence theorem together with Lemma
5.16
.

We can now deduce the desired
L^{-2}
