bound for the separation between layers, as well as the decay in
L^{2}
of the nonlocal mean curvature of each of the individual layers as
s\to 1
. We remark once again that we are making no assumptions on the dimension
n
in this entire section.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
E\subset M
be
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, and assume that (
95
)–(
98
) hold true, with
(1-s)=\sigma\in(0,1/2)
. There exists
C<\infty
, depending only on
\Lambda
,
n
and the constants in (
95
)–(
98
), such that for every
i
such that

\min_{\overline{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2},

we have that

\sigma\int_{B^{\prime}_{1/2}}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{|g_{i+1}-g_{i}|^{2}}\leq C.

(105)

Moreover, defining
f_{i}:=H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]
, we have that

\|f_{i}\|_{L^{2}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)]))}\leq C\sqrt{\sigma}.

(106)

Proof.

Step 1.

In Proposition
5.13
we have shown that

\sigma\int_{B^{\prime}_{1/2}}\frac{dx^{\prime}}{|g_{i+1}-g_{i}|^{1+s}}\leq C.

To reach (
105
), we need to upgrade the exponent in the denominator from
(1+s)
to
2
. For this, it suffices to notice that since by Lemma
5.14
we have that
(g_{i+1}-g_{i})\in(\sigma^{4}/C,1)
, we can then estimate

|g_{j}-g_{i}|^{s}=\bigl(1+O(\sigma|\log\sigma|)\bigr)|g_{j}-g_{i}|.

(107)

Step 2.
We deduce (
106
) from (
105
).

As before, we use the notation
\Omega_{0}=\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-1,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We want to split the contributions of each of the individual layers of
E
to the total nonlocal mean curvature. For that, define the set
A_{i}=\varphi(\{x_{n}<g_{i}(x^{\prime})\}\cap\Omega_{0})
, so that
\partial A_{i}=\varphi(\Gamma_{i})\cup\varphi(\partial\Omega_{0}\cap\{x_{n}<g_{i}(x^{\prime})\})
. In other words, in coordinates
A_{i}
comprises all the space below the graph
\Gamma_{i}
, and the set
\partial A_{i}
corresponds to a portion of the layer
\Gamma_{i}
(plus some extra piece contained in
\partial\Omega_{0}
). Define also the sets
E_{i}=A_{i}\setminus A_{i-1}
, so that (up to possibly interchanging
E
with its complement) in particular it follows that
E\cap\varphi(\Omega_{0})=\bigcup_{i\in 2\mathbb{Z}}E_{i}
.

Let
i
be such that
\min_{\overline{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
, with
i
even (the odd case is essentially identical), and let
p\in\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap\{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/2}\})
. With the definitions above, it is immediate to see that we can split

\displaystyle\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})(q)K_{s}(p,q)=\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}dq\,(\chi_{A_{i}}-\chi_{A_{i}^{c}})(q)K_{s}(p,q)

\displaystyle-2\sum_{j<i,\,j-i\,{\rm odd}}\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}dq\,\chi_{E_{j}}(q)K_{s}(p,q)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j>i,\,j-i\,{\rm even}}\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}dq\,\chi_{E_{j}}(q)K_{s}(p,q)\,.

On the other hand, since
E
is an
s
-minimal surface in particular it has zero NMC at
p
, so that recalling Definition
5.2
we have that (we omit
dq
in the notation from now on)

\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})K_{s}(p,q)+\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})^{c}}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})K_{s}(p,q)=\int_{M}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})K_{s}(p,q)=0\,.

Combining both expressions, we can then write

\displaystyle-\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}(\chi_{A_{i}}-\chi_{A_{i}^{c}})K_{s}(p,q)=-\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})^{c}}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})K_{s}(p,q)

\displaystyle-2\sum_{j<i,\,j-i\,{\rm odd}}\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}\chi_{E_{j}}K_{s}(p,q)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j>i,\,j-i\,{\rm even}}\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}\chi_{E_{j}}K_{s}(p,q)\,.

Applying the divergence theorem on the left hand side using Lemma
5.16
, we then find that

\displaystyle H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})](p)=-2\sigma\sum_{j<i,j-i\,even}\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}\chi_{E_{j}}K_{s}(p,q)+2\sigma\sum_{j>i,j-i\,even}\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}\chi_{E_{j}}K_{s}(p,q)+\sigma{\rm Ext}(p)\,,

(108)

where

{\rm Ext}(p):=-\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})^{c}}(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})K_{s}(p,q)-\int_{\varphi(\partial\Omega_{0})}\langle\nu_{\partial\Omega_{0}}(q),\nabla_{q}K_{s-2}(p,q)\rangle_{g}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(q)\,.

In other words, (
108
) says that, since the NMC of
E
is zero, the “individual” NMC of
\varphi(\Gamma_{i})
in the sense of Definition
5.17
is equal to the contribution from the other layers plus an exterior error.

Now, it is clear that the
E_{j}
with
j<i
are contained in
A_{i-1}:=\varphi(\{x_{n}<g_{i-1}(x^{\prime})\}\cap\Omega_{0})
, and likewise the
E_{j}
with
j>i
are contained in
B_{i+1}:=\varphi(\{x_{n}>g_{i+1}(x^{\prime})\}\cap\Omega_{0})
, so that

\Big|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\Big|(p)\leq 2\sigma\Big|\int_{A_{i-1}}K_{s}(p,q)\Big|+2\sigma\Big|\int_{B_{i+1}}K_{s}(p,q)\Big|+\sigma\Big|{\rm Ext}\Big|(p)\,.

Using this observation, passing to coordinates with
\varphi^{-1}
(letting
x:=\varphi^{-1}(p)
, and using Lemma
3.10
and the flatness assumptions) and then integrating by parts (once again thanks to Lemma
5.16
/the divergence theorem, applied also with the Euclidean kernel
\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s}}
), we can bound

\displaystyle\Big|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\Big|(p)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma\Big|\int_{A_{i-1}}K_{s}(p,q)\Big|+2\sigma\Big|\int_{B_{i+1}}K_{s}(p,q)\Big|+\sigma\Big|{\rm Ext}\Big|(p)

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\int_{\varphi^{-1}(A_{i-1})}dy\,\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s}}+C\sigma\int_{\varphi^{-1}(B_{i+1})}dy\,\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s}}+\sigma\Big|{\rm Ext}\Big|(p)

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\int_{\Gamma_{i-1}}d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s-1}}+C\sigma\int_{\Gamma_{i+1}}d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s-1}}+\sigma{\rm Ext}_{2}(p)\,.

Here, the new exterior error term is

\displaystyle{\rm Ext}_{2}(p)

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})^{c}}dq\,(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E^{c}})K_{s}(p,q)\Big|+\Big|\int_{\varphi(\partial\Omega_{0})}\langle\nu_{\Sigma}(q),\nabla_{q}K_{s-2}(p,q)\rangle_{g}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(q)\Big|

\displaystyle\ \ +\Big|\int_{\partial\Omega_{0}\cap\{x_{n}<g_{i-1}(x^{\prime})\}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s-1}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(q)\Big|+\Big|\int_{\partial\Omega_{0}\cap\{x_{n}>g_{i+1}(x^{\prime})\}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s-1}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(q)\Big|\,.

We can bound the first term in
{\rm Ext}_{2}(p)
by a uniform constant using (
21
) (with
\alpha=0
). Moreover, by an application of (
20
), we can bound the (differentiated) kernel in the second term by a uniform constant (since
\textnormal{dist}(p,q)\geq\frac{1}{C}>0
for all
q
in
\varphi(\Omega_{0})^{c}
), and likewise for the two last terms involving
\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s-1}}
(since
|x-y|\geq\frac{1}{C}
for all
y
in
\partial\Omega_{0}
). Therefore,
{\rm Ext}_{2}(p)\leq C
.

Substituting this above and using Lemma
5.12
and (
107
), we can estimate

\displaystyle\Big|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\Big|(p)

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\int_{\Gamma_{i-1}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s-1}}+C\sigma\int_{\Gamma_{i+1}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s-1}}+\sigma{\rm Ext_{2}}(p)

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\sigma}{|g_{i}(x^{\prime})-g_{i-1}(x^{\prime})|^{s}}+C\frac{\sigma}{|g_{i+1}(x^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|^{s}}+C\sigma

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\sigma}{|g_{i}(x^{\prime})-g_{i-1}(x^{\prime})|}+C\frac{\sigma}{|g_{i+1}(x^{\prime})-g_{i}(x^{\prime})|}+C\sigma\,.

(109)

Applying the
L^{-2}
estimate in (
105
), we conclude the desired bound for the
L^{2}
norm of
H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]
.
∎

5.3.2
Uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates for almost-stable graphs in dimension
n=3

In this section we will prove that (for
n=3
) almost-stability implies a uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
bound for the layers of our surface. We remark that this is the only section of the article in which the restriction to dimension
3
is used.

Interestingly, our strategy is somewhat different from the one in
[
21
]
, and it allows us to avoid using the nonlocal mean curvature equation for graphs, which could be complicated to implement in a Riemannian setting. We show instead that, even before upgrading the regularity of our layers to a uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimate, the local and nonlocal mean curvatures of our layers are much closer to each other than their natural size. This allows us to argue using the classical minimal graph equation instead of the nonlocal one in Proposition
5.24
, the latter being the crucial step in dimension
n=3
which allows to decouple the separation estimate from the regularity estimates. Moreover, the
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity result in
[
21
]
is only obtained for
\alpha
sufficiently small, whereas our strategy allows us to obtain it for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

With an eye towards future directions, using that one can show the proximity of the nonlocal mean curvatures to the local ones before upgrading the regularity of our layers to the uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimate will probably be essential to extend our results to dimensions
4\leq n\leq 7
. Indeed, it hints at the possibility of using the Toda system (obtained in
[
21
]
under the assumption of uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
convergence as
s\to 1
to a hyperplane) prior to knowing this desired uniform regularity.

We begin the section by showing a rough
C^{2,\alpha}
bound which explodes as
\sigma\to 0
, but which surprisingly will still allow us to compare the nonlocal and local mean curvatures.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
E\subset M
be
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, and assume that (
95
)–(
98
) hold true, with
(1-s)=\sigma\in(0,1/8)
. Let
\alpha\in(0,1/4)
. Then, for every
i
such that

\min_{\overline{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2},

we have that, for every
\alpha\in(0,1/4)
, there exists a constant
C<\infty
depending only on
\Lambda
,
n
,
s_{0}
,
\alpha
and the constants in (
95
)–(
98
), and in particular independent of
s
, such that

\|g_{i}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathcal{B}_{1/4}^{\prime})}\leq C\sigma^{-12\alpha}.

(110)

Proof.

Step 1.
We first show the Hölder estimate
\|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})](\cdot)\|_{C^{0,1/2}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)]))}\leq C\sigma^{-3}
.

We start by using Lemma
5.14
in (
109
), which gives the rough pointwise estimate

\Big|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\Big|(p)\leq C\sigma^{-1}\,.

(111)

To find a higher-order bound, we differentiate both sides of equation (
108
) over
\Gamma_{i}
. Doing this, and then reproducing the computations that led to (
109
) from (
108
), we can compute

\displaystyle|\nabla_{p}^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]|(p)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma\sum_{j<i,j-i\,{\rm odd}}\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}dq\,\chi_{E_{j}}(q)|\nabla_{p}^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}K_{s}(p,q)|

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +2\sigma\sum_{j>i,j-i\,{\rm even}}\int_{\varphi(\Omega_{0})}dq\,\chi_{E_{j}}(q)|\nabla_{p}^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}K_{s}(p,q)|+\sigma|\nabla^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}{\rm Ext}_{1}|(p)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma\int_{A_{i-1}}|\nabla_{p}^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}K_{s}(p,q)|+2\sigma\int_{B_{i+1}}|\nabla_{p}^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}K_{s}(p,q)|+C\sigma

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\int_{\varphi^{-1}(A_{i-1})}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s+1}}+C\sigma\int_{\varphi^{-1}(B_{i+1})}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s+1}}+C\sigma

\displaystyle\leq C\sigma\int_{\Gamma_{i-1}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s}}+C\sigma\int_{\Gamma_{i+1}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+s}}+C\sigma

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\sigma}{|g_{i}(0)-g_{i-1}(0)|^{2}}+C\frac{\sigma}{|g_{i+1}(0)-g_{i}(0)|^{2}}+C\sigma\,.

(112)

Using Lemma
5.14
once again, we get the pointwise bound

|\nabla_{p}^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]|(p)\leq C\sigma^{-3}\,.

(113)

Combining equations (
111
) and (
113
), we find that
\|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})](\cdot)\|_{C^{0,1/2}(\Gamma_{i}\cap B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1))}\leq C\sigma^{-3}
. We could have argued by interpolation between Hölder spaces and obtained a bound of order
\sigma^{-2}
, but we do not care about the precise negative exponent at this point.

Step 2.
Schauder estimates and interpolation.

Define
A_{i}=\varphi(\{x_{n}<g_{i}(x^{\prime})\}\cap\Omega_{0})
, so that
\varphi^{-1}(\partial A_{i})\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1/2,1/2)]=\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1/2,1/2)]
is a
C^{2}
graph over
B^{\prime}_{1/2}
parametrised by
g_{i}
. By Step 1, it moreover verifies that

\|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})](\cdot)\|_{C^{0,1/2}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-3/4,\,3/4)]))}\leq C\sigma^{-3}.

Then, by the Schauder estimate for the nonlocal mean curvature equation in
[
29
]
(in its version for graphs), we deduce that
\|g_{i}\|_{C^{2,1/4}(\mathcal{B}_{1/4}^{\prime})}\leq C\sigma^{-3}
. On the other hand, by assumption (
98
) we have that
\|g_{i}\|_{C^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime})}\leq C
. By interpolation, we deduce that

\|g_{i}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathcal{B}_{1/4}^{\prime})}\leq C(\alpha)\sigma^{-12\alpha}

for every
\alpha\in(0,1/4)
, as desired.
∎

The next proposition gives a precise representation for the gradient of the singular kernel
K_{s}
up to a lower order error. The fact that we manage to get an error of two orders less will be crucial in the proof of Lemma
5.23
.

Proposition 5.21
.

Assume that the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p,\varphi)
are satisfied, where
\varphi
corresponds to the restriction of the exponential map
{\rm exp}_{p}
to
\mathcal{B}_{4}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, the difference between the gradient of
K_{s}
and the gradient of its Euclidean representative is of two orders less. More precisely, using the notation in Proposition
3.9
, there is
r=r(n)>0
such that if
y\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)
then

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}K_{s}(p,\varphi_{p}(y))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{y}\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{|y|^{n+s}}+O\Big(\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s-1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=-(n+s)\alpha_{n,s}\,\frac{y}{|y|^{n+s+2}}+O\Big(\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s-1}}\Big)\,.

Proof.

The proof is given in Appendix
D
.
∎

The next proposition simply records the standard fact that having flatness assumptions centered at some point implies that, in a smaller neighbourhood, normal coordinates centered at any other point also induce flatness assumptions. See also Remark 1.6 in
[
49
]
.

Proposition 5.22
.

Assume that the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p_{0},\varphi)
are satisfied. Then, there exists a small constant
r_{\circ}=r_{\circ}(n)>0
such that for every
x\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
and
r\in(0,r_{0})
, letting
\varphi_{q}:\mathcal{B}_{r}\to M
denote the restriction of the exponential map
exp_{q}
centered at
q=\varphi(x)
, then the flatness assumptions
FA_{l}(M,g,r,q,\varphi_{q})
are also satisfied. Moreover, the change of coordinates
F_{q}=\varphi^{-1}\circ\varphi_{q}
is a well-defined diffeomorphism onto its image and both
F_{q}
and
F_{q}^{-1}
have bounded
C^{3}
norm.

The next lemma shows that the local and nonlocal mean curvatures, of a uniformly
C^{2}
graph whose
C^{2,\alpha}
seminorm is allowed to degenerate with a small rate as
\sigma\to 0
, are nevertheless close to each other.

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
1-s=\sigma\in(0,1/2)
, and assume that
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p_{0},\varphi)\hskip 7.0pt\mbox{is satisfied}
. Let
\alpha>0
, and let
g:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/2}\to(-1/2,1/2)
be a
C^{2,\alpha}
function with

\|\nabla g\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime})}+\|D^{2}g\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime})}<\delta\quad\mbox{and}\quad[g]_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq C\sigma^{-{1/4}}\,.

Define
\Gamma
as the graph of
g
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1/2,1/2)
, so that
\varphi(\Gamma)
is a
C^{2,\alpha}
hypersurface in
M
. Then, the nonlocal mean curvature (NMC) of
\varphi(\Gamma)
is close to its classical mean curvature (or local mean curvature, LMC) with a small error. Precisely, if
p=\varphi(x)
with
x\in\Gamma\cap[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/4}\times(-1/2,1/2)]
, then

H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma)](p)=c_{\circ}H[\varphi(\Gamma)](p)+O(\sigma^{3/4})\,,

where
c_{\circ}:=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\partial\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime})}{2(n-1)}
.

Proof.

Step 1.
We begin by showing that the Riemannian NMC of the set and the Euclidean NMC of its representative in normal coordinates are close.

Let
x\in\Gamma\cap[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1/4}\times(-1/2,1/2)]
be as in the statement of the lemma. By Proposition
5.22
, we can consider normal coordinates centered at
p=\varphi(x)
and then our setting still applies. More precisely,
FA_{l}(M,g,r,p,\varphi_{p})
is satisfied for some
r
sufficiently small. Moreover,

\hat{\Gamma}:=\Big[\varphi_{p}^{-1}\circ\varphi(\Gamma)\Big]\cap\Big[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{r/2}\times(-r/2,r/2)\Big]

is still a
C^{2,\alpha}
surface, which (possibly after a rotation and making
r
sufficiently smaller) is given in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{r/2}\times(-r/2,r/2)
by the graph of some function
\hat{g}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{r/2}\to(-r/2,r/2)
with

\hat{g}(0)=|\nabla\hat{g}(0)|=0\,,

(114)

and satisfying the bounds

\|\nabla\hat{g}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{r/2}^{\prime})}+\|D^{2}\hat{g}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{r/2}^{\prime})}<C\delta\quad\mbox{and}\quad[\hat{g}]_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq C\sigma^{-{1/4}}\,.

Now, thanks to taking normal coordinates, we have that
g_{ij}(0)=\delta_{ij}
and
\nabla g_{ij}(0)=0
. This will simplify the computations that will follow. We start by writing

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma)](p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}H_{s}[\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})](p)+\sigma\int_{\varphi(\Gamma)\setminus\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})}\langle\nu_{\Sigma}(q),\nabla_{q}^{M}K_{s-2}(p,q)\rangle_{g}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}H_{s}[\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})](p)+O(\sigma)\,,

(115)

where we have used (
20
) with
s-2
instead of
s
to bound the second term. This shows that we can work with
\hat{\Gamma}
and
\varphi_{p}
instead of
\Gamma
and
\varphi
without any loss of generality.
Now, passing to coordinates we can write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}H_{s}[\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})](p)

\displaystyle=\sigma\int_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})}\langle\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})}(q^{\prime}),\nabla_{q^{\prime}}^{M}K_{s-2}(p,q^{\prime})\rangle_{g}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\langle\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})}(\varphi_{p}(y)),\nabla_{q^{\prime}}^{M}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{p}(0),\varphi_{p}(y))\rangle_{g(\varphi_{p}(y))}\sqrt{|g_{ij}^{\hat{\Gamma}}|(y)}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)\,.

(116)

A simple Riemannian computation, which we give in Appendix
C
, allows us to compute the terms in the inner product, obtaining that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}H_{s}[\varphi(\hat{\Gamma})](p)

\displaystyle=\sigma\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}[\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}(y)]^{t}g^{-1}(y)\nabla_{y}^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{p}(0),\varphi_{p}(y))\,\sqrt{|g_{ij}^{\hat{\Gamma}}|(y)}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)\,.

(117)

Here all products involving
g
denote products of matrices. Now, since
g(y)=Id+O(|y|^{2})
thanks to having taken normal coordinates, we can exchange all instances of the Riemannian metric
g
by the identity metric up to a controlled quadratic error. More precisely, we can estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}H_{s}[\varphi(\hat{\Gamma})](p)

\displaystyle=\sigma\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\big[\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}(y)\cdot\nabla_{y}^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{p}(0),\varphi_{p}(y))\big]\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)

(118)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt+O\Big(\sigma\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|y|^{2}|\nabla_{y}^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{p}(0),\varphi_{p}(y))|\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)\Big)\,,

where
\cdot
denotes the usual Euclidean scalar product.

Proposition
5.21
(applied with
s-2
instead of
s
) allows us now to compare the gradient of the kernel
K_{s-2}
with the one of the corresponding Euclidean kernel. We reach

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}H_{s}[\varphi(\hat{\Gamma})](p)

\displaystyle=-(n+s-2)\alpha_{n,(s-2)}\sigma\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\frac{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}(y)\cdot y}{|y|^{n+s}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt+O\Big(\sigma\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|y|^{2}\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s-1}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)\Big)\,.

Setting
c(n,s)=-\frac{2(n+s-2)}{s}\alpha_{n,(s-2)}
and bounding the second integral by a uniform constant, we can then write

\displaystyle H_{s}[\varphi(\hat{\Gamma})](p)

\displaystyle=c(n,s)\sigma\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\frac{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}(y)\cdot y}{|y|^{n+s}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt+O(\sigma)\,.

Now, the last expression is exactly the expression for the Euclidean nonlocal mean curvature of
\hat{\Gamma}
, plus an
O(\sigma)
error term. In other words, together with (
115
) we have found that

H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma)](p)=H_{s}[\hat{\Gamma}](0)+O(\sigma)\,,

where the NMC on the left and right hand sides are respectively on
M
and on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Step 2.
We now show that
H_{s}[\hat{\Gamma}](0)=c_{\circ}H[\hat{\Gamma}](0)+O(\sigma^{3/4})
, with
c_{\circ}:=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\partial\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime})}{2(n-1)}
. This is an exclusively Euclidean computation and which we reproduce from
[
21
]
, with the caveat that we are not assuming uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
bounds for our graphs but rather bounds which degenerate as
\sigma\to 0
.

Observe first that, from the assumption that

\hat{g}(0)=|\nabla\hat{g}(0)|=0\,,

(119)

we have that

H[\hat{\Gamma}](0)=-\Delta\hat{g}(0).

Thus our goal is to relate
H_{s}[\hat{\Gamma}](0)
with
\Delta\hat{g}(0)
.

Recall that

H_{s}[\hat{\Gamma}](0)=c(n,s)\sigma\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\frac{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}(y)\cdot y}{|y|^{n+s}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)=c(n,s)\sigma\int_{\mathcal{B}_{r/2}^{\prime}}\frac{(-\nabla\hat{g}(y^{\prime}),1)\cdot(y^{\prime},\hat{g}(y^{\prime}))}{(|y^{\prime}|^{2}+\hat{g}(y^{\prime})^{2})^{\frac{n+s}{2}}}\,dy^{\prime}.

Now, on the one hand, we have

I_{1}:=\sigma\int_{B_{\varrho}^{\prime}}\frac{(-\nabla\hat{g}(y^{\prime}),1)\cdot(y^{\prime},\hat{g}(y^{\prime}))}{(|y^{\prime}|^{2}+\hat{g}(y^{\prime})^{2})^{\frac{n+s}{2}}}\,dy^{\prime}=(1+O(\varrho^{2}))\sigma\int_{B_{\varrho}^{\prime}}\frac{\hat{g}(y^{\prime})-\nabla\hat{g}(y^{\prime})\cdot y^{\prime}}{|y^{\prime}|^{n+s}}\,dy^{\prime},

where we have used that
|y^{\prime}|^{2}+\hat{g}(y^{\prime})^{2}=|y^{\prime}|^{2}(1+\hat{g}(y^{\prime})^{2}/|y^{\prime}|^{2})=|y|^{2}(1+O(\varrho^{2}))
for
y^{\prime}\in B_{\varrho}^{\prime}
,
thanks to (
114
).

Note that our assumptions on
[\hat{g}]_{C^{2,\alpha}}
give the Taylor expansions

\big|\hat{g}(y^{\prime})-\tfrac{1}{2}y^{\prime}\cdot D^{2}\hat{g}(0)y^{\prime}\big|\leq C\sigma^{-1/4}|y^{\prime}|^{2+\alpha}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\big|\nabla\hat{g}(y^{\prime})-D^{2}\hat{g}(0)y^{\prime}\big|\leq C\sigma^{-1/4}|y^{\prime}|^{1+\alpha}.

Hence,
choosing
\varrho=\sigma
, and using that
\sigma^{\sigma}=\exp(\sigma\log\sigma)=1+O(\sigma|\log\sigma|)
as
\sigma\downarrow 0
, we obtain

\begin{split}I_{1}&=(1+O(\varrho^{2}))\sigma\left(\int_{B_{\varrho}^{\prime}}\frac{-\tfrac{1}{2}y^{\prime}\cdot D^{2}\hat{g}(0)y^{\prime}}{|y^{\prime}|^{n+s}}\,dy^{\prime}+O\Big(C\sigma^{-1/4}\int_{B_{\varrho}^{\prime}}\frac{2|y^{\prime}|^{2+\alpha}}{|y^{\prime}|^{n+s}}\,dy^{\prime}\Big)\right)\\
&=(1+O(\varrho^{2}))\left(\frac{{\rm trace}(D^{2}\hat{g}(0))}{2(n-1)}\sigma\int_{B_{\varrho}^{\prime}}\frac{|y^{\prime}|^{2}}{|y^{\prime}|^{n+s}}\,dy^{\prime}+\sigma^{3/4}O(\varrho^{\alpha+1-s})\right)\\
&=(1+O(\varrho^{2}))\left(-\Delta\hat{g}(0)\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\partial B_{1}^{\prime})}{2(n-1)}\sigma\frac{\varrho^{1-s}}{1-s}+\sigma^{3/4}O(\varrho^{\alpha+1-s})\right)\\
&=-c_{\circ}\Delta\hat{g}(0)+O(\sigma|\log\sigma|+\sigma^{3/4+\alpha}).\end{split}

On the other hand, using that
|\hat{g}(y^{\prime})|+|y^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\hat{g}(y^{\prime})|\leq\frac{1}{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2}+|y^{\prime}|^{2}
(thanks to our assumptions on
\hat{g}
),
we obtain that

I_{2}:=\sigma\int_{B_{r/2}^{\prime}\setminus B_{\varrho}^{\prime}}\frac{(-\nabla\hat{g}(y^{\prime}),1)\cdot(y^{\prime},\hat{g}(y^{\prime}))}{(|y^{\prime}|^{2}+\hat{g}(y^{\prime})^{2})^{\frac{n+s}{2}}}\,dy^{\prime}

can be bounded by

\big|I_{2}\big|\leq\sigma\int_{B_{r/2}^{\prime}\setminus B_{\varrho}^{\prime}}\frac{\frac{3}{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2}}{|y^{\prime}|^{n+s}}\,dy^{\prime}=C\sigma\int_{\varrho}^{1}r^{-s}\,dr=C(1-\sigma^{\sigma})=O(\sigma|\log\sigma|).

Since
H_{s}[\hat{\Gamma}](0)=I_{1}+I_{2}
, these considerations complete the proof of Step 2.

Step 3.
Conclusion of the proof.

Combining Steps 1 and 2, we have seen that

H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma)](p)=c_{0}H[\hat{\Gamma}](0)+O(\sigma^{3/4})\,.

On the other hand, the identity

H[\hat{\Gamma}](0)=H[\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})](p)

(120)

holds, where the left and right mean curvatures are computed on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and on
M
respectively, thanks to having taken normal coordinates centered at
p
; a proof is given in Appendix
C
. Therefore, we conclude that

H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma)](p)=c_{0}H[\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})](p)+O(\sigma^{3/4})=c_{0}H[\varphi(\Gamma)](p)+O(\sigma^{3/4})

as desired.
∎

With the results up to now, we can show the optimal separation estimate between layers in dimension
3
.

Proposition 5.24
.

Let
n=3
. Assume that (
95
)–(
98
) are satisfied, and let
E\subset M
be
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, with
(1-s)=\sigma\in(0,1/8)
. Then, there exists
c>0
, depending only on
\Lambda
,
n
and the constants in (
95
)–(
98
), such that for every
i
such that

\min_{\overline{B}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2},

we have the separation estimate

\inf_{B^{\prime}_{1/2}}(g_{i+1}-g_{i})\geq c\sigma^{1/2}>0\,.

(121)

Moreover, we have the pointwise mean curvature estimates

\|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\|_{L^{\infty}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)]))}\leq C\sqrt{\sigma}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|H[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\|_{L^{\infty}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)]))}\leq C\sqrt{\sigma}.

(122)

Proof.

Step 1.
Let us show the pointwise separation estimate in (
121
).

It is well known that the classical mean curvature can be written, for a graph in coordinates, as an operator depending on the graph and the coefficients of the metric. Moreover, the difference of the parametrising functions of two parallel graphs satisfies an elliptic equation. The precise statement is that, for
x^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}_{3/4}
and
v:=g_{i+1}-g_{i}
, we have that (see for example
[
23
, page 237]
)

\displaystyle h(x´):

\displaystyle=H[\varphi(\Gamma_{i+1})](\varphi(x^{\prime},g_{i+1}(x^{\prime})))-H[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})](\varphi(x^{\prime},g_{i}(x^{\prime})))

(123)

\displaystyle=a_{ij}(x^{\prime})v_{x_{i}x_{j}}(x")+b_{i}(x^{\prime})v_{x_{i}}+cv,

where the
a_{ij}
form a uniformly elliptic matrix and
b_{i}
and
c
are uniformly bounded.

Moreover, we know from Proposition
5.19
that the NMCs
H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]
and
H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i+1})]
are bounded in
L^{2}
by
C\sqrt{\sigma}
, which together with the combination of (
110
) (with
\alpha=\frac{1}{48}
, so that
12\alpha=1/4
) and Lemma
5.23
gives that the LMCs
H[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]
and
H[\varphi(\Gamma_{i+1})]
are bounded in
L^{2}
by
C\sqrt{\sigma}
as well. Thus (
123
) gives that

\|h\|_{L^{2}(B_{3/4}^{\prime})}\leq C\sqrt{\sigma}\,.

On the other hand, by the Harnack inequality for uniformly elliptic operators, see
[
36
, Theorems 8.17 and 8.18]
,
we have that

\sup_{B_{1/2}^{\prime}}v\leq C\inf_{B_{1/2}^{\prime}}v+C\|h\|_{L^{q}(B_{3/4}^{\prime})}

for every
q>n^{\prime}/2
, with
n^{\prime}:=n-1
. For
n=3
we may take
q=2
, thus obtaining that

\sup_{B_{1/2}^{\prime}}(g_{i+1}-g_{i})\leq C\inf_{B_{1/2}^{\prime}}(g_{i+1}-g_{i})+C\sqrt{\sigma}.

(124)

We now argue as in
[
21
, Proposition 3.5]
. Still in dimension
n=3
(and thus
n^{\prime}=2
), assume that
(g_{i+1}-g_{i})(x_{\circ}^{\prime})=\delta\sqrt{\sigma}
for some
x^{\prime}_{\circ}\in B^{\prime}_{1/2}
and let us prove a lower bound for
\delta
.

For
r\in(0,1/4)
we now dilate around
x_{\circ}=(x_{\circ}^{\prime},g_{i}(x_{\circ}^{\prime}))\in\Gamma_{i}
and
we obtain new surfaces
\Gamma_{i,r}:=\frac{1}{r}(\Gamma_{i}-x_{\circ})

which have graphical expressions
x_{n}=g_{i,r}(x^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-1,1)
, where
g_{i,r}(x^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{r}g_{i}(x_{\circ}^{\prime}+rx^{\prime})
. As we have used several times, our assumptions (
95
)–(
98
) and the
\Lambda
-almost stability condition are all preserved in this rescaled setting, considering the manifold
(M,\frac{1}{r^{2}}g)
and the new parametrisation
\varphi(rx+x_{0})
; see for example Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
5.10
.

Then, reapplying the argument above we deduce that (
124
) also holds with
g_{i}
replaced by
g_{i,r}
. Since
(g_{i+1,r}-g_{i,r})(0)=\frac{1}{r}(g_{i+1}-g_{i})(x_{\circ}^{\prime})=\frac{\delta\sqrt{\sigma}}{r}
, this gives that

\sup_{B_{r/2}^{\prime}(x_{\circ}^{\prime})}(g_{i+1}-g_{i})=r\sup_{B_{1/2}^{\prime}}(g_{i+1,r}-g_{i,r})\leq Cr\left(\frac{\delta\sqrt{\sigma}}{r}+\sqrt{\sigma}\right).

In other words, for all
r\geq\delta
we have that
\sup_{B_{r/2}^{\prime}(x_{\circ}^{\prime})}(g_{i+1}-g_{i})\leq Cr\sqrt{\sigma}
.

But then using again Proposition
5.19
and the fact that
n^{\prime}=2
, we obtain that

C\geq\int_{B_{1/4}^{\prime}(x_{\circ}^{\prime})}\frac{\sigma}{(g_{i+1}-g_{i})^{2}}\geq\frac{\sigma}{C}\int_{\delta}^{1/4}\frac{r\,dr}{(Cr\sqrt{\sigma})^{2}}=\frac{1}{C}\int_{\delta}^{1/4}\frac{dr}{r}\geq\frac{|\log\delta|}{C},

(125)

which proves that
\delta\geq c>0
.

Step 2.
Now that we know the pointwise separation estimate in (
121
), using it in equation (
109
) gives the pointwise bound for the NMC,

\|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\|_{L^{\infty}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)))}\leq C\sqrt{\sigma}.

Combining this once again with Lemma
5.23
, which we can apply thanks to (
110
) in Proposition
5.20
, we complete the proof of (
122
).
∎

We finally obtain the uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimate under the assumptions in the section.

Corollary 5.25
.

Let
n=3
. Assume that (
95
)–(
98
) are satisfied, and let
E\subset M
be
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, with
(1-s)=\sigma\in(0,1/8)
. Given
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists
C>0
, depending only on
\alpha
,
\Lambda
,
n
and the constants in (
95
)–(
98
), such that for every
i
such that

\min_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}^{\prime}}|g_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2},

we have that

\|g_{i}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime})}\leq C
.

Proof.

In (
112
) from Proposition
5.20
we found the pointwise bound

|\nabla_{p}^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})](p)|\leq C\frac{\sigma}{|g_{i}(0)-g_{i-1}(0)|^{2}}+C\frac{\sigma}{|g_{i+1}(0)-g_{i}(0)|^{2}}+C\sigma\,,

which combined with the separation estimate (
121
) shows that

|\nabla_{p}^{\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})](p)|\leq C\,.

On the other hand, by (
122
) in Proposition
5.24
we know that

\|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\|_{L^{\infty}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)]))}\leq C\sqrt{\sigma}.

Then, by interpolation between Hölder spaces, we deduce that

\|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\|_{C^{\beta}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)]))}\leq C\sigma^{(1-\beta)/2}

for all
\beta\in(0,1)
.

By the Schauder estimate for the nonlocal mean curvature equation in
[
29
]
(in its version for graphs), since the NMC
H_{s}
is an operator of order
1+s
, we deduce that
\|g_{i}\|_{C^{2,1+s+\beta-2}(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime})}\leq C(\beta)
. Given
\alpha
as in the statement, choose some
\beta>\alpha
in
(0,1)
, say
\beta=\alpha+(1-\alpha)/2
. Choosing then
s_{0}=s_{0}(\alpha)
in
(0,1)
large enough, so that
1+s_{0}+\beta-2\geq\alpha
, we conclude that

\|g_{i}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(\mathcal{B}_{1/2}^{\prime})}\leq C(\alpha)

for all
s\in[s_{0},1)
.

The proof for the range
s\in(7/8,s_{0})
instead, in which
\sigma>(1-s_{0})>0
, is essentially trivial at this point. First, by the lower separation bounds that we have found (here any rough bound, such as the one in Lemma
5.14
, suffices), there is a uniform
r_{\circ}>0
such that our surface is a single graph in balls of radius
r_{\circ}
for this range of
s
. Then, differentiating (
108
) two times and arguing exactly as in the proof of Proposition
5.20
, we get a uniform pointwise bound

\|\nabla^{2,\varphi(\Gamma_{i})}H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\|_{L^{\infty}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)]))}\leq C\,,

or for any chosen derivative of
H_{s}
for that matter. By interpolation, in particular we get a uniform bound

\|H_{s}[\varphi(\Gamma_{i})]\|_{C^{1,1/4}(\varphi(\Gamma_{i}\cap[B^{\prime}_{1/2}\times(-1,1)]))}\leq C\,,

which again by the Schauder estimate in
[
29
]
gives a bound for the
C^{3,1+s+(1+1/4)-3}=C^{3,s-3/4}
norm of
g_{i}
. Since
s\geq 7/8>3/4
, in particular we find a bound for the
C^{3}
norm of
g_{i}
, thus also a bound for its
C^{2,\alpha}
norm as desired. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
\gamma_{n}
be as in (
11
), and let
\Omega_{0}=\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-1,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Assume that (
95
)–(
98
) are satisfied, and let
E\subset M
be
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, with
(1-s)=\sigma\in(0,1/8)
. Moreover, assume that the metric
g
(in coordinates) and the Euclidean metric are
\delta
-close in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
, in the sense that

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}_{4}}\sup_{i,j}|g_{ij}(x)-\delta_{ij}(x)|\leq\delta\,.

Then, there exists a function
\varepsilon(t)
, depending only on
\Lambda
and
n
, such that
\lim_{t\to 0}\varepsilon(t)=0
and

|(1-s)\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\Omega_{0}))-\gamma_{n}\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\Omega_{0}))|\leq\varepsilon(\sigma)+\varepsilon(\delta)[\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\Omega_{0}))+(1-s)\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\Omega_{0}))]

holds.

Proof.

Let
A_{i}=\varphi(\{x_{n}<g_{i}(x^{\prime})\}\cap\Omega_{0})
,
B_{i}=\varphi(\{x_{n}>g_{i}(x^{\prime})\}\cap\Omega_{0})
, and
E_{i}=A_{i}\setminus A_{i-1}
. In particular (up to possibly interchanging
E
with its complement) it follows that
E\cap\varphi(\Omega_{0})=\bigcup_{i\in 2\mathbb{Z}}E_{i}
. We can write the fractional perimeter as the contribution of each subgraph
A_{i}
plus an error:

\displaystyle{\rm Per}_{s}(E;\Omega_{0})

\displaystyle=2\int_{E}\int_{E^{c}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}=\sum_{i\in 2\mathbb{Z}}2\int_{E_{i}}\int_{E^{c}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in 2\mathbb{Z}}2\int_{E_{i}}\int_{E_{i-1}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}+\sum_{i\in 2\mathbb{Z}}2\int_{E_{i}}\int_{E_{i+1}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}

\displaystyle\qquad+O\Big(\sum_{i\in 2\mathbb{Z}}2\int_{E_{i}}\int_{A_{i-3}\cup B_{i+2}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2\int_{E_{j}}\int_{E_{j-1}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}+O\Big(\sum_{i\in 2\mathbb{Z}}\int_{E_{i}}\int_{A_{i-3}\cup B_{i+2}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2\int_{A_{j-1}}\int_{A_{j-1}^{c}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}+O\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{E_{i}}\int_{A_{i-2}\cup B_{i+1}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}{\rm Per}_{s}(A_{j};\Omega_{0})+O\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{E_{i}}\int_{A_{i-2}\cup B_{i+1}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}{\rm Per}_{s}(A_{j};\Omega_{0})+O\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{A_{i}}\int_{B_{i+1}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}\Big)\,.

Thanks to the separation estimates,
{\rm dist}(A_{i},B_{i+1})\geq c\sqrt{\sigma}
, so that passing to coordinates in
q
and using Lemma
3.10
we can bound

\displaystyle\int_{A_{i}}\int_{B_{i+1}}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A_{i}}\int_{\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})\setminus B_{c\sqrt{\sigma}}^{c}(p)}K_{s}(p,q)dV_{p}dV_{q}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A_{i}}\int_{\{|\varphi^{-1}(p)-y|\geq c\sqrt{\sigma}\}}\frac{C}{|\varphi^{-1}(p)-y|^{n+s}}dV_{p}dy

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{|c\sqrt{\sigma}|^{s}}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\leq\frac{C}{\sigma}\varepsilon(\sigma)\,.

A more precise computation would lead to the much better bound
C\log{\sigma}
, but this suffices for our purposes since it shows that

\displaystyle(1-s){\rm Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\Omega_{0}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(1-s){\rm Per}_{s}(A_{j};\varphi(\Omega_{0}))+\varepsilon(\sigma)\,.

(126)

Moreover, we obviously have

\displaystyle{\rm Per}(E;\varphi(\Omega_{0}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}{\rm Per}(A_{j};\varphi(\Omega_{0}))\,.

(127)

Now, thanks to the assumption on the metric,

|{\rm Per}(A_{j};\varphi(\Omega_{0}))-{\rm Per}(\varphi^{-1}(A_{j});\Omega_{0})|\leq\varepsilon(\delta){\rm Per}(A_{j};\varphi(\Omega_{0}))\leq\varepsilon(\delta)\,,

(128)

and likewise

|(1-s){\rm Per}_{s}(A_{j};\varphi(\Omega_{0}))-(1-s){\rm Per}_{s}(\varphi^{-1}(A_{j});\Omega_{0})|\leq\varepsilon(\delta)\,.

(129)

On the other hand, the
\varphi^{-1}(A_{j})
are sets with uniformly
C^{2}
(graphical) boundaries and which are transversal to
\partial\Omega_{0}
in a quantified way thanks to their graphical structure. We can then apply
[
17
, Lemma 11]
separately to each of the
\varphi^{-1}(A_{j})
, obtaining that

|(1-s){\rm Per}_{s}(A_{j};\varphi(\Omega_{0}))-{\rm Per}(A_{j};\varphi(\Omega_{0}))|\leq\varepsilon(\sigma)\,.

(130)

Combining (
126
)–(
130
), we conclude the desired result.
∎

5.3.3
Uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
regularity for almost-stable surfaces in dimension
n=3

In the previous section we obtained a
C^{2}
-implies-
C^{2,\alpha}
result. We will now prove that uniform
C^{2}
(and thus also
C^{2,\alpha}
) estimates actually hold unconditionally. We start with a simple general lemma about writing sets with bounded curvatures locally as graphs.

Lemma 5.27

(
Curvature bounds imply graphicality
)
.

Assume that the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p_{0},\varphi)
are satisfied. Let
E\subset M
be an
s
-minimal surface such that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial E}|\leq C_{1}
in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
and

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2}))\leq\frac{\kappa}{1-s}=\frac{\kappa}{\sigma}\,.

(131)

Then, up to scaling the metric by a constant factor, performing a rotation in coordinates and considering a different value of
\kappa
, the hypotheses (
95
)–(
98
) are satisfied. More precisely, given
\delta>0
, there exist
C\geq 1
(depending only on
n,C_{1}
and
\delta
) and some rotation
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,Cg,4,p_{0},\psi)\hskip 7.0pt\mbox{is satisfied}

(132)

with
\psi(x)=\varphi(\frac{1}{C}\mathcal{R}x)
,

E

has fractional perimeter (as a subset of
(M,Cg)
)

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\psi(\mathcal{B}_{2}))\leq\frac{C\kappa}{1-s}=\frac{C\kappa}{\sigma}\,,

(133)

and moreover, denoting
\Omega_{0}=\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-1,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
as per usual,

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}(\partial E)\mbox{ coincides with }\ \Gamma=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\,\Gamma_{i}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\,\{x_{n}=g_{i}(x^{\prime})\}\ \mbox{ inside }\Omega_{0},\quad{\mbox{for some }}g_{i}:\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}

(134)

\displaystyle{\mbox{satisfying}}\quad g_{1}<g_{2}<\cdots<g_{N}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\|\nabla g_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime})}+\|D^{2}g_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime})}<\delta\,.

(135)

Proof.

Let
x
be the closest point in
\varphi^{-1}(\partial E)\cap\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}
to
0
, with
\varepsilon
to be selected later depending only on
n,C_{1}
and
\delta
. If there is no such point, the Proposition is trivially true with
C=\frac{4}{\varepsilon}
. Otherwise, perform a rotation
\mathcal{R}
in coordinates so that
\mathcal{R}x=(0,...,0,|x|)
. Then, defining
F=\mathcal{R}(\varphi^{-1}(E)-x)
, we have that
0\in F
and moreover the tangent space
T_{0}E^{\prime}
is oriented orthogonally to the vertical direction
e_{n}=(0,...,0,1)
, i.e.
T_{0}F=\{y\,:\,y_{n}=0\}
. Then, (the boundary of)
F
satisfies the hypotheses (
134
)–(
135
) in place of
\Gamma
, up to possibly replacing
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-1,1)
and
\delta
by
\mathcal{B}_{1/C_{2}}^{\prime}\times(-1/C_{2},1/C_{2})
and
C_{2}
in their statements for some
C_{2}=C_{2}(C_{1},n)
— this is a standard fact, which follows from arguing by Taylor expansion as in the proof of Lemma
5.12
using the second fundamental form bound (which passes to
F
) and the fact that
T_{0}F=\{y_{n}=0\}
. Then, we can choose
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{2},n,\delta)
so that, considering the rescaled set
C_{3}F
, the hypotheses (
134
)–(
135
) will be satisfied with
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-2,2)
and
\delta
instead. Since
\mathcal{R}x=(0,...,0,|x|)
, we deduce that the set
C_{3}\mathcal{R}(\varphi^{-1}(E))=C_{3}F+C_{3}\mathcal{R}x
satisfies the same hypotheses with
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-2+C_{3}|x|,2+C_{3}|x|)\subset\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-2+C_{3}\varepsilon,2)
instead as the domain, so that letting
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{C_{3}}
we have shown that
C_{3}\mathcal{R}(\varphi^{-1}(E))
satisfies (
134
)–(
135
) with
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\prime}\times(-1,1)
and
\delta
.

Defining
C=C_{3}
, so that
\psi(x)=\varphi(\frac{1}{C_{3}}\mathcal{R}^{-1}x)
, since
\psi^{-1}(\partial E)=C_{3}\mathcal{R}(\varphi^{-1}(E))
we conclude that (
134
)–(
135
) hold just as written. Moreover, as we have repeatedly used during the article, the condition (
132
) then holds thanks to (b) in Remark
3.8
, and (
133
) holds thanks to (
33
). This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 5.28
.

Let
M
be a closed manifold of dimension
3
. Let
s_{0}\in(s_{0},1)
and
s\in(0,1)
. Assume that the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p_{0},\varphi)
are satisfied, and let
E
be a
\Lambda
-almost stable
s
-minimal surface in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
. Assume, moreover, that
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\Omega_{0}))\leq\frac{\kappa}{1-s}=\frac{\kappa}{\sigma}
. Then
E
satisfies uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates and uniform separation estimates of order
\sqrt{\sigma}
in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
, in the sense that there are some positive constants
r
,
C
depending on
n,\Lambda,s_{0}
and
\kappa
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
, after a rotation
\varphi^{-1}(\partial E)\cap\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)
is a union of
C^{2,\alpha}
graphs
g_{i}
with uniform estimates
\|g_{i}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}}\leq C
and at vertical separation
\inf(g_{i+1}-g_{i})\geq\frac{1}{C}\sqrt{\sigma}
.

Proof.

The combination of Lemma
5.27
with Corollary
5.25
and Proposition
5.24
readily shows that, in order to prove the Theorem, it suffices to see that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial E}|\leq C
in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{3/2})
, where
C
depends on the constants in the statement of the Theorem.
Letting
F:=\varphi^{-1}(\partial E)
, for convenience we will actually prove that

|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial F}(x)|{\rm dist}(x,\partial\mathcal{B}_{7/4})\leq C\quad\mbox{for all }x\in\partial F\cap\mathcal{B}_{7/4},

(136)

which considering
x\in\mathcal{B}_{3/2}
implies that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial E}|\leq C
in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{3/2})
for a different choice of
C
(determined by the flatness assumptions).

To prove (
136
), assume by
contradiction that
there exist sequences
s_{k}
,
E_{k}\subset M_{k}
(satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
5.28
), with associated
F_{k}=\varphi_{k}^{-1}(\partial E_{k})
, such that

\max_{x\in\partial F_{k}\cap\varphi(B_{7/4})}|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial F_{k}}(x)|{\rm dist}(x,\partial\mathcal{B}_{7/4})=:\mathcal{M}_{k}\uparrow\infty,

and let
x_{k}
denote a sequence of points where the previous maxima are attained.

Let us consider

R_{k}:=\frac{\mathcal{M}_{k}}{{\rm dist}(x_{k},\partial\mathcal{B}_{7/4})}.

By construction,
F_{k}
satisfies
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial F_{k}}(x_{k})|=R_{k}
. Moreover, letting
r={\rm dist}(x_{k},\partial\mathcal{B}_{7/4})
, we have that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial F_{k}}|\leq 2R_{k}
in
\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(x_{k})
.

Now, by Proposition
5.22
, up to performing a change of coordinates we can (and do) assume w.l.o.g. that moreover
x_{k}=0
and
g_{ij}(0)=\delta_{ij}
, which will simplify the proof. We should also modify the quantities above accordingly by uniformly controlled multiplicative constants, but we omit this for clarity since it introduces no change in the argument.

Considering the rescaled set
\widetilde{F}_{k}=R_{k}F_{k}
, we then have that

|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}}(0)|=1
and
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}}|\leq 2
in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}/2}
. Moreover, defining
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(\cdot)=\varphi_{k}(\frac{1}{R_{k}}\cdot)
, we can write
\widetilde{F}_{k}=\widetilde{\varphi}^{-1}_{k}(E_{k})
, and then by (b) in Remark
3.8
the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M_{k},R_{k}^{2}g_{k},4R_{k},p_{k},\widetilde{\varphi}_{k})
are satisfied. Furthermore, by (
34
), up to changing the value of
\kappa
the bound (
96
) is satisfied. We recall that
\mathcal{M}_{k}\uparrow\infty
by assumption.

We divide the remainder of the proof into two steps.

Step 1
. Let us show first that necessarily
s_{k}\to 1
. Indeed, suppose (up to extracting a subsequence)
that
s_{k}\to s\in[s_{*},1)
.
Then, thanks to (uniform, since
s_{k}
is bounded away from 1) layer separation estimates —here we can even use some
rough layer separation estimate as in Lemma
5.14
— and the standard
C^{3}
regularity of fractional minimal
surfaces (e.g. arguing as in the last part of the proof of Corollary
5.25
), by Ascoli–Arzelà we would find that the
\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}

converge locally in
C^{2}
(as submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
)
to an embedded hypersurface
\partial\widetilde{F}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
of class
C^{2}
. Moreover, by Remark
5.9
the
\partial E_{k}
are
\Lambda/R_{k}^{n+s_{k}-2}
-almost stable in
(M_{k},R_{k}^{2}g_{k})
, which since
R_{k}\to\infty
gives immediately that
\partial\widetilde{F}
must be a stable
s
-minimal surface. See e.g. Step 2 of the proof of
[
14
, Proposition 6.1]
for a similar argument on how stability passes to the limit in the present case.
Hence by the Bernstein–type result for
C^{2}
stable
s
-minimal surfaces in
[
14
, Corollary 2.12]
,
\widetilde{F}
must be a half-space.
On the other hand, passing to the limit the equation
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}}(0)|=1
we obtain, thanks to the
C^{2}
convergence, that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial\widetilde{F}}(0)|=1
, which is a contradiction.

Step 2
. In light of Step 1, from now on we assume that
s_{k}\to 1
(or equivalently that
\sigma_{k}:=(1-s_{k})\to 0
).
Fix
R\geq 1
, and let
\Gamma_{k,\ell}
, with
1\leq\ell\leq N_{k}
, be the connected components
of the
C^{2}
hypersurfaces
\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2R}
.

On the one hand, since
\widetilde{F}_{k}
satisfies
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}}|\leq 2
, the combination of Lemma
5.27
and Corollary
5.25
(plus a scaling+finite covering argument as usual) shows that the
\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}
satisfy uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates in
\mathcal{B}_{R}
. Moreover, by Lemma
5.15
we deduce that the number of layers
N_{k}
of
\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}
in
B_{R}
is bounded. Therefore, up to passing to a subsequence we can assume that
N=N_{k}
is constant.

Then, by Ascoli-Arzelà, up to passing to a further subsequence (which again we do not relabel) as
k\to\infty
each of the
\{\Gamma_{k,l}\}_{l=1}^{N}
converge simultaneously as
C^{2}
submanifolds to limit surfaces
\{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{l}\}_{l=1}^{N}
. Moreover, since for a fixed
k
the
\{\Gamma_{k,l}\}_{l=1}^{N}
are disjoint, the
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{l}
can only possibly touch tangentially, but they cannot traverse each other. Now, by (
122
) in Proposition
5.24
, as
k\to\infty
the local mean curvatures of the
\widetilde{\varphi}(\Gamma_{k,l})
are converging to zero, which together with the fact that the metric
g_{ij}
in coordinates is converging to the Euclidean metric (recall that we could assume that
g_{ij}(0)=\delta_{ij}
, and combine this with (
17
) with
R=R_{k}
large) shows that the local (i.e. classical) mean curvatures of the
\Gamma_{k,l}
are converging to zero as well. Thus, the
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{l}
are classical minimal surfaces. This implies, by the maximum principle for classical minimal surfaces, that for
i\neq j
the surfaces
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{i}
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{j}
are actually either identical or disjoint.

Let the multiplicity
n_{l}
denote the number of layers that have collapsed onto the same limit
\Gamma_{l}
; it is clear then that the
\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}
converge in the varifold sense to the varifold
V=\sum_{l}n_{l}\Gamma_{l}
in
B_{R}
(perhaps after decreasing
R
slightly to ensure that the
\Gamma_{l}
are transversal to
\partial\mathcal{B}_{R}
). Moreover, from this and Lemma
5.26
, the mass
\|V\|
of this varifold satisfies

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}\|V\|_{\mathcal{B}_{R}}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}\sum_{l}n_{l}\textnormal{Per}(\Gamma_{l},\mathcal{B}_{R})

(137)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{n}\lim_{k}\textnormal{Per}(\widetilde{F}_{k},\mathcal{B}_{R})=\lim_{k}(1-s_{k})\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\widetilde{F}_{k},\mathcal{B}_{R})

\displaystyle=\lim_{k}(1-s_{k})\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E_{k},\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(\mathcal{B}_{R}))\,,

where the last fractional perimeter is computed on
(M_{k},R_{k}^{2}g_{k})
. The last equality follows, once again, from the fact that the metric coefficients are converging to the Euclidean ones in the coordinates given by
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}
, as shown by a change of variables and our estimates for the kernel
K_{s}
(see Proposition 4.3 and Lemma 4.4 in
[
19
]
for full details).

Note that, in particular, from the expression above we deduce that

\textnormal{Per}(\cup_{l}\Gamma_{l};\mathcal{B}_{R})\leq CNR^{n-1}=CR^{n-1}\,.

(138)

Claim.

V
(or equivalently, each of the
\Gamma_{l}
) is a stable classical minimal surface.

Proof of the claim.

More generally, we have the following. Given a vector field
X\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{\rho};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, define
X_{k}:=(\widetilde{\varphi}_{j})_{*}X
and extend it by zero to a vector field on
\widetilde{M}_{k}
. Fix
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
\psi_{X}^{t}
and
\psi_{X_{k}}^{t}
denote their corresponding flows at time
t
. Then,

\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}(1-s_{k})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{k}}(\psi_{X_{k}}^{t}(E_{k});\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(\mathcal{B}_{R}))\to\gamma_{n}\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}\|(\psi_{X}^{t})_{\#}V\|_{\mathcal{B}_{R}}\,\quad\mbox{as }k\to\infty\,.

(139)

The proof is as the one for (
76
), defining the single variable functions
f_{k}(t)=(1-s_{k})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{k}}(\psi_{X_{k}}^{t}(E_{k});\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(\mathcal{B}_{R}))
and
f(t)=\gamma_{n}\|(\psi_{X}^{t})_{\#}V\|_{\mathcal{B}_{R}}
, showing that
f_{k}(t)\to f(t)
as
k\to\infty
thanks to Lemma
5.26
as in (
137
), and applying Lemma
3.12
to see that the functions
\frac{d^{\ell}}{dt^{\ell}}f_{k}(t)
are locally uniformly bounded. See
[
19
, Lemma 4.4]
for full details.
∎

With the claim at hand, we can apply the curvature estimates for
C^{2}
stable minimal hypersurfaces with area bounds of type (
138
), see
[
49
]
, and deduce that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\cup_{l}\Gamma_{l}}|\leq\frac{C}{R}
in
B_{R/2}
. On the other hand, let
\Gamma_{k,0}
denote the component of
\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}
that passes through the origin (recall that
0\in\partial\widetilde{F}_{k}
), which verifies that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\Gamma_{k,0}}(0)|=1
. Thanks to the
C^{2}
convergence, we deduce that
|{\rm I\negthinspace I}_{\Gamma_{0}}(0)|=1
as well, where
\Gamma_{0}
denotes the component of
\cup_{l}\Gamma_{l}
obtained as a limit of the
\Gamma_{k,0}
. Choosing
R
large enough so that
\frac{C}{R}<1
, this clearly gives a contradiction.
∎

5.4
Convergence of s-minimal surfaces with bounded index to classical minimal surfaces

We continue to assume that
l=n+2

in this section—recall (
95
).

5.4.1
Estimates for
s
-minimal surfaces with bounded index

Up to now, we have obtained estimates in the almost-stable case. We will now focus on the finite Morse index case. The pointwise
C^{2}
estimates and pointwise classical mean curvature estimates do not necessarily hold anymore in the bounded index case, since we expect that neck-pinching may occur by analogy with classical minimal surfaces, as in the case of the rescalings of a catenoid converging to a hyperplane. On the other hand, as recorded in the next Proposition, the powerful covering argument in Lemma
5.11
will remarkably allow us to obtain a bound for the classical perimeter and a decay for the
L^{1}
-norm of the mean curvature also in this case, since these are integral quantities with a positive power-type scaling. In particular, we will use this in the next subsection to obtain the convergence, in the sense of varifolds, to a stationary limit in a very simple manner.

Proposition 5.29
.

Let
M
be a closed manifold of dimension
3
, and assume that the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p,\varphi)
are satisfied. Let
E
be an
s
-minimal surface in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, with Morse index bounded by
m
and with

\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2}))\leq\frac{\kappa}{1-s}=\frac{\kappa}{\sigma}\,.

(140)

Then, there exists
C>0
, depending only on
n
,
m
and
\kappa
, such that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1}))\leq C

(141)

and

\int_{\partial E\cap\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})}|H_{\partial E}|\leq C\sqrt{1-s}\,.

(142)

Proof.

Step 1.
We first prove (
141
) in the case where
E
is actually
\Lambda
-almost stable in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, for some fixed
\Lambda
(say
\Lambda=1
), with
C
depending on
\Lambda
.

First, observe that by Theorem
5.28
, we know a uniform bound on the second fundamental form of
\partial E
in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
. Therefore, in the rescaled setting given by Lemma
5.27
the hypotheses (
132
)–(
135
) are satisfied, which shows that Lemma
5.15
is applicable in this rescaled setting and thus (
141
) holds. Scaling back this information, what we have shown is precisely that if
E
is
\Lambda
-almost stable in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, then it satisfies that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(B_{\frac{1}{2C}}))\leq C\,,

where
C
has the right dependencies. As usual, a scaling + finite covering argument then gives that, up to changing the value of
C
, actually

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi(B_{1}))\leq C\,,

which concludes Step 1.

Step 2.
We prove (
141
) under the hypotheses of the present Proposition
5.29
.

By the same arguments above, the result in Step 1 rescales appropriately upon looking at smaller scales and centering at different points. Then, the argument using Lemma
5.11
that we employed in the proof of Theorem
5.4
(perimeter estimate in the finite index case) to deduce the latter from Proposition
5.10
(perimeter estimate in the
\Lambda
-almost stable case) applies almost word-by-word to the present situation, and thus shows (
141
) in our finite index setting.

Step 3.
We also prove (
142
), which corresponds to a bound on the
L^{1}
norm of the mean curvature, under the hypotheses of the present Proposition
5.29
.

To do so, we follow a similar strategy to Steps 1–2. First, assume that
E
is
\Lambda
-almost stable in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
for some fixed
\Lambda
, just as in Step 1. Then (
142
) is indeed true: By the combination of Theorem
5.28
and Lemma
5.27
, after rescaling the metric appropriately the hypotheses of Proposition
5.24
are satisfied. Applying (
122
), scaling back the resulting information and performing a finite covering argument, we deduce that the mean curvature
H_{\partial E}
satisfies a uniform pointwise bound of type
C\sqrt{\sigma}
in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
. Moreover, by Step 1 itself we know that
\partial E
has bounded classical perimeter in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
. Therefore, integrating
H_{\partial E}
over
\partial E\cap\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})
we see that if
E
is
\Lambda
-almost stable in
\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{2})
for some fixed
\Lambda
, then

\int_{\partial E\cap\varphi(\mathcal{B}_{1})}|H_{\partial E}|\leq C\sqrt{1-s}\,.

The finite index case follows from the
\Lambda
-almost stable case, which we have just proved, by arguing as in Step 2. Indeed, observe that the statement we have just shown can be appropriately rescaled, upon looking at smaller scales and centering at different points. More precisely, the
L^{1}
norm of the mean curvature of a hypersurface scales, upon zooming in by a factor
r
, with power
r^{n-2}
, which is a positive power of
r
for
n=3
or higher. Then, the covering argument in Step 2 of the proof of Theorem
5.4
can also be applied in our case: Indeed, Lemma
5.11
applies to subadditive quantities which satisfy a positive power-type scaling
r^{\beta}
, which includes the case of the
L^{1}
norm of the mean curvature of a hypersurface and
\beta=n-2
. This gives the desired result.
∎

With the results up to now, we can obtain our first global result.

Proposition 5.30
.

Let
E\subset M
be an
s
-minimal surface with index at most
m
and a fractional perimeter bound
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;M)\leq\frac{\kappa}{1-s}
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(M,m,\kappa)
such that

\textnormal{Per}(E;M)\leq C\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{\partial E\cap M}|H_{\partial E}|\leq C\sqrt{1-s}\,.

Proof.

Since
M
is closed, there exists a number
\delta>0
with the property that, given any
p\in M
, the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4\delta,p,\varphi_{p})
are satisfied for some
\varphi_{p}
; see Remark
3.6
. Having fixed such a
\delta>0
, by compactness we can find a finite collection of points
p_{1},...,p_{N}
such that the sets
\varphi_{p_{1}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta/32}(p_{1})),...,\varphi_{p_{N}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta/32}(p_{N}))
cover
M
. Moreover, by (
34
) in Lemma
3.17
and the assumption on
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;M)
, up to having chosen
\delta
small enough we deduce that
\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E;\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta/16}(p_{i})))\leq\frac{C}{1-s}\delta^{n-s}
for each
i
. Applying then Proposition
5.29
, appropriately rescaled (i.e. considering the manifold
(M,(\delta/32)^{-2}g)
, by our usual argument), and scaling back, we deduce that

\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta/32}(p_{i})))\leq C\delta^{n-1}

(143)

and

\int_{\partial E\cap\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta/32}(p_{i}))}|H_{\partial E}|\leq C\sqrt{1-s}\,\delta^{n-2}\,,

where
C
has the dependencies indicated in the statement of the proposition.

Adding up these inequalities for
i=1,...,N
, since the
\varphi_{p_{i}}(\mathcal{B}_{\delta/32}(p_{i}))
cover
M
we reach the conclusion of the theorem.
∎

5.4.2
Convergence to a smooth classical minimal surface – Proof of Theorem
1.4

This subsection proves, in several steps, the subsequential convergence (as
s\to 1
) of sequences of
s
-minimal surfaces with bounded Morse index and fractional perimeters to limiting smooth, classical minimal surfaces, culminating in the proof of Theorem
1.4
.

In fact, using the results in the previous section, we can readily prove the convergence to a limit stationary varifold. The remainder of the section will then focus on proving its regularity.

Theorem 5.31
.

Let
M
be a closed manifold of dimension
3
. Let
s_{i}\to 1^{-}
as
i\to\infty
, and let
E_{i}\subset M
be an associated sequence of
s_{i}
-minimal surfaces with index
\leq m
and
\limsup_{i}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)\leq C_{1}
. Then, a subsequence of the
\partial E_{i}
converges, in the varifold sense, to a limit integral stationary varifold
V
. Moreover, the
\partial E_{i}
in the subsequence converge in the Hausdorff sense to
\Sigma:={\rm supp}(V)
.

Proof.

Step 1.
Varifold convergence.

The convergence in the varifold sense of a subsequence (not relabeled) of the
\partial E_{i}
to a limit integral varifold
\Sigma
follows directly from Proposition
5.30
, by Allard’s compactness theorem (
[
1
]
). To check the stationarity, let
X
be a smooth vector field on
M
, and denote its flow at time
t
by
\psi_{X}^{t}
. By Proposition
5.30
, there is a uniform constant
C
such that we can bound

\Bigg|\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}(\psi_{X}^{t}(E_{i});M)\Bigg|=\Bigg|\int_{\partial E_{i}}X\cdot\vec{H}_{\partial E_{i}}\Bigg|\leq\|X\|_{C^{0}}\int_{\partial E_{i}}|H_{\partial E_{i}}|\leq C\sqrt{1-s_{i}}\|X\|_{C^{0}}\,.

(144)

Letting
i\to\infty
, since
\sqrt{1-s_{i}}\to 0
the convergence in the varifold sense shows that the limit varifold
V
has vanishing first variation, or in other words it is stationary.

Step 2.
Convergence in Hausdorff distance.

Let
\delta>0
be such that the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4\delta,p,\varphi_{p})
are satisfied for every
p\in M
, and such that
\varphi_{p}
corresponds to the exponential map in normal coordinates; see Remark
3.6
. Up to arguing in what follows over the rescaled manifold
(M,\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}g)
instead, we can also just assume that actually
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p,\varphi_{p})
holds.

Now, by the varifold convergence proved in Step 1, to obtain the Hausdorff convergence it suffices to prove that the
E_{i}
satisfy lower perimeter estimates, meaning that there exists a constant
c=c(M)>0
such that (for
i
large enough) if
p\in\partial{E_{i}}
then

cr_{2}^{n-1}\leq\textnormal{Per}(E;\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{B}_{r_{2}}))

for all
0<r_{2}<c
. To prove that this holds, fix
i
, and let
p\in\partial{E_{i}}
. Since the fixed set
E_{i}
has a smooth boundary, performing a blow-up of
E_{i}
around
p
we see that the corresponding sequence of rescalings converges locally (in normal coordinates, say) in
H^{s_{i}/2}
norm to a half-space in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Scaling back this information, this shows that

\liminf_{r_{1}\to 0}\frac{\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{B}_{r_{1}}))}{r_{1}^{n-s}}=c(n,s_{i})\,,

where the universal constant

c(n,s_{i}):=\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(\{x_{n}\leq 0\};\mathcal{B}_{1})\geq\frac{c(n)}{1-s_{i}}>0

comes from the contribution in
\mathcal{B}_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the fractional perimeter of a half-space. Multiplying by
(1-s_{i})
and applying Lemma
3.17
, we obtain that

c(n)\leq(1-s_{i})\liminf_{r_{1}\to 0}\frac{\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{B}_{r_{1}}))}{r_{1}^{n-s}}\leq C(1-s_{i})+C\frac{(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{B}_{r_{2}}))}{r_{2}^{n-s}}

for all
r_{2}\leq 1
. Selecting
i
large enough so that
C(1-s_{i})\leq c(n)/4
, we deduce that

cr_{2}^{n-s}\leq(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{B}_{r_{2}}))

for some
c=c(n)>0
. Combining this with the interpolation result of (
24
), we conclude that

cr_{2}^{n-1}\leq\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{B}_{r_{2}}))\,,

which is precisely what we wanted to prove.
∎

The rest of the section focuses on proving the smoothness of
\Sigma
. We begin with some preliminary results. The first one is the following lemma, which shows that the Morse index can concentrate at most at
m
many points along a subsequence.

Lemma 5.32

(
Almost stability outside of
m
points
)
.

Let
E_{i}
be as in Theorem
5.31
. Then, up to passing to a subsequence
E_{i_{j}}
, there exist points
p_{1},...,p_{l}
in
M
with
l\leq m
such that the following holds: Given any
p\neq p_{1},...,p_{l}
, there exists a radius
r_{p}>0
such that the
E_{i_{j}}
are eventually
\Lambda_{p}
-almost stable on
B_{r_{p}}(p)
for some
\Lambda_{p}<\infty
.

Proof.

Define the concentration scale by

R(p,i,C)=\sup\{r>0:\mbox{ a.s. ineq. }\eqref{lamasineq}\mbox{ holds for }E_{i}\mbox{ on }B_{r}(p)\mbox{ with constant }C\}\,.

Define also the ”nonlocal index concentration set” of the
E_{i}
(or equivalently of the associated sequence of indices
\{i\}
) as the set

IC(\{i\})=\{p\in M\ :\ \limsup_{C}\,\liminf_{i}\,R(p,i,C)\,=\,0\}.

Step 1
. We show that, up to passing to a subsequence, the nonlocal index concentration set has at most
m
points.

Indeed, assume by contradiction that every subsequence has at least
m+1
points in its concentration set. In particular, for our entire sequence
E_{i}
there exist
p_{1},...,p_{m+1}
in the nonlocal index concentration set. We focus on
p_{1}
. Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
, define
C_{j}=j
and
\varepsilon_{j}=1/j
; by definition, there exists a subsequence of indices
\{i_{j}^{1}\}_{j}
such that
R(p_{1},i_{j}^{1},C_{j})<\varepsilon_{j}
, or in other words
R(p_{1},i_{j}^{1},j)<1/j
. This implies that
q_{1}:=p_{1}
belongs to the nonlocal index concentration set of the subsequence of surfaces
E_{i_{j}}
, i.e.
q_{1}\in IC(\{i_{j}^{1}\}_{j})
.

Now, if
m>0
(otherwise the Lemma is trivial), by our contradiction assumption
E_{i_{j}^{1}}
needs to have another point
q_{2}
in its index concentration set, since it must have at least
(m+1)\geq 2
such points in total. Therefore, there exists some subsequence of the
\{i_{j}^{1}\}_{j}
, denoted by
\{i_{j}^{2}\}_{j}
, such that
R(q_{2},i_{j}^{2},j)<1/j
. Moreover, since
i_{j}^{2}\geq i_{j}^{1}
it is immediate to see that
R(q_{1},i_{j}^{2},j)<1/j
still holds.

Now, both
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are in the index concentration set
IC(\{i_{j}^{2}\}_{j})
. If
(m+1)>2
, by our contradiction assumption there must exist another point
q_{3}
which is also in the set, and we can proceed as before; iterating this argument
(m+1)
times, we find
q_{1},...,q_{m+1}
and a subsequence
\{i_{j}^{m+1}\}_{j}
as above such that
R(q_{1},i_{j}^{m+1},j)<1/j,...,R(q_{m+1},i_{j}^{m+1},j)<1/j
.

Select now
\delta
small enough so that the balls
B_{\delta}(q_{1}),...,B_{\delta}(q_{m+1})
are at a positive distance
d>0
from each other. The almost-stability inequality in Lemma
5.7
, applied with
E=E_{i_{j}^{m+1}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{k}=B_{\delta}(q_{k})
for
k=1,...,m+1
, says that
E_{i_{j}^{m+1}}
is
\Lambda
-almost stable on one of the balls, say
B_{\delta}(q_{k_{j}})
, with a constant
\Lambda<\infty
independent of
j
. Then, up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that the
E_{i_{j}^{m+1}}
are actually all
\Lambda
-almost stable on the same ball, say
B_{\delta}(q_{1})
. In other words,
\delta\leq R(q_{1},i_{j}^{m+1},\Lambda)
for all
j
. Taking
j
large enough so that
j\geq\Lambda
, we deduce that

\delta\leq R(q_{1},i_{j}^{m+1},\Lambda)\leq R(q_{1},i_{j}^{m+1},j)<1/j\,.

But then, by taking
j
large enough so that also
1/j<\delta
, we reach a contradiction. This concludes the proof of the claim in Step 1.

Step 2
. Conclusion of the proof.

Let
\{i_{j}\}_{j}
be the subsequence given by the statement of Step
1
, and let
p_{1},...,p_{l}
be all the points in the nonlocal index concentration set of the
E_{i_{j}}
, so that
l\leq m
. Consider a point
p
different from the
p_{1},...,p_{l}
, so that it is not in the nonlocal index concentration set. Then, by definition, there exist a radius
r_{p}>0
and numbers
\Lambda(p)
and
j_{0}(p)
such that, for all
j\geq j_{0}
, we have that
R(p,i_{j},\Lambda)\geq r_{p}
, or in other words the
E_{i_{j}}
are eventually
\Lambda(p)
-almost stable on
B_{r_{p}}(p)
. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The next proposition shows the regularity of
\Sigma
, the support of
V
, outside of the finitely many points where the index can concentrate.

Proposition 5.33
.

Let
V
be the limit integral stationary varifold obtained in Theorem
5.31
as a subsequential limit of the
\partial E_{i}
. Then there exist points
p_{1},...,p_{l}
in
M
, with
l\leq m
, such that
\Sigma={\rm supp}(V)
corresponds to a smooth classical minimal surface outside of
p_{1},...,p_{l}
. Moreover, up to passing to a subsequence, the
\partial E_{i}
converge as (multi-sheeted)
C^{2}
local normal graphs over any compact subset of
\Sigma\setminus\{p_{1},...,p_{l}\}
.

Proof.

Let
p_{1},...,p_{l}
be as in Lemma
5.32
, and pass to a subsequence (still labeled
E_{i}
) verifying the conclusions of both Theorem
5.31
and Lemma
5.32
. Then, given
p\neq p_{1},...,p_{l}
, for
i
large enough the
E_{i}
are
\Lambda(p)
-almost stable in
B_{r_{p}}(p)
for some
\Lambda(p)<\infty
and
r_{p}>0
. By Theorem
5.28
, which (after scaling) we can apply with some
\varphi_{p}
up to choosing a smaller value for
r_{p}
, the
E_{i}
satisfy then uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates in
B_{r_{p}}(p)
. Moreover, by Proposition
5.29
(rescaled appropriately), the
E_{i}
have perimeter bounded by
Cr_{p}^{n-1}
inside the ball.

Therefore, by Lemma
5.27
, up to making
r_{p}
even smaller the set
\varphi^{-1}(\partial E_{i})
is made of only a finite, bounded number
d_{i}
of uniformly
C^{2,\alpha}
graphs
\{\Gamma_{i,l}\}_{l=1}^{d_{i}}
. Moreover, by (
122
) the mean curvatures of the
\varphi(\Gamma_{i,l})
are going to
0
as
s\to 1
. Then, by standard Ascoli-Arzelà arguments, the
E_{i}
converge as
C^{2}
submanifolds to
\Sigma={\rm supp}(V)
in
B_{r_{p}}(p)
, which shows that
\Sigma
is a union of ordered
C^{2}
minimal hypersurfaces around
p
. Then, the maximum principle implies that these minimal hypersurfaces are actually disjoint as well. Moreover, by the usual regularity estimates for the classical minimal surface equation, we can bootstrap the
C^{2}
regularity and deduce that
\Sigma
is actually a union of ordered, disjoint smooth (
C^{\infty}
) submanifolds in
B_{r_{p}}(p)
. Since the choice of
p\neq p_{1},...,p_{l}
was arbitrary, we conclude that
\Sigma
is smooth everywhere except possibly at the points
p_{1},...,p_{l}
.

Let now
K
be a compact subset
K\subset\Sigma\setminus\{p_{1},...,p_{l}\}
. By the above, the
E_{i}
eventually satisfy
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates around any point different from
p_{1},...,p_{l}
; by compactness of
K
, covering it with finitely many balls where such
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates hold we deduce that, actually, the
E_{i}
eventually satisfy uniform
C^{2,\alpha}
estimates (depending on
K
) in a whole neighbourhood of
K
. Since the
E_{i}
are converging to
\Sigma
, we conclude that we can eventually write the
E_{i}
, locally, as normal graphs over
K
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to deduce the crucial result that the mass of our limit varifold
V
is the limit of the fractional perimeters of the
E_{i}
, which we had not addressed up to now. We more generally prove the stronger result that the variations of the varifold mass of
V
of any order are the limits of the corresponding variations of the fractional perimeters of the
E_{i}
. The proof is delicate, since it needs to account for the potentially singular convergence around the
p_{1},...,p_{l}
as well as the risk (when localising the fractional perimeter) of concentration of mass/classical perimeter close to boundaries of domains but which the fractional perimeter would not be able to capture.

Proposition 5.34
.

In the setting of Theorem
5.31
,

\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E_{i};M)=\gamma_{n}\|V\|_{M}\,,

with
\gamma_{n}
as in (
11
).

More generally, we have the following. Let
X
be a vector field of class
C^{\infty}
on
M
, and let
\psi^{t}:=\psi_{X}^{t}
denote its flow at time
t
. Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi^{t}(E_{i});M)\to\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}\|\psi_{\#}^{t}(V)\|_{M}\,\quad\mbox{as }i\to\infty\,.

(145)

Proof.

Step 1.
We prove that the fractional perimeters of the
E_{i}
converge to the varifold mass of
V
.

Let
\delta>0
, which will be fixed until the end of the proof and then made to go to
0
. Let
\Sigma
be as in Proposition
5.33
, so that the
E_{i}
converge as uniform
C^{2}
normal graphs over
K:=\Sigma\setminus\{B_{\delta}(p_{1})\cup...\cup B_{\delta}(p_{l})\}
. Define the compact manifold with boundary
N:=M\setminus\{B_{4\delta}(p_{1})\cup...\cup B_{4\delta}(p_{l})\}
; by slightly increasing the value of
\delta
if necessary, we can moreover assume that
\Sigma
intersects
\partial N
transversely and only in its smooth part.
Let now
\{\mathcal{C}_{j}\}
be a decomposition of
N
as in Lemma
4.8
, with associated
(1+\delta)
-biLipschitz equivalences
F_{j}:\mathcal{C}_{j}\to C_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
which are given by the restriction of geodesic normal coordinate maps
\widetilde{F}_{j}:B_{r_{j}}\to\mathcal{B}_{r_{j}}
to the
C_{j}
. By the proof of Lemma
4.8
and the assumption that
\Sigma
is transverse to
\partial N
, it is simple to see that we can moreover require that
\Sigma
intersect each of the
\mathcal{C}_{j}
transversely and only in the smooth part of
\mathcal{C}_{j}
.

It is clear then that, since the
E_{i}
are converging (with multiplicity) as
C^{2}
submanifolds to
\Sigma
in
N
, for all
i
large enough the same transversality properties are also true for the
E_{i}
, and in a quantifiable way. We record in a claim the precise property that we will need, and which follows directly from this discussion. We will use the notation
\mathcal{N}_{r}F
to denote the set (neighborhood) of points at distance at most
r
from a set
F
.

Claim.
There exists a constant
C
such that, for all
i
large enough and
r
small enough,
\partial E_{i}\cap\bigcup_{j}\mathcal{N}_{3r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}
can be covered with
Cr^{2-n}
(or less) balls
\{B_{r/2}(q_{k})\}_{k}
of radius
r/2
.

This is to be read as “since
\Sigma
is smooth and quantitatively transversal to the
\mathcal{C}_{j}
, it accumulates area close to the boundaries of the
\mathcal{C}_{j}
comparably to how a hyperplane passing through the center of a unit ball does so relative to the boundary of said ball. Since the
\partial E_{i}
converge in
C^{2}
to
\Sigma
in
K
, the same is true of the
\partial E_{i}
”. In particular, if
\{B_{r/2}(q_{k})\}_{k}
is a collection of balls of radius
r
as in the claim and
r
is small enough, by the classical perimeter bound in (
141
) for
i
large enough we can bound

\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})\leq\sum_{k}\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};B_{r/2}(q_{k}))\leq Cr^{2-n}r^{n-1}=Cr\,,

which goes to
0
as
r\to 0
and thus shows that the
E_{i}
do not accumulate perimeter close to the boundary of the
\mathcal{C}_{j}
. This simple technical assumption, which is satisfied by our
E_{i}
, is essential to guarantee that the fractional and classical perimeters of our objects inside the
\mathcal{C}_{j}
are comparable.

We are now ready to begin the proof of Step 1. We will omit the constant
\gamma_{n}
in what follows, and every instance of a fractional perimeter should be understood as incorporating this multiplicative constant in front.

Observe that, by transversality, we can split

\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M)=\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M\setminus N)+\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};N)=\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M\setminus N)+\sum_{j}\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})\,.

An iterated application of the triangle inequality then leads to the bound

\displaystyle\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M)-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)\Big|=

(146)

\displaystyle=\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M\setminus N)+\sum_{j}\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M\setminus N)-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M\setminus N)\Big|+\sum_{j}\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})\Big|

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\Big|(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)-[(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M\setminus N)+\sum_{j}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})]\Big|\,.

Since
M\setminus N\subset\cup B_{4\delta}(p_{i})
, we immediately see that we can bound the first term as

\displaystyle\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M\setminus N)-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M\setminus N)\Big|\leq C(\delta^{n-1}+\delta^{n-s})+\varepsilon(\sigma)\leq C\delta^{n-1}+\varepsilon(\sigma)\,,

(147)

simply by applying (
141
) and (
34
) on each of the balls
B_{4\delta}(p_{i})
and bounding the remaining nonlocal interactions between different balls by
\varepsilon(\sigma)
thanks to disjointness (up to choosing
\delta
small enough) of the
B_{4\delta}(p_{i})
.

The second term is the main one. To obtain a bound for
\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})\Big|
, we can argue as in Proposition
5.26
, using
F_{j}^{-1}:C_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{C}_{j}
as a chart parametrisation and the fact that
F_{j}
is a
(1+\delta)
-biLipschitz equivalence. The only difference is that
C_{j}
is not a cylinder, so that the
F_{j}(E_{i})
cannot be exactly written as a union of subgraphs inside
C_{j}
; on the other hand, the
F_{j}
are the restriction of geodesic normal coordinate maps
\widetilde{F}_{j}:B_{r_{j}}\to\mathcal{B}_{r_{j}}
to the
C_{j}
, so that (up to choosing
r_{j}
small enough) the
\widetilde{F}_{j}(E_{i})
are indeed a union of subgraphs
A_{k}
inside
\mathcal{B}_{r_{j}}
. We can then decompose
F_{j}(E_{i})=\cup_{k}(A_{k}\cap C_{j})
, where (as argued at the beginning) the
A_{k}\cap C_{j}
are quantitatively transversal the
\partial C_{j}
. This allows us to apply
[
17
, Lemma 11]
to each of the
A_{k}\cap C_{j}
, exactly as in the proof of Proposition
5.26
. Anyhow, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon(\delta)[\sum_{j}\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})+\sum_{j}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})]+\varepsilon(\sigma)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon(\delta)+\varepsilon(\sigma)\,,

(148)

where we have bounded the sum of the classical and fractional perimeters inside the
\mathcal{C}_{j}
by the total ones in
M
(which are uniformly bounded thanks to our assumptions and Proposition
5.30
).

We are left with bounding the third term in the RHS of (
146
), i.e. seeing that
(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)
is very close to the sum of fractional perimeters in
M\setminus N
and in the
\mathcal{C}_{j}
. It is here where we need the quantitative transversality property stated above to get rid of the interactions. Let
I(A,B):=\iint_{E_{i}\cap A\times E_{i}^{c}\cap B}K(p,q)
. Then, we can compute

\displaystyle\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)

\displaystyle=\iint_{E_{i}\cap M\times E_{i}^{c}\cap M}K(p,q)=I(M,M)

\displaystyle=I(M\setminus N,M\setminus N)+I(M\setminus N,N)+\sum_{j}I(\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{C}_{j})+\sum_{j}I(C_{j},M\setminus\mathcal{C}_{j})

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M\setminus N)+\sum_{j}\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})+I(M\setminus N,N)+\sum_{j}I(C_{j},M\setminus\mathcal{C}_{j})\,.

We can then bound

\displaystyle\Big|\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)-[\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M\setminus N)+\sum_{j}\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})]\Big|=

\displaystyle=I(M\setminus N,N)+\sum_{j}I(C_{j},M\setminus\mathcal{C}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq I(M\setminus N,N)+\sum_{j}\Big[I(\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})+I(\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j},M\setminus C_{j})+I(\mathcal{C}_{j},(M\setminus\mathcal{C}_{j})\setminus\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})\Big]\,.

(149)

Since
M\setminus N
is formed of
l
balls of radius
\delta
, we can bound

(1-s_{i})I(M\setminus N,N)\leq(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(M\setminus N;M)\leq lC\delta^{n-s}\,.

Moreover, since
\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}
and
M\setminus\mathcal{C}_{j}
are at a positive distance
r
from each other, we have that

(1-s_{i})I(\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j},M\setminus C_{j})\leq(1-s_{i})\sum_{j}\iint_{(\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})\times(M\setminus\mathcal{C}_{j})}K(p,q)\leq(1-s_{i})C(r,\delta)=C(r,\delta)\varepsilon(\sigma)\,,

and likewise

(1-s_{i})I(\mathcal{C}_{j},(M\setminus\mathcal{C}_{j})\setminus\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})\leq C(r,\delta)\varepsilon(\sigma)\,.

Therefore, by (
149
) we see that

\displaystyle(1-s_{i})\Big|\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)-[\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M\setminus N)+\sum_{j}\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})]\Big|\leq

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\leq lC\delta^{n-s}+C(r,\delta)\varepsilon(\sigma)+\sum_{j}I(\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})\,.

(150)

It remains to bound
I(\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})
. For that, recall that
\partial E_{i}\cap\mathcal{N}_{2r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}
is contained in a union
\cup_{k}B_{r/2}(q_{k})
of at most
Cr^{2-n}
balls of radius
r/2
, as we recorded in the claim at the beginning of the proof. Considering the balls
B^{k}:=B_{r}(q_{k})
of radius
r
instead, observe that if
p\in E_{i}\cap\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}
and
q\in E_{i}^{c}\cap\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}
, and either
p\notin\cup_{k}B^{k}
or
q\notin\cup_{k}B^{k}
, then either
{\rm dist}(p,\cup_{k}B_{r/2}(q_{k}))\geq r/2
or
{\rm dist}(q,\cup_{k}B_{r/2}(q_{k}))\geq r/2
. Therefore, we can bound

\displaystyle{\rm dist}(p,q)

\displaystyle\geq{\rm dist}(p,\partial E_{i})+{\rm dist}(\partial E_{i},q)
