\displaystyle=\min\{{\rm dist}(p,\partial E_{i}\cap\mathcal{N}_{2r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}),{\rm dist}(p,\partial E_{i}\cap(\mathcal{N}_{2r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})^{c})\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\min\{{\rm dist}(q,\partial E_{i}\cap\mathcal{N}_{2r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}),{\rm dist}(q,\partial E_{i}\cap(\mathcal{N}_{2r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})^{c})\}

\displaystyle\geq\min\{{\rm dist}(p,\cup_{k}B_{r/2}(q_{k})),r\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\min\{{\rm dist}(q,\cup B_{r/2}(q_{k})),r\}

\displaystyle\geq r/2\,.

Since
K_{s}(p,q)
is bounded for
(p,q)
with
{\rm dist}(p,q)\geq r/2>0
, for example thanks to (
61
), this shows that there is a constant
C(r)
such that

I(\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\cup_{k}B^{k},\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})+I(\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\cup_{k}B^{k})\leq C(r)\,,

so that we can bound

\displaystyle I(\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq I(\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k},\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k})+C(r)

\displaystyle\leq I(\cup_{k}B^{k},\cup_{k}B^{k})+C(r)\,.

(151)

Observe that

\displaystyle I(\cup_{k}B^{k},\cup_{k}B^{k})

\displaystyle=\iint_{(E_{i}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k})\times(E_{i}^{c}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k})}K(p,q)\leq\iint_{(E_{i}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k})\times(E_{i}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k})^{c}}K(p,q)=\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k};M)\,.

Then, applying the interpolation result of (
25
), we can bound

\displaystyle I(\cup_{k}B^{k},\cup_{k}B^{k})

\displaystyle\leq\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k};M)\leq\frac{C}{1-s_{i}}\big[\textnormal{Per}(\partial(E_{i}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k});M)\big]^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{1-s_{i}}\big[\textnormal{Per}(\partial E_{i}\cap\cup_{k}B^{k};M)+\textnormal{Per}(\cup_{k}\partial B^{k};M)\big]^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{1-s_{i}}\big[\sum_{k}\textnormal{Per}(\partial E_{i}\cap B^{k};M)+\sum_{k}\textnormal{Per}(\partial B^{k};M)\big]^{s}\,.

The computation after the claim at the beginning of the proof shows then that both sums can be bounded by
Cr
. Substituting into (
151
),

\displaystyle(1-s_{i})I(\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{r}\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{s}+(1-s_{i})C(r)\,,

which by (
150
) shows that

\displaystyle(1-s_{i})\Big|\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)-[\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M\setminus N)+\sum_{j}\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};\mathcal{C}_{j})]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\delta^{n-s}+Cr^{s}+C(r)\varepsilon(\sigma)\,.

Substituting this bound as well as (
147
), (
148
) into (
146
), we can finally estimate

\displaystyle\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M)-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\delta^{n-1}+Cr^{s}+C(r)\varepsilon(\sigma)+\varepsilon(\delta)\,.

Fix
\delta>0
. Choosing
r
small enough so that
Cr^{s}\leq\delta
, and then choosing
i
large enough (thus
\sigma
small enough) so that
C(r)\varepsilon(\sigma)\leq\delta
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M)-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\delta

for all
i
large enough, or in other words

\displaystyle\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Big|\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M)-(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\delta\,.

By arbitrariness of
\delta
we find that
\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)=\lim_{i}\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M)
,
which since the
\partial E_{i}
converge in the varifold sense to
V
shows that
\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(E_{i};M)=\|V\|_{M}

as desired.

Step 2.
We now show (
145
), which states the convergence of the derivatives of the fractional perimeters along the flow of a vector field to the derivatives of the mass of
V
.

The argument is as in the proof of (
76
) simpler version of the proof of (
139
). Let
X
be a vector field on
M
, and let
\psi^{t}:=\psi_{X}^{t}
denote its flow at time
t
. Define
f_{i}(t)=(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s_{i}}(\psi^{t}(E_{i});M)
, and likewise
f(t)=\|\psi_{\#}^{t}(V)\|_{M}
. In step
1
we have precisely shown that
f_{i}(0)\to f(0)
as
i\to\infty
. It is very simple to see that, given any other
t
, we have that
f_{i}(t)\to f(t)
as well: Indeed, we have only used the regular convergence of the
E_{i}
to
\Sigma
outside of a small set and the estimates such as (
34
) for the
E_{i}
on that small set; considering the translated sets
\psi^{t}(E_{i})
for a fixed
t
preserves all these properties, since
\psi^{t}
is just a fixed diffeomorphism of
M
, and in particular the small set of possibly bad convergence of the
\psi^{t}(E_{i})
towards
\psi^{t}(\Sigma)
is just the flow at time
t
of the corresponding set at time
0
.

Therefore, we see that the single-variable functions
f_{i}(t)
converge pointwise to
f(t)
. By Lemma
3.12
and the standing assumption that the
E_{i}
have uniformly bounded fractional perimeters (upon multiplication by
(1-s_{i})
), we also know that the functions
\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}f_{i}(t)
are locally uniformly bounded. By Arzelà-Ascoli, we deduce that
\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}f_{i}(t)
converges locally uniformly and moreover the limit needs to be
\lim_{i}\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}f_{i}(t)=\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}f(t)
. This concludes the proof of (
145
).
∎

We can now finally prove the regularity of the limit surface
\Sigma
.

Theorem 5.35
.

Let
V
be the limit integral stationary varifold obtained in Theorem
5.31
. Then,
\Sigma={\rm supp}(V)
is a smooth surface.

Proof.

Let
p_{1},...,p_{l}
and
\Sigma
be as in Proposition
5.33
.

Step 1.
Stability on a punctured ball: Given any
p\in M
, we show that there exists
r_{p}>0
such that
\Sigma
is stable on
B_{r_{p}}\setminus\{p\}
.

Assume first that
p\neq p_{1},p_{2},...,p_{l}
. By Lemma
5.32
, there exist
r_{p}>0
and
\Lambda_{p}>0
such that the
E_{i}
are eventually
\Lambda
-almost stable on
B_{r_{p}}(p)
. This implies that
\Sigma
is stable in
B_{r_{p}}(p)
: Let
X
be a smooth vector field supported in
B_{r_{p}}(p)
, and let and let
\psi^{t}:=\psi_{X}^{t}
denote its flow at time
t
. Then, by (
145
) and the definition of
\Lambda
-almost stability (see Definition
5.7
, and recall the last line in Definition
5.3
) we have that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\|\psi_{\#}^{t}(V)\|_{M}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i}\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi^{t}(E_{i});M)\geq-\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})\Lambda_{p}\Big(\int_{\partial E_{i}\cap B_{r_{p}}(p)}|X\cdot\nu_{\Sigma}|\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq-\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})\Lambda_{p}[\|X\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\textnormal{Per}(\partial E_{i};M)]^{2}=0\,,

as desired.

Let now
p
be one of the
p_{1},...,p_{l}
instead. If (arguing by contradiction) we assume that there exists no
r_{p}>0
such that
\Sigma
is stable on
B_{r_{p}}(p)\setminus\{p\}
, given
\delta>0
we can in particular find
(m+1)
vector fields
X_{1}
,…,
X_{m+1}
, supported respectively on small disjoint annuli
A_{1},...,A_{m+1}
centered at
p
and contained in
B_{\delta}(p)
, such that
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\|(\psi_{X_{k}}^{t})_{\#}V\|_{M}<0
for every
k
.

On the other hand, for each
i
we can find (by Lemma
5.8
) one of the
A_{1},...,A_{m+1}
such that
E_{i}
is
\Lambda
-almost stable in it, for some
\Lambda<\infty
depending only on the fixed
A_{1},...,A_{m+1}
. Thus, by passing to a subsequence (which we do not relable), we can assume that the
E_{i}
are all
\Lambda
-almost stable on the same annulus, say
A_{1}
. From this we deduce (see (
85
)) that
\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=0}\text{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X_{1}}^{t}(E_{i}))\geq-C
for some constant
C<\infty
depending on
\Lambda
,
X_{1}
and
\sup_{i}\textnormal{Per}(\partial E_{i};M)
, the supremum of classical perimeters being bounded thanks to Proposition
5.30
. By Proposition
5.34
, we can then pass to the limit as
i\to\infty
to deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\|(\psi_{X_{1}}^{t})_{\#}V\|_{M}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\big|_{t=0}\text{Per}_{s}(\psi_{X_{1}}^{t}(E_{i}))\geq-\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})C=0\,,

which is a contradiction.

This concludes the proof of Step
1
.

Step 2.
We prove that
\Sigma={\rm supp}(V)
is smooth everywhere.

In Proposition
5.33
we have proved that the support
\Sigma
of
V
is a smooth (minimal) surface in all of
M\setminus\{p_{1},...,p_{l}\}
. In particular, we have that (with
n=3
)

\mathcal{H}^{n-3}(\textnormal{Sing}(\Sigma))=\mathcal{H}^{0}(\textnormal{Sing}(\Sigma))\leq l<\infty\,.

We will now show that if
p
is one of the
p_{1},...,p_{l}
, then
\Sigma
is also smooth around
p
. First, by Step 1, we deduce that
V
is a stable integral varifold on a punctured ball
B_{r_{p}}\setminus\{p\}
. Moreover, we have just argued that
\mathcal{H}^{n-3}(\textnormal{Sing}(\Sigma))<\infty
with
n=3
. The classical result
6
6

6

Recall that
n=3
refers to ambient dimension in the present article. This corresponds to
n=2
in the notation employed in
[
49
]
.
in
[
49
]
on the regularity of stable minimal surfaces with small singular set (points, in our case, which have null
2
-capacity on a surface) can then be used to show that
\Sigma
is actually smooth also at the point
p
. To be precise, for
n>3
this would be precisely the result in
[
49
]
, and in the present case
n=3
the removability of point singularities was first given in
[
52
]
by using a tangent cone analysis on top of the results in
[
49
]
. See also the appendix of
[
50
]
for how a simple capacitary argument allows to apply
[
49
]
directly instead.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
1.4
. We have essentially already obtained most of its points.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Step 1.
Convergence to a limit.

By Theorem
5.31
, we have the convergence of a subsequence of the
\partial E_{i}
to a limit stationary integral varifold
V
in the varifold sense. Moreover, by Theorem
5.35
, this varifold is supported on a smooth surface
\Sigma
. Let
\Sigma_{1},...,\Sigma_{m}
denote the connected components of
\Sigma
; then, by the Constancy Theorem (see
[
51
, Theorem 41.1]
), the
\Sigma_{k}
are minimal surfaces and there are integers
n_{k}
such that
V=\sum_{k=1}^{m}n_{k}[\Sigma_{k}]
, where
[\Sigma_{k}]
denotes the integer rectifiable varifold associated to the smooth surface
\Sigma_{k}
. Combining this with Proposition
5.34
, we deduce that

\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})\textnormal{Per}_{s}(E_{i};M)=\lim_{i}\textnormal{Per}(E_{i};M)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}n_{k}\rm{Per}(\Sigma_{k})\,.

Step 2.
Constancy of the multiplicity in the convergence.

Let
p_{1},...,p_{l}
be as in Proposition
5.33
. We want to show that a subsequence of the
E_{i}
converges towards
\Sigma\setminus\{p_{1},...,p_{l}\}
with constant multiplicity over each component. Let then
K:=K_{\delta}=\Sigma_{k}\setminus\{B_{\delta}(p_{1})\cup...\cup B_{\delta}(p_{l})\}
. If
\delta
is small enough, then
K_{\delta}
is still a connected set
7
7

7

Thanks to
\Sigma_{k}
being smooth and connected, so that locally around the
p_{j}
, topologically we are just removing a disk from a bigger disk.
. Moreover, by Proposition
5.33
, the
E_{i}
are eventually graphs over
K_{\delta}
. Fix
i
; then, since (given a natural number
d\in\mathbb{N}
) having a fixed number of leaves
d
is both an open and closed condition, by connectedness the number of leaves of
E_{i}
over the limit is constant on
K_{\delta}
. Now, denoting this multiplicity by
d=d_{i}
, a priori it could vary when varying the index
i
; on the other hand,
d_{i}
is uniformly bounded, since
\limsup_{i}d_{i}\textnormal{Per}(\Sigma\cap K_{\delta})\leq\limsup_{i}\textnormal{Per}(E_{i})
and the latter quantity is uniformly bounded by Proposition
5.30
. Therefore, passing to a subsequence we can take
d_{i}
to be identical for all
i
. Finally, observe that choosing any
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
does not change the result, since
K_{\delta}\subset K_{\delta^{\prime}}
and as argued above the number of leaves over
K_{\delta}^{\prime}
needs to be constant, therefore (for
i
large enough) this number coincides with the one over
K_{\delta}
.

Step 3.
The minimal surface
\Sigma
has Morse index bounded by
\mathfrak{p}
.

To check this, consider
(\mathfrak{p}+1)
vector fields
X_{0},...,X_{\mathfrak{p}}
of class
C^{\infty}
on
M
.

Letting
a:=(a_{0},a_{1},...,a_{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{p}+1}
and
X[a]=a_{0}X_{0}+...+a_{\mathfrak{p}}X_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we can define the quadratic form
Q_{i}(a):=(1-s_{i})\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi^{t}_{X[a]}(E_{i}))
, which we can write as
Q_{i}(a)=Q_{i}^{kl}a_{k}a_{l}
for some coefficients
Q_{i}^{kl}
. From (
145
) and the polarization identity for a quadratic form, it is immediate to see that
Q_{\varepsilon_{j}}^{kl}\to Q_{0}^{kl}
as
j\to\infty
, where
Q_{0}(a):=\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\|(\psi_{X_{[a]}}^{t})_{\#}V\|_{M}=Q_{0}^{kl}a_{k}a_{l}
.

Now, since the
E_{i}
have Morse index
\leq\mathfrak{p}
, by definition we know that for every
i
there must exist some
a^{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}
such that

Q_{i}(a^{i})=(1-s_{i})\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\textnormal{Per}_{s}(\psi^{t}_{X[a^{i}]}(E_{i}))\geq 0\,;

(152)

the convergence of the coefficients
Q_{i}^{kl}
to
Q_{0}^{kl}
then immediately shows that
Q_{0}(a)\geq 0
for some
a\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}
as well, which is precisely a restatement of the fact that
\Sigma
have Morse index at most
\mathfrak{p}
.
∎

6
Density and equidistribution of classical minimal surfaces – Proofs of Corollary
1.5
and Theorems
1.6
-
1.7

With the results up to now, we can give:

Proof of Corollary
1.5
.

Given
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, let
\{s_{i}\}_{i}
be a sequence of numbers in
(0,1)
such that
\lim_{i}s_{i}=1
and

\lim_{i}(1-s_{i})l_{s_{i}}(\mathfrak{p},M)=\liminf_{s\to 1}(1-s)l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},M)=l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)\,,

which exists by definition (
3
) of
l_{1}(\mathfrak{p},M)
. Let moreover
E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s_{i}}
be given by Theorem
1.1
. Applying Theorem
1.4
to the
\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s_{i}}
, which are smooth
s_{i}
-minimal surfaces with Morse index bounded by
\mathfrak{p}
and which satisfy the fractional perimeter bound

(1-s_{i}){\rm Per}_{s_{i}}(\partial E_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s_{i}})=(1-s_{i})l_{s_{i}}(\mathfrak{p},M)\leq C\mathfrak{p}^{s_{i}/n}\,,

we conclude the desired result.
∎

In what follows, it will be useful to keep in mind the notation and properties in (
36
)–(
39
) and (
66
)–(
65
). To lighten the notation, we will write
l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}(N,h)
instead of
l_{s}(\mathfrak{p},(N,h))
when metrics appear.

We start with the following simple lemma, an analogue of
[
44
, Lemma 1]
and which shows that the
\mathfrak{p}
-widths are continuous (more precisely, locally Lipschitz) with respect to the metric:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\widetilde{g}
be a
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric on
M
, and let
C_{1}<C_{2}
be positive constants. Then, there is
K=K(\widetilde{g},C_{1},C_{2})
such that

|\mathfrak{p}^{-1/n}l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}(M,g^{\prime})-\mathfrak{p}^{-1/n}l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}(M,g)|\leq K|g^{\prime}-g|_{\widetilde{g}}\,,

for any
g,g^{\prime}\in\{h\mbox{ smooth metric on }M:C_{1}\widetilde{g}\leq h\leq C_{2}\widetilde{g}
and any
p\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By Step 2 in the proof of Theorem
1.3
, we know that
l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}(M,g)=l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g)
. Therefore, the lemma is equivalent to proving that

|\mathfrak{p}^{-1/n}l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g^{\prime})-\mathfrak{p}^{-1/n}l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g)|\leq K|g^{\prime}-g|_{\widetilde{g}}\,.

To prove that this, we start by looking at the fractional Allen-Cahn functional for some
s\in(0,1)
. We consider Lemma
4.5
, with
(M_{1},g_{1})=(M,g^{\prime})
and
(M_{2},g_{2})=(M,g)
,
\Omega_{1}=M_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}=M_{2}
, and
F=id_{M}
viewed as a map
F:M_{1}\to M_{2}
. Then, if
u\in H^{s/2,d}(M_{1})=H^{s/2,d}(M_{2})
, it holds that

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u\circ F,M_{1})\leq\text{Lip}(F)^{3n+s}\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(u,M_{2})\,,

and then, by equality of the spaces
H^{s/2,d}(M_{1})
and
H^{s/2,d}(M_{2})
, taking
p
-th min-max values according to (
45
) we get that

c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M_{1})\leq\text{Lip}(F)^{3n+s}c_{\varepsilon,s}^{d}(\mathfrak{p},M_{2})\,.

Sending
\varepsilon
to
0
, we deduce that

l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M_{1})\leq\text{Lip}(F)^{3n+s}l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M_{2})\,.

Now, we can bound

\displaystyle\rm{Lip}(F)^{3n+s}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sup_{(p,q)\in M\times M}\frac{\rm{dist}_{M_{2}}(p,q)}{\rm{dist}_{M_{1}}(p,q)}\Bigg)^{3n+s}\leq\Bigg(\sup_{(p,q)\in M\times M}\frac{\sup_{v\in TM}\frac{|v|_{g_{2}}}{|v|_{g_{1}}}\rm{dist}_{M_{1}}(p,q)}{\rm{dist}_{M_{1}}(p,q)}\Bigg)^{3n+s}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sup_{v\in TM}\frac{|v|_{g_{2}}}{|v|_{g_{1}}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}=\Bigg(1+\sup_{v\in TM}\frac{|v|_{g_{2}}-|v|_{g_{1}}}{|v|_{g_{1}}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg(1+\sup_{v\in TM}\frac{|v|_{\widetilde{g}}}{|v|_{g_{1}}}\sup_{v\in TM}\frac{|v|_{g_{2}}-|v|_{g_{1}}}{|v|_{\widetilde{g}}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}\leq\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{C_{1}}|g_{2}-g_{1}|_{\widetilde{g}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{C_{1}}|g^{\prime}-g|_{\widetilde{g}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}\,.

Substituting, and using again the definition of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, we get that

l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g^{\prime})\leq\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{C_{1}}|g^{\prime}-g|_{\widetilde{g}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g)\,,

(153)

or in other words

l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g^{\prime})-l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g)\leq\Bigg[\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{C_{1}}|g^{\prime}-g|_{\widetilde{g}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}-1\Bigg]l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g)\,.

Now, applying first (
153
) with
(M,g)
and
(M,\widetilde{g})
instead on the right hand side, and using then the upper bound in (
48
) with
\Omega
equal to the Riemannian manifold
(M,\widetilde{g})
, we get that

(1-s)l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g^{\prime})\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}-(1-s)l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g)\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}\leq C(\widetilde{g},C_{1},C_{2})\Bigg[\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{C_{1}}|g^{\prime}-g|_{\widetilde{g}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}-1\Bigg]\,,

or equivalently

(1-s)l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g^{\prime})\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}\leq(1-s)l_{s,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g)\mathfrak{p}^{-s/n}+C(\widetilde{g},C_{1},C_{2})\Bigg[\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{C_{1}}|g^{\prime}-g|_{\widetilde{g}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}-1\Bigg]\,.

Taking finally
\liminf_{s}
on both sides and substracting again, by definition of
l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}
we get that

l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g^{\prime})\mathfrak{p}^{-1/n}-l_{1,\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M,g)\mathfrak{p}^{-1/n}\leq C(\widetilde{g},C_{1},C_{2})\Bigg[\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{C_{1}}|g^{\prime}-g|_{\widetilde{g}}\Bigg)^{3n+s}-1\Bigg]\,,

from which the desired result follows by a Taylor expansion.
∎

The short proof of Theorem
1.6
on the density for generic metrics then follows as in
[
40
]
(see also
[
33
]
). We indicate the few modifications needed.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

The proof is just as in
[
40
, Proposition 3.1]
, which uses the minimal surfaces associated to the Almgren-Pitts spectrum and the Weyl Law associated to them. One simply need substitute the continuity of the
\mathfrak{p}
-widths in
[
40
, Lemma 2.1]
by Lemma
6.1
, the attainability of the min-max widths in
[
40
, Proposition 2.2]
by Corollary
1.5
, and the Weyl Law from
[
42
]
by Theorem
1.3
.
∎

Likewise, one can obtain Theorem
1.7
on the equidistribution of classical minimal surfaces for generic metrics:

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

The proof in
[
44
]
, which again uses the minimal surfaces and Weyl Law associated to the Almgren-Pitts spectrum, can be replicated in our case. The result in
[
44
, Lemma 1]
on the Lipschitz continuity of the
\mathfrak{p}
-widths is replaced by Lemma
6.1
from the present paper, the attainability of the min-max widths in
[
40
, Proposition 2.2]
(which is used in the proof of
[
44
, Lemma 2]
) is replaced by Corollary
1.5
, and the Weyl Law from
[
42
]
is replaced by Theorem
1.3
.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
3.19

Proof of Theorem
3.19
.

The existence of a critical point
u_{\varepsilon,\mathfrak{p}}
with energy
c_{\varepsilon,s}
follows from applying a min-max theorem to the functional
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as the set of min-max families, exactly as in the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 3.4]
. The fact that
u_{\varepsilon,\mathfrak{p}}
has index bounded by
\mathfrak{p}
comes from the fact that min-max critical points of families of the type (
35
) satisfy this upper index bound; this is thanks to the Hausdorff measure condition
\mathcal{H}^{\mathfrak{p}}(A)<\infty
, as described in
[
41
]
, which intuitively corresponds to considering sets
A
with at most
\mathfrak{p}
parameters in the min-max.

Finally, the upper and lower energy bounds in (
37
) follow the same strategy as in the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 3.2]
. To be precise, in
[
19
]
we considered the family

\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\big\{A=f(\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}})\ :\ f\in C^{0}(\mathbb{S}^{p};H^{s/2}(M)\backslash\{0\})\hskip 7.0pt\text{and}\hskip 7.0ptf(-x)=-f(x)\ \forall\ x\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}\big\}

instead.

By the Borsuk–Ulam theorem, given
A\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{p}}
(and assuming that the Hausdorff condition holds) then

A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as well. This inclusion was, in fact, the only property that was used to prove the lower bounds, thus they hold for
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as well.

On the other hand, the upper bounds were shown by constructing an explicit
f:\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}\to H^{s/2}(M)\setminus{0}
such that all elements in
A=f(\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathfrak{p}}
have low energy. If the Hausdorff measure condition
\mathcal{H}^{\mathfrak{p}}(A)<\infty
holds, then
A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and thus the same set
A
works in our case. Unfortunately, the only reasonable way of showing the measure condition is to see that
f:\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}\to H^{s/2}(M)\setminus{0}
is Lipschitz, which is not true for the function
f
considered in
[
19
, Theorem 3.2]
– in fact, it is only
\beta
-Holder continuous for an intermediate exponent
\beta\in(0,1)
in general. We now explain the construction and how to adapt it to our case to obtain a Lipschitz map.

Essentially, via the exponential map and a finite covering of our manifold with
\mathfrak{p}
small balls, in
[
19
]
the construction is reduced to the case
M=\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}(0)
, the Euclidean ball with radius
r_{\mathfrak{p}}=C\mathfrak{p}^{-\frac{1}{n}}
. Then,
f
is defined as the map which sends
a=(a_{0},a_{1},\dotsc,a_{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}
to the function
u_{a}\in H^{s/2}(M)\setminus\{0\}
defined by
u_{a}(x)={\rm sgn}(P_{a}(x_{n}))
with
P_{a}(z)=a_{0}+a_{1}z+\dotsc+a_{p}z^{\mathfrak{p}}
. Therefore,
u_{a}
is a characteristic function with values
\pm 1
depending on the sign of
P_{a}(x_{n})
, and it jumps at most
\mathfrak{p}
times when
x_{n}
coincides with a root of
P_{a}
with odd multiplicity. This shows that
u_{a}
has BV norm at most
C\mathfrak{p}\cdot r_{\mathfrak{p}}^{n-1}=C\mathfrak{p}^{1/n}
, which by the interpolation result in (
23
) shows that it has
H^{s/2}
norm at most
\frac{C}{1-s}\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}
as desired.

The only reason why this assignation
f:\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}\to H^{s/2}(M)\setminus\{0\}
might not be Lipschitz is that, when changing
a
, two roots of
P_{a}
might approach each other (or approach
\pm r_{\mathfrak{p}}
). Just as an example, consider
t\in N:=(0,1)
and
P_{t}(z)=z(z-t)
, which has the roots
0
and
t
. Define the single-variable function
u_{t}(z)={\rm sgn}(P_{t}(z))
for
z\in(-1,1)
, so that
u_{t}
takes the value
-1
on
(-1,0)
,
+1
on
(0,t)
, and
-1
again on
(t,1)
. As
t\to 0
, it is clear that
[u_{t}]_{H^{s/2}(-1,1)}\to 0
: for example, by applying (
23
) to
u_{t}+1
, we see that
[u_{t}]_{H^{s/2}(-1,1)}^{2}\leq Ct^{1-s}
, or equivalently
[u_{t}]_{H^{s/2}(-1,1)}\leq Ct^{\frac{1-s}{2}}
. On the other hand, as a function of
t
,
t\mapsto Ct^{\frac{1-s}{2}}
is not Lipschitz, and in general this bound cannot be improved.

Going back to the case
M=\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, to solve this issue we regularise our functions in a simple way. Consider a smooth, nondecreasing, odd function
\eta
such that
\eta(x)={\rm sgn}(x)
for
|x|\geq 1
and
\eta(x)=0
only at
x=0
. Define
\eta_{\delta}(x)=\eta(x/\delta)
, which as
\delta\to 0
converges to
{\rm sgn}(x)
, and set
u_{a,\delta}(x)=\eta_{\delta}\circ P_{a}(x_{n})
, which is a smoothed out version of the function
u_{a}(x)
. Therefore, the
BV
norm of
u_{a,\delta}
is bounded in the same way as for
u_{a}
, since the function
u_{a,\delta}(x)
varies in total just as much (it is monotone where
P_{a}
is, and there are at most
(\mathfrak{p}-1)
values of
x_{n}
where
P_{a}(x_{n})
changes monotonicity since it is a polynomial of degree at most
\mathfrak{p}
). The interpolation result (
23
) then shows once again that
[u_{a,\delta}]_{H^{s/2}(\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}})}\leq\frac{C}{1-s}\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}
. Moreover, since
W(\pm 1)=0
and
W(s)\leq\frac{1}{4}
for
s\in[-1,1]
, we see that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{a\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{s}}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}}W(u_{a,\delta})\leq\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{s}}\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{a\in\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}}\big|\{x:|P_{a}(x)|\leq\delta\}\cap\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}\big|=0\,,

where the last equality follows from the fact that the set of
x\in\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}
where the polynomial
P_{a}(x_{n})
—whose vector of coefficients
a
has norm one— is close to zero has very small measure. This elementary fact is easy to see, for example by a compactness+contradiction argument.

Then, by choosing (fixing)
\delta>0
small enough depending on
\mathfrak{p}
and
\varepsilon
, we find that
\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon,s}(u_{a,\delta},\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}})\leq\frac{C}{1-s}\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}
. Moreover, by the definition of
\eta
we see that
u_{a,\delta}
is never the zero function, since neither is
P_{a}
, and moreover
u_{-a,\delta}=-u_{a,\delta}
by oddness of
\eta
. Let
f:\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}\to H^{s/2}(M)\setminus\{0\}
,
f(a)=u_{a,\delta}
. To see that
A=f(\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}})
is in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, it remains only to see that
f
is a Lipschitz function, since it will imply that
\mathcal{H}^{\mathfrak{p}}(A)<\infty
; with this, we will conclude the proof of the desired upper bounds in (
37
).

Now, letting
g(a,x):=u_{a,\delta}(x)
, by smoothness in
(a,x)
we can bound
\|g\|_{C^{2}}\leq C(\delta)
for some constant
C(\delta)
, the value of which is not important (as we have already fixed
\delta
). This shows that, given
a
and
b=a+h
in
\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\displaystyle[u_{a+h,\delta}-u_{a,\delta}]_{H^{s/2}(M)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}\times\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\Big((u_{a+h,\delta}-u_{a,\delta})(x)-(u_{a+h,\delta}-u_{a,\delta})(y)\Big)^{2}K(x,y)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}\times\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\Big(\int_{0}^{|h|}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big[u_{a+s\frac{h}{|h|},\delta}(x)-u_{a+s\frac{h}{|h|},\delta}(y)\big]ds\Big)^{2}K(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq|h|^{2}\iint_{\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}\times\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\sup_{s}\big[\frac{\partial}{\partial s}u_{a+s\frac{h}{|h|},\delta}(x)-\frac{\partial}{\partial s}u_{a+s\frac{h}{|h|},\delta}(y)\big]^{2}K(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq|h|^{2}\|g\|_{C^{2}}\iint_{\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}\times\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}}|x-y|^{2}K(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq|h|^{2}C(\delta)\,,

which after taking square roots on both sides proves that
a\mapsto u_{a,\delta}
is a Lipschitz map from
\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{p}}
to
H^{s/2}(\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}})
. This concludes the proof in the case
M=\mathcal{B}_{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}
. As mentioned at the beginning, in general one actually decomposes
M
as a union of
\mathfrak{p}
subsets which (passing to normal coordinates) are of this type; see the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 3.2]
. The additional interactions between the respective subsets can simply be bounded directly by the fractional perimeters of the subsets, including in the proof of the Lipschitz property above in the general case.
∎

Appendix B
Step 4 of the proof of Proposition
4.7

In Step 1, given
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(\Omega)
, we constructed
A^{\prime}\subset H^{s/2}(M)\setminus\{0\}
with energy not surpassing that of
A
by much (in the sense that every
U\in A^{\prime}
is obtained from some
u\in A
by
U\big|_{C_{i}}=u\circ F_{i}
, and the bound (
59
) holds). On the other hand, we explained that
f:H^{s/2,d}(\Omega)\to H^{s/2,d}(M)
defined by
f(u):=U
may not necessarily be a Lipschitz map; in case it were, it would imply that
A^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(M)
and we would conclude the proof.

Fix
A\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{d}(\Omega)
. We want to regularise the map
f
(restricted to
A
) by a very close equivariant map
f_{\delta}:A\to H^{s/2}(M)\setminus\{0\}
which is moreover Lipschitz, which will allows us to conclude the result. Let
\xi:[0,+\infty)\to[0,1]
be a smooth, increasing function such that
\xi(0)=0
and
\xi(x)=1
for
x\geq 1
. Let
\delta>0
, which we will choose later
depending on
A
, and set

\xi_{\delta}(t)=\xi(t/\delta)

and
\eta_{\delta}(p)=\xi_{\delta}\circ{\rm dist}(p,\cup_{i}\partial C_{i}))
. The assignation
f_{\delta}(u)=U_{\delta}:=\eta_{\delta}U
is still equivariant, and it is not zero if
u
isn’t either. Define
A_{\delta}^{\prime}=f_{\delta}(A)
.

We now compute:

\displaystyle[U_{\delta}]_{H^{s/2}(M)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{j}}(U_{\delta}(p)-U_{\delta}(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}((\eta_{\delta}U)(p)-(\eta_{\delta}U)(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i\neq j}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{j}}((\eta_{\delta}U)(p)-(\eta_{\delta}U)(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}\eta_{\delta}^{2}(p)(U(p)-U(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+2\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}U(p)^{2}(\eta_{\delta}(p)-\eta_{\delta}(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+4\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times(M\setminus C_{i})}\eta_{\delta}^{2}(p)U^{2}(p){\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq\,.

Using that
\eta_{\delta}\leq 1
and
|\eta_{\delta}(p)-\eta_{\delta}(q)|\leq C(\delta){\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)
, together with (
61
) we can bound

\displaystyle 2\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}\eta_{\delta}^{2}(p)(U(p)-U(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\qquad+2\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}U(p)^{2}(\eta_{\delta}(p)-\eta_{\delta}(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq\leq

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}(U(p)-U(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq+C(\delta)\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}U(p)^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s-2)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}(U(p)-U(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq+C(\delta)\sum_{i}\int_{C_{i}}U(p)^{2}\,dp

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta)\sum_{i}\|U\|_{H^{s/2}(C_{i})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta)\sum_{i}\|u\|_{H^{s/2}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}^{2}.

We now bound the outer interaction term, using (
61
) and the fact that
\eta_{\delta}(p)\leq C(\delta)\text{dist}_{M}(p,\partial C_{i})
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}

\displaystyle\iint_{C_{i}\times(M\setminus C_{i})}\eta_{\delta}^{2}(p)U^{2}(p){\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{C_{i}\times(M\setminus C_{i})}\eta_{\delta}^{2}(p)U^{2}(p)\text{dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq\leq C\sum_{i}\int_{C_{i}}\eta_{\delta}^{2}(p)U^{2}(p)\text{dist}_{M}(p,\partial C_{i})^{-s}

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta)\sum_{i}\int_{C_{i}}U^{2}(p)\text{dist}_{M}(p,\partial C_{i})^{2-s}\leq C(\delta)\sum_{i}\int_{C_{i}}U^{2}(p)

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta)\sum_{i}\|U\|_{H^{s/2}(C_{i})}^{2}\leq C(\delta)\sum_{i}\|u\|_{H^{s/2}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}^{2}\,.

In other words, we have found that
\|U_{\delta}\|_{H^{s/2}(M)}^{2}=\|U_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}+[U_{\delta}]_{H^{s/2}(M)}^{2}\leq C(\delta)\sum_{i}\|u\|_{H^{s/2}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}^{2}
. Applying this to
u_{1}-u_{2}
instead, we conclude that our assignation
f_{\delta}(u)=U_{\delta}
is Lipschitz. This shows that
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(A_{\delta}^{\prime})\leq C(\delta)\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(A)<\infty
and therefore
A_{\delta}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

We will now show that for
\delta>0
small enough depending on
A
, we have that

\sup_{u\in A}|\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U,M)-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{d}(U_{\delta},M)|\leq\frac{C}{1-s}

(154)

for some constant
C
depending only on the
C_{i}\subset M
(but not on
A
). For that, we will separately show that

\|U_{\delta}-U\|_{H^{s/2}(M)}\leq\frac{C}{1-s}

and

\sup_{u\in A}|\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Pot}(U_{\delta},M)-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Pot}(U,M)|\leq C

for
\delta>0
small enough (in fact, the second error can be made to go to zero).

Let
V=U-U_{\delta}=(1-\eta_{\delta})U
. The computations above with
V
in place of
U_{\delta}
give that

\displaystyle[V]_{H^{s/2}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}(1-\eta_{\delta})^{2}(p)(U(p)-U(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+2\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}U(p)^{2}(\eta_{\delta}(p)-\eta_{\delta}(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+4\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times(M\setminus C_{i})}(1-\eta_{\delta})^{2}(p)U^{2}(p){\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq\,.

Since
|U|\leq 1
, we can just bound the last term by
\frac{C}{1-s}
with a
C
constant depending on the fractional perimeters of the
C_{i}
. Moreover, using (
27
) and the fact that
\|\eta_{\delta}\|_{BV(M)}
is uniformly bounded independently of
\delta
(which is clear from its definition as a regularisation of a characteristic function) we can bound the second term:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}U(p)^{2}(\eta_{\delta}(p)-\eta_{\delta}(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{M\times M}(\eta_{\delta}(p)-\eta_{\delta}(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{1-s}\,.

Furthermore, from the definition of
\eta_{\delta}
we can bound

\displaystyle\|U\|_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}}(1-\eta_{\delta})^{2}(p)U(p)^{2}\,dp\leq\sum_{i}\int_{C_{i}\cap\{p:{\rm dist}(p,\partial C_{i})\leq\delta\}}U(p)^{2}\,dp

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\iint_{C_{i}\times C_{i}}(1-\eta_{\delta})^{2}(p)(U(p)-U(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{i}\iint_{(C_{i}\cap\{p:{\rm dist}(p,\partial C_{i})\leq\delta\})\times C_{i}}(U(p)-U(q))^{2}{\rm dist}_{M}(p,q)^{-(n+s)}\,dp\,dq\,,

both of which go uniformly to zero as
\delta\to 0
thanks to the equi-integrability in
H^{s/2}
of the
u
in
A
(which follows immediately from the compactness of
A
, or more precisely the equivalent characterisation of being a totally bounded set of functions, together with the fact that a finite family of functions is always equi-integrable).
Putting everything together, we see that

\|U_{\delta}-U\|_{H^{s/2}(M)}=\|V\|_{H^{s/2}(M)}\leq\frac{C}{1-s}

for
\delta=\delta(A)
small enough.

Finally, regarding the difference in potential energies, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Pot}(U_{\delta},M)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{s}}\int_{M}W(U_{\delta}(p))\,dp=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{s}}\int_{M}W(\eta_{\delta}(p)U(p))\,dp\,,

so that (since
|U|,|U_{\delta}|,|\eta_{\delta}|\leq 1
)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Pot}(U_{\delta},M)-\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Pot}(U,M)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{s}}|\{p:U_{\delta}(p)\neq U(p)\}|=\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{s}}|\{p:{\rm dist}(p,\cup_{i}\partial C_{i})\leq\delta\}|\leq C

for
\delta
small enough depending on
\varepsilon
.

With this, we end the proof of (
154
). Using the
A_{\delta}
instead of
A
in Steps 1–3, the only difference being that we need to consider an additional constant error term on the RHS of (
59
) (and which immediately gets killed after (
60
) since it gets divided by
\mathfrak{p}^{s/n}
before letting
\mathfrak{p}\to\infty
), we finish the proof of Proposition
4.7
.

Appendix C
Some Riemannian computations

Proof of (
117
) from (
116
).

For a function
f(q^{\prime})
on
M
, its gradient
\nabla_{q^{\prime}}^{M}f
at
q^{\prime}=\varphi_{q}(y)
is represented in coordinates by the Euclidean vector
\varphi_{q}^{*}(\nabla_{q^{\prime}}^{M}f)=g^{-1}(y)\nabla_{y}^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\varphi(y))
. Here
g^{-1}
denotes the inverse of the matrix
g=(g_{ij})_{ij}
corresponding to the metric of
M
in coordinates, and the product is to be understood as a matrix product. This shows, then, that
\nabla_{q^{\prime}}^{M}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{q}(0),\varphi_{q}(y))
corresponds to
g^{-1}\nabla_{y}^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{q}(0),\varphi_{q}(y))
in coordinates.

Regarding the normal vector
\nu_{\varphi_{q}(\hat{\Gamma})}(\varphi_{q}(y))
, it corresponds to

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}(\nu_{\varphi_{q}(\hat{\Gamma})})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}(y)

(155)

in coordinates: Indeed, let
v\in T_{q^{\prime}}M
be tangent to the surface
\varphi_{q}(\hat{\Gamma})
, which means that its Euclidean representative
\varphi^{*}(v)
is tangent to
\hat{\Gamma}
at
y
. Then,

\displaystyle\langle v,\varphi_{*}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}})\rangle_{g}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}[(\varphi_{*})^{-1}(v)]^{t}gg^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}[(\varphi_{*})^{-1}(v)]^{t}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}=0

by orthogonality. Furthermore,

\displaystyle|\varphi_{*}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}})|_{g}=\frac{1}{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}gg^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}=\frac{1}{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}=1\,.

These two properties imply that
\varphi_{*}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}})
is the unit vector orthogonal to
\varphi_{q}(\hat{\Gamma})
, as we wanted to see.

Combining our computations so far, we conclude that

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})}(\varphi_{p}(y)),\nabla_{q^{\prime}}^{M}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{p}(0),\varphi_{p}(y))\rangle_{g(\varphi_{p}(y))}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}\big[g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}(y)\big]^{t}g\big[g^{-1}\nabla_{y}^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{p}(0),\varphi_{p}(y))\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{t}g^{-1}\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}}\big[\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}(y)\big]^{t}g^{-1}(y)\nabla_{y}^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{s-2}(\varphi_{p}(0),\varphi_{p}(y))\,,

which leads to (
117
) from (
116
).
∎

Proof of (
120
).

We now show (
120
), i.e. that the mean curvature of
\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})
at
p\in M
is the same as the mean curvature of
\hat{\Gamma}
at
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
thanks to having taken normal coordinates. Let
X_{i}=\varphi_{*}(e_{i})
, where
e_{1},...,e_{n}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Consider the second fundamental form of
\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})
at
p
, represented by the matrix
\Big(\langle\nabla_{X_{i}}\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})},X_{j}\rangle_{g}(p)\Big)_{ij}
in our coordinates. Here
\nabla_{V}W
indicates covariant differentiation with respect to the Levi-Civita connection, which letting
\varphi^{*}(V)=V^{i}e_{i}
and
\varphi^{*}(W)=W^{j}e_{j}
has the coordinate expression

\varphi^{*}(\nabla_{V}W)=V^{i}\frac{\partial W^{j}}{\partial x^{i}}e_{j}+V^{i}W^{j}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}e_{k}\,.

Now, in normal coordinates, the metric is Euclidean at the origin and its first derivatives vanish there; this also gives that the Christoffel symbols
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}
of the Levi-Civita connection vanish at
0
. Therefore, we can compute

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{X_{i}}\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})},X_{j}\rangle_{g}(p)

\displaystyle=\big([\varphi^{*}(\nabla_{X_{i}}\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})})]^{t}g[e_{j}]\big)(0)=\frac{\partial\varphi^{*}(\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})})}{\partial x^{i}}\Big|_{x=0}\cdot e_{j}\,.

Now, differentiating (
155
) and recalling once again that
g
and its first derivatives are Euclidean at the origin, we see that actually
\frac{\partial\varphi^{*}(\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})})}{\partial x^{i}}\Big|_{x=0}=\frac{\partial\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}{\partial x^{i}}\Big|_{x=0}
, thus we arrive at

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{X_{i}}\nu_{\varphi_{p}(\hat{\Gamma})},X_{j}\rangle_{g}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\nu_{\hat{\Gamma}}}{\partial x^{i}}\Big|_{x=0}\cdot e_{j}\,.

On the other hand, the expression on the right is actually the
(i,j)
-th entry of the second fundamental form of
\hat{\Gamma}
at
0
, which shows that actually both matrices are equal. Since the traces of the second fundamental forms are precisely the corresponding mean curvatures, this concludes our proof.
∎

Appendix D
Gradient behaviour of the kernel
K_{s}
– Proof of Proposition
5.21

We will first show three intermediate results on the gradient behaviour of the heat kernel
H_{M}
, after which the proof of Proposition
5.21
will be given.

Proposition D.1
.

Let
M
be a closed,
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Assume that the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M,g,4,p_{0},\varphi)
are satisfied, with
l=n+2
. Let
H_{M}(p,q,t)
be the heat kernel of
M
, and let
E_{M}:=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{{\rm dist}_{M}^{2}(p,q)}{4t}}
. Then, writing
H(x,y,t):=H_{M}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y),t)
and
E(x,y,t):=E_{M}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y),t)
, there are constants
C,c,r>0
depending only on
n
such that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}(H-E)|(x,y,t)\leq C|x-y|\Big(1+\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{t}\Big)E+Ct^{l+1}+Ce^{-c/t}

for all
(x,y,t)\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times(0,1]
.

Proof of Proposition
D.1
.

Step 1.
Reduction to the case of a torus with perturbed metric.

As usual,
g_{ij}
will denote the coefficients of the metric
g
in the coordinates given by
\varphi
. We want to embed isometrically a piece of our manifold
M
inside a torus with perturbed metric (from its original flat one). For that, let
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a standard cutoff, with
\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\leq\eta\leq\chi_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
. Consider the
n
-dimensional flat torus
(\mathbb{T}^{n},\delta_{ij})
of side length
8
, obtained from the hypercube of side length
8
centered at
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
by identifying opposite faces, and let
\varphi^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
denote the quotient map defining
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Define a new Riemannian manifold
M^{\prime}=(\mathbb{T}^{n},g^{\prime})
, where
g^{\prime}_{ij}:=\eta g_{ij}+(1-\eta)\delta_{ij}
is just the usual flat metric except on
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathcal{B}_{2})\subset\mathbb{T}^{n}
. It is obvious then that
M^{\prime}
satisfies the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA}_{\ell}(M^{\prime},g^{\prime},1,p_{0}^{\prime}=0,\varphi^{\prime})
, since by construction
g_{ij}\equiv g^{\prime}_{ij}
in
\mathcal{B}_{1}
in the coordinates given by
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
. Thanks to this, as recorded in
[
18
, Lemma 2.17]
the heat kernels of both manifolds in
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
are actually quantitatively close, in the sense that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}(H-H^{\prime})|(x,y,t)\leq Ce^{-c/t}\,;

(156)

we reproduce here the precise statement for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma D.2

(
Localisation principle
,
[
18
]
)
.

Let
(M,g)
and
(M^{\prime},g^{\prime})
be two Riemannian
n
-manifolds. Assume that both
M
and
M^{\prime}
satisfy the flatness assumptions
{\rm FA_{\ell}}(M,g,1,p_{0},\varphi)
and
{\rm FA_{\ell}}(M^{\prime},g,1,p_{0}^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
respectively, and suppose that
g_{ij}\equiv g^{\prime}_{ij}
in
\mathcal{B}_{1}(0)
in the coordinates induced by
\varphi^{-1}
and
(\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}
.

Then, letting
H(x,y,t):=H_{M}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y),t)
and
H^{\prime}(x,y,t):=H_{M^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime}(x),\varphi^{\prime}(y),t)
, we have that the difference
(H-H^{\prime})(x,y,t)
is of class
C^{\ell}
in
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}(0)\times\mathcal{B}_{1/2}(0)\times[0,\infty)
and

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x^{\alpha}}\frac{\partial^{|\beta|}}{\partial y^{\beta}}(H-H^{\prime})(x,y,t)\bigg|\leq C\exp(-c/t)\quad\mbox{for }(x,y,t)\in\mathcal{B}_{1/2}(0)\times\mathcal{B}_{1/2}(0)\times[0,\infty),

whenever
\alpha
and
\beta
are multi-indices satisfying
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq\ell
,
with
C,c>0
depending only on
n
and
\ell
.

Moreover, defining
E_{M^{\prime}}:=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{{\rm dist}_{M^{\prime}}^{2}(p,q)}{4t}}
and
E^{\prime}(x,y,t):=E_{M^{\prime}}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y),t)
, there is
r=r(n)>0
such that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}(E-E^{\prime})|(x,y,t)=0

(157)

for
(x,y,t)\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times(0,\infty)
, simply because
E\equiv E^{\prime}
in this domain. Indeed, by a trivial comparison with the Euclidean metric, the flatness assumptions imply that there is some small
r=r(n)>0
for which if
(x,y)\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)
, then the distances
{\rm dist}_{M}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))
and
{\rm dist}_{M^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime}(x),\varphi^{\prime}(y))
are completely determined by the metrics
g_{ij}\equiv g^{\prime}_{ij}
in
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, and therefore they are actually identical. Since
E
and
E^{\prime}
depend only on
{\rm dist}_{M}
and
{\rm dist}_{M^{\prime}}
, we conclude (
157
).

Combining (
156
) and (
157
), we deduce that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}(H-E)|(x,y,t)\leq|\nabla_{y}(H^{\prime}-E^{\prime})|(x,y,t)+Ce^{-c/t}

(158)

for
(x,y,t)\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times(0,\infty)
, thus reducing our problem to showing the proximity of
H^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
.

Step 2.
Closeness between
\nabla_{y}H^{\prime}
and
\nabla_{y}E^{\prime}
.

Now, the closeness between
H_{M^{\prime}}
and
E_{M^{\prime}}
is precisely the content of the heat kernel expansion on a compact manifold. A precise statement that will serve for our purposes is the following:

Proposition D.3

(
Heat kernel expansion
)
.

Let
(N,h)
be a closed,
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold. Consider the new manifold
(N,h^{\prime})
, and assume that
\frac{1}{C}h\leq h^{\prime}\leq Ch
and
\|h^{\prime}\|_{C^{k}((N,h))}\leq C
for all
1\leq k\leq l=n+2
. The latter means that, given any pair
X,Y
of vector fields on
(N,h)
with
C^{l}
norm bounded by
1
,
h^{\prime}(X,Y)
is a function on
(N,h)
with
C^{l}
norm bounded by
C
.

Then, there is
a constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(h,C,l)
such that there exist smooth functions
u_{0},...,u_{l}
on
M\times M
with
C^{2}
norms bounded by
C^{\prime}
such that

\|H_{N,h^{\prime}}-E_{N,h^{\prime}}(u_{0}+tu_{1}+...+t^{l}u_{l})\|_{C^{2}(M\times M)}\leq C^{\prime}t^{l+1}

for all
t\in(0,1]
. Moreover,
u_{0}(p,p)\equiv 1
and
\nabla_{y}u_{0}(p,p)\equiv 0
.

Proof of Proposition
D.3
.

For
h^{\prime}=h
, i.e. without quantifying the result on the metric, this is a standard result obtained by constructing the heat kernel on a closed manifold using some parametrix approach. The fact that the coefficients in the expansion can be quantified in terms of the metric, in the sense that the result holds uniformly for
h^{\prime}
satisfying the assumptions in the present proposition, follows from a careful inspection of the version of the approach mentioned above which uses normal coordinates to construct the parametrix. This is a standard way of constructing the parametrix, which the reader can find for example in
[
48
, Chapter 3]
. In this approach, one first constructs locally a parametrix working on normal coordinates around every point of the manifold, by explicitly solving certain ODEs in polar coordinates to define an approximate heat kernel (parametrix) of the form

H^{(0)}=E_{N,h^{\prime}}(u_{0}+tu_{1}+...+t^{l}u_{l})

on a neighbourhood of the diagonal of the manifold. The
u_{i}
are explicit in the metric in normal coordinates, and since the latter (together with derivatives) is naturally uniformly controlled for all our admissible
h^{\prime}
, so are the
u_{i}
. In the precise case of
u_{0}
, we have that
u_{0}(p,q)=\mathcal{D}^{-1/2}
with

\mathcal{D}={\rm det}\big[D_{{\rm exp}_{p}^{-1}(q)}{\rm exp}_{p}\big]=1+O({\rm dist}_{N,h^{\prime}}^{2}(p,q))\,.

This shows that
u_{0}(p,p)\equiv 1
and
\nabla_{y}u_{0}(p,p)\equiv 0
.

One then proceeds by extending in space the parametrix to a function
H^{(0)}
on all of
M\times M
simply by the use of a cutoff which is identically
1
on a neighbourhood of the diagonal. Of course, our assumptions imply that any fixed choice of such a cutoff function has uniformly bounded
C^{l}
norm for all our admissible
h^{\prime}
.

Finally, one constructs the heat kernel on
M\times M
iteratively, essentially by considering the error
LH^{(k)}
of the approximate heat kernel
H^{(k)}
at a certain step
k
to vanishing under the heat operator
L:=\partial_{t}-\Delta
, convolving this error with the approximate heat kernel
H^{(k)}
itself, and adding this quantity to
H^{(k)}
to obtain a better approximation
H^{(k+1)}
; see
[
48
, page 98]
for the formal computation. This procedure converges to the true (unique) solution
H_{N,h^{\prime}}
, and the difference/error between
H^{(0)}
and
H_{N,h^{\prime}}
is then given by an explicit infinite sum made of iterated convolutions between
H^{(0)}
and
LH^{(0)}
. This error difference (together with its first
\lfloor l-n/2\rfloor\geq 2
spatial derivatives) is then shown to be uniformly bounded by
Ct^{l+1}
, see
[
48
, Lemma 3.18]
, for all
t\in(0,1]
, which gives our desired result. Here
C
is a constant which depends on
H^{(0)}
,
LH^{(0)}
and the metric tensor of
(N,h^{\prime})
, which shows that
C
is uniformly controlled for all
h^{\prime}
: since
H^{(0)}
is explicitly constructed in terms of the metric in normal coordinates (as explained at the beginning), both
H^{(0)}
and
LH^{(0)}
(and, of course, the metric tensor itself
h^{\prime}
as well) are uniformly controlled for all
h^{\prime}
satisfying our assumptions.
∎

Applying Proposition
D.3
with
N=\mathbb{T}^{n}
,
h=\delta_{ij}
and
h^{\prime}=g^{\prime}
, and noting that the conditions
\frac{1}{C}h\leq h^{\prime}\leq Ch
and
\|h^{\prime}\|_{C^{k}((N,h))}\leq C
for all
1\leq k\leq l
hold for some
C=C(n)
thanks to the flatness assumptions and the fact that
h=h^{\prime}
outside of
\varphi^{\prime}(\mathcal{B}_{2})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}(H^{\prime}-E^{\prime})|(x,y,t)

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla_{y}(H^{\prime}-u_{0}E^{\prime})|+|\nabla_{y}((u_{0}-1)E^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla_{y}(H^{\prime}-u_{0}E^{\prime})|+|u_{0}-1||\nabla_{y}E^{\prime}|+|E^{\prime}||\nabla_{y}(u_{0}-1)|

\displaystyle\leq|\nabla_{y}\big[E^{\prime}(tu_{1}+...+t^{l}u_{l})\big]|+Ct^{l+1}+|u_{0}-1||\nabla_{y}E^{\prime}|+|E^{\prime}||\nabla_{y}(u_{0}-1)|

\displaystyle\leq Ct|\nabla_{y}E^{\prime}|+CtE^{\prime}+Ct^{l+1}+|u_{0}-1||\nabla_{y}E^{\prime}|+E^{\prime}|\nabla_{y}(u_{0}-1)|\,.

We have bounded the
u_{k}
and their derivatives by uniform constants, with the exception of
(u_{0}-1)
. For this quantity, given that
u_{0}(p,p)\equiv 1
and
\nabla^{N}u_{0}(p,p)\equiv 0
, bounding the second derivatives of
u_{0}
by uniform constants we have the Taylor expansion
|u_{0}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))-1|\leq C|x-y|^{2}
, which leads to

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}(H^{\prime}-E^{\prime})|(x,y,t)

\displaystyle\leq C(t+|x-y|^{2})|\nabla_{y}E^{\prime}|+C|x-y|E^{\prime}+Ct^{l+1}\,.

(159)

From the explicit expression of
E^{\prime}
we can bound
|\nabla_{y}E^{\prime}|\leq C\frac{|x-y|}{t}E^{\prime}
. Substituting into (
159
) and combining the resulting expression with (
158
), together with the fact that
E\equiv E^{\prime}
for
(x,y,t)\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times(0,\infty)
we conclude the proof of Proposition
D.1
.
∎

We can now finally give the proof of Proposition
5.21
.

Proof of Proposition
5.21
.

Define the explicit kernel
\widetilde{K}_{s}:=\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{{\rm dist}^{n+s}(p,q)}
. Observe that, letting
E_{t}:=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{{\rm dist}(p,q)^{2}}{4t}}
, the explicit kernel corresponds exactly to
\widetilde{K}_{s}=\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int dt\,\frac{E_{t}}{t^{1+s/2}}
. Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\nabla_{q}K_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\nabla_{q}\int\frac{H_{t}}{t^{1+s/2}}dt=\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int\frac{\nabla_{q}E_{t}}{t^{1+s/2}}dt+\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int\frac{\nabla_{q}(H_{t}-E_{t})}{t^{1+s/2}}dt

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q}\widetilde{K}_{s}+\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int\frac{\nabla_{q}(H_{t}-E_{t})}{t^{1+s/2}}dt\,,

(160)

Now, we can compute

\displaystyle\nabla_{q}\widetilde{K}_{s}(p,q)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q}\frac{\alpha_{n,s}}{{\rm dist}_{M}^{n+s}(p,q)}

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{n,s}(n+s)\frac{\nabla_{q}\,{\rm dist}(p,q)}{{\rm dist}_{M}^{n+s+1}(p,q)}\,.

(161)

Observe that the gradient
\nabla_{q}\,{\rm dist}(p,q)
corresponds to the tangent vector of the geodesic from
p
to
q
. Recalling that
\varphi={\rm exp}_{p}
on its domain of definition, letting
q:=\varphi(y)
we deduce that
[D_{y}\varphi]^{-1}(\nabla_{q}\,{\rm dist}(p,q))=\frac{y}{|y|}
. On the other hand, thanks to this and the Gauss Lemma we can write, for a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{v}{\rm dist}(p,\varphi(y))

\displaystyle=[D_{q}{\rm dist}(p,\cdot)]\circ[D_{y}\varphi](v)=\langle\nabla_{q}{\rm dist}(p,q),D_{y}\varphi(v)\rangle_{g}

\displaystyle=\langle D_{y}({\rm exp}_{p})(\frac{y}{|y|}),D_{y}({\rm exp}_{p})(v)\rangle_{g}=\frac{y}{|y|}\cdot v\,,

which shows that

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}{\rm dist}(p,\varphi(y))=\frac{y}{|y|}=[D_{y}\varphi]^{-1}(\nabla_{q}\,{\rm dist}(p,q))\,.

We remark that for a function different from the Riemannian distance, the equality between the LHS and RHS would not be true in general.

In particular, from (
161
) we immediately deduce that

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}\widetilde{K}_{s}(p,\varphi(y))

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{n,s}(n+s)\frac{y}{|y|^{n+s+2}}\,,

so that thanks to (
160
) and the flatness assumptions we arrive at

\displaystyle\Big|\nabla_{y}K_{s}(p,\varphi(y))-[-\alpha_{n,s}(n+s)\frac{y}{|y|^{n+s+2}}]\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\nabla_{y}K_{s}(p,\varphi(y))-\nabla_{y}\widetilde{K}_{s}(p,\varphi(y))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\Big|\nabla_{q}K_{s}(p,q)-\nabla_{q}\widetilde{K}_{s}(p,q)\Big|_{g}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|\nabla_{q}(H_{t}-E_{t})(p,q)|_{g}}{t^{1+s/2}}dt

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{s/2}{\Gamma(1-s/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|\nabla_{y}(H_{t}-E_{t})(p,\varphi(y))|}{t^{1+s/2}}dt\,.

(162)

We will now bound (
162
) by
O\Big(\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s-1}}\Big)
and conclude.

By Proposition
D.1
, we have the estimate

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}(H-E)|(p,y,t)\leq C|y|\Big(1+\frac{|y|^{2}}{t}\Big)E(p,y,t)+Ct^{l+1}+Ce^{-c/t}

for all
(x,y,t)\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times\mathcal{B}_{r}(0)\times(0,1]
.

Since
l\geq 1
, we can easily bound

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{t^{l+1}+e^{-c/t}}{t^{1+s/2}}dt=\int_{0}^{1}t^{l-s/2}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{e^{-c/t}}{t^{1+s/2}}dt\leq C\,.

Using that

E_{t}(p,\varphi(y))=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{{\rm dist}(p,\varphi(y))^{2}}{4t}}=\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}e^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{4t}}

and performing the change of variables
r=\frac{t}{|y|^{2}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{C|y|\Big(1+\frac{|y|^{2}}{t}\Big)E(p,y,t)}{t^{1+s/2}}\,dt

\displaystyle=C|y|\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Big(1+\frac{|y|^{2}}{t}\Big)e^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{4t}}}{t^{1+(n+s)/2}}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C|y|^{-(n+s-1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1+r^{-1}}{r^{1+(n+s)/2}}e^{-1/r}\,dr

\displaystyle=C|y|^{-(n+s-1)}\,.

Finally,
\nabla H_{t}
and
\nabla E_{t}
are uniformly bounded for large times: it is clear for
E_{t}
from its explicit formula, and a constant bound for the derivatives of
H_{t}
is obtained in the proof of
[
18
, Theorem 2.13]
, by combining Lemma
D.2
with
[
18
, Proposition 2.19]
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{|\nabla_{y}(H_{t}-E_{t})(p,\varphi(y))|}{t^{1+s/2}}dt\leq C\,.

Putting everything together, we conclude the bound for (
162
) and the proof of Proposition
5.21
.
∎
